accept	accept	line		accept [WHO|NAME]\naccept an offer of friendship, or of an inventory item\nhelp groups: giving people waiting\nkeywords: yes agree receive incoming friendship request friend item gift inventory offer received
accept:intro	accept	intro		`accept` says yes to an offer of friendship, or to an item somebody\nhas handed over.  `decline` is the same question answered the other\nway, and both of them are what `offers` lists.\n\nWith nothing after it, it takes the only offer waiting.  With\nsomething after it, that names one: part of the name of whoever\noffered friendship, or part of the name of the item offered, or the\nwhole name of whoever sent the item.\n\n    accept\n    accept lantern\n\nWhat is typed is tried against the items first and only then against\nthe friendship offers, so a word that could be either takes the item.\n\nNaming settles an ambiguity, and a name that does not settle one is\nrefused rather than guessed at: a word matching two friendship offers,\nor two of the items, says how many it matched and answers neither.  The\nwhole of a name beats part of another, so an item called "a lamp" is\nstill reachable while "a lamp stand" is on offer beside it.  Two items\nwith the same name from the same person is the one ambiguity no name\nwill settle, and `waiting` is the way through -- it numbers them, and\n`answer N` takes the one meant.
accept:example:1	accept	example		accept
accept:example:2	accept	example		accept lantern
accept:section:An item lands in the folder the shell is in	accept	section	An item lands in the folder the shell is in	`cd Objects` then `accept` puts it in Objects.  Answering the same\noffer through `waiting` and `answer` instead puts it in the Objects\nfolder, since a number typed at a listing says nothing about where\nanything should go.\n\nA shell standing in a folder that has since been renamed or removed\nhas nowhere to accept into, and says so rather than letting the grid\nchoose.
accept:section:Which kind a bare command means	accept	section	Which kind a bare command means	Where one of each is waiting, the bare command takes the friendship\noffer; the item has to be named.\n\nWhere only items are waiting, the bare command means them: the only\none is taken, and two or more are refused with a count.  Naming one of\nthe two is what to do next.
accept:section:When another client got there first	accept	section	When another client got there first	slbotd, or another shell on the same avatar, may have answered the\noffer already.  slgod is asked before anything is sent, and if it\nsays so the answer is refused with who answered and how, and nothing\ngoes -- the offer has left the list by then either way.
accept:section:What the report does not say	accept	section	What the report does not say	Whether the item actually arrived.  The grid does the moving and says\nnothing about it, so the report means the answer was sent, and looking\nin the folder afterwards is what says it is there.\n\nAccepting a friendship also records it here.  The grid tells the side\nthat offered and tells the side that accepted nothing, so without that\nrecord the friend list would stay wrong until the next login.
accept:section:Examples	accept	section	Examples	The only offer waiting:\n\n    accept\n\nOne of several, by who sent it or what it is called:\n\n    accept Example\n    cd Objects\n    accept lantern\n\nSee also: `decline`, `offers`, `waiting` and `answer` for the same\noffers alongside everything else that wants an answer, and `ls` for\nlooking at what arrived.
accept:example:3	accept	example	Examples	accept Example
accept:example:4	accept	example	Examples	cd Objects
agents	agents	line		agents\nthe sessions this daemon holds, oldest first; * is the default\nhelp groups: avatars\nkeywords: avatars accounts profiles bots sessions logged in running which avatar daemon list
agents:intro	agents	intro		`agents` is the listing `login` and `logout` name something out of.\nThe first column is the profile -- the name those two take -- not the\navatar's own name beside it.\n\n    agents\n\nSessions the daemon holds come first, oldest first.  A session that\nwas stopped sits at the end of that group rather than keeping its\nplace.  Profiles the daemon knows of but is not holding follow, in\nthe order it lists them.  The order is not alphabetical.\n\nA star marks the session a command that names no avatar drives: the\nfirst one the daemon still holds.  A session this shell is attached\nto says so at the end of its line.  The two are often the same and\ndo not have to be.\n\nA shell that logged in for itself has no daemon to ask, and prints\nthe one avatar it has.
agents:example:1	agents	example		agents
agents:section:What stands where the region would	agents	section	What stands where the region would	A session that is up shows the region its avatar is in.  Anything\nelse shows its state instead, with the reason after it where there\nis one:\n\n    connecting  a login in flight, or a session being brought back\n    configured  a profile exists and nothing has asked for it\n    stopped     down and staying down; the reason says which sort\n    failed      a login was tried and refused; the detail says how\n\n`connecting` is two situations.  A login that has not finished\nreads `logging in`.  A session the daemon holds and has lost the\ncircuit to shows the error as its reason; the supervisor is already\nreconnecting, so waiting is the right thing to do about either.\n\n`stopped` is three.  `logged out` is somebody having asked here.\nAnything beginning `ended by the grid` is the avatar having been\nthrown off -- commonly because it is logged in somewhere else --\nand the grid's own words follow.  The third is an error the daemon\nwill not retry, with no phrase in front of it.\n\nConfigured, stopped and failed are what the listing is for.  Told\nonly whether a session is connected, a profile nobody has ever\nstarted and an avatar somebody logged out an hour ago look identical\n-- and the second is not a fault, it is somebody using that avatar\nin a viewer.
agents:section:The star	agents	section	The star	The default moves only when the session it is on goes away.\nBringing another avatar up does not move it, attaching does not\nmove it, and a session that drops and is reconnected keeps its\nplace.  An avatar that has been logged out gives up its place, so\nlogging it back in puts it at the end of the queue rather than at\nthe head.\n\nA session shown as connecting keeps the star -- a reconnection never\ncost it its place, and a bare command goes to it and waits.  A\nprofile the daemon holds no session for is never starred.  When\nevery session has been stopped there is no star at all.
agents:section:Examples	agents	section	Examples	    agents\n\nSee also: `login`, `logout`, `status` for how one session's circuit\nis doing, and `viewer` for handing one over to a real viewer.
answer	answer	line		answer [--file PATH] N [BUTTON|TEXT|L$FEE]\nanswer one of them: yes, a button by name or number, what a text box wants, or the fee a group asks\nhelp groups: waiting\nkeywords: respond reply yes accept dialog menu button choose text box teleport request permission invitation join group fee
answer:intro	answer	intro		`answer` says yes to one of the things `waiting` lists, by its number.\n\nWhat yes means depends on what is asking: a teleport is taken, an\noffered item is taken into Objects, a friendship is accepted, a script\nis granted the whole of what it asked for, a group invitation is\njoined.  A dialog has buttons, so the word after the number picks one,\neither by its number in the listing or by its label.\n\n    answer 1\n    answer 3 Yes\n    answer 3 2\n\nA dialog with one button needs no word after the number; that button\nis pressed.  Several buttons and nothing after the number is refused,\nwith the labels listed.\n\nA script is granted every bit it asked for, including debit, which\nspends this avatar's money.  The listing names the bits; `no` refuses\nthe whole request.\n\nAn offered item taken here lands in Objects, not in the folder the\nshell is in.  `accept` is the command that puts one in the current\nfolder.
answer:example:1	answer	example		answer 1
answer:example:2	answer	example		answer 3 Yes
answer:example:3	answer	example		answer 3 2
answer:section:A text box	answer	section	A text box	Everything after the number is the answer:\n\n    answer 4 the north gate\n\nWith nothing after the number it opens the multi-line form.  Lines are\ntyped until `^D` at the end of one ends them -- in front of anything,\n`^D` deletes forward as it does at the prompt.  ESC starts the answer\nagain and `^C` sends nothing.\n\nA text box carries 254 bytes and no more.  A longer answer is refused\noutright rather than cut short, and the refusal says how many bytes\nwere offered against the 254 there was room for.
answer:example:4	answer	example	A text box	answer 4 the north gate
answer:option:--file PATH	answer	option	--file PATH	Answer a text box from this file, newlines and all.  It is refused on\nanything that is not a text box.  Without it the answer is typed after\nthe number, or as several lines at the prompt.  A file is the way to\nsend newlines; it is not a way to send more than 254 bytes.\n\nThe flag goes in front of the number.  `answer 4 --file notes.txt`\ntakes `--file notes.txt` as the text of the answer.
answer:section:A group invitation with a fee has to name the fee	answer	section	A group invitation with a fee has to name the fee	This is the only kind that can spend money as part of saying yes.  The\nfee is in the waiting listing, and where it is not zero it has to be\ntyped back before the answer will go:\n\n    answer 3 L$50\n\n`L$50` and `50` are the same amount.  An amount that does not match\nwhat the invitation said is refused, with both figures named.  Typing\nthe fee does not cap what the group charges -- the simulator takes the\ngroup's fee whatever is sent.\n\nAn invitation that did not say what joining costs has to be answered\nwith an amount too: `answer N L$0` if the invitation is free, and the\nreply says plainly that the group will charge its fee whatever was\ntyped.  An invitation that said there is no fee is the one that takes\na bare `answer N`.\n\nNothing answers a join, so `group` is what says whether it worked.
answer:example:5	answer	example	A group invitation with a fee has to name the fee	answer 3 L$50
answer:section:Accepting a teleport waits for the avatar to arrive	answer	section	Accepting a teleport waits for the avatar to arrive	A teleport out of this region is followed rather than fired off: the\nsession moves with the avatar, and `answer` returns once it is\nstanding in the region it was invited to.  A line is printed before\nthe request goes, saying whose offer is being taken, and another\nafterwards saying where the avatar ended up.  The offer names who made\nit and whatever they typed with it; the region is not in the message\nat all.  A lure to somewhere in the same region arrives at once.\n\nWaiting stops after thirty seconds.  Running out is not usually a slow\ngrid: a teleport asked for while another is under way is answered with\nnothing whatever, so it is what accepting a second offer too soon\nlooks like.  `where` says whether the avatar moved.\n\nEverything this shell knew about the region left behind is gone on\narrival -- see `tp`, which says what goes and what survives.
answer:section:Examples	answer	section	Examples	    answer 1\n    answer 3 Yes\n    answer 3 L$50\n    answer --file notes.txt 4\n\nSee also: `waiting`, `no`, `ignore`, `accept` for friendship and\ninventory offers by name, and `tp` for going somewhere nobody offered.
answer:example:6	answer	example	Examples	answer --file notes.txt 4
auto	auto	line		auto [-n COUNT]\nthe objects benchmarks run in; -n sets up that many\nhelp groups: avatars\nkeywords: benchmark slots hud objects scripts run at once parallel set up how many
auto:intro	auto	intro		`auto` is about the worn objects that slrun and slbench run their\nscripts in.  With nothing asked it reports what this avatar has: how\nmany of the objects are worn, and therefore how much can run at once.\nWith `-n` it sets that many up, and prints which object went to which\nslot.\n\n    auto\n\nNothing else in this shell needs it.  It is here because the answer\nto "why will only eight scripts run at a time" is a number nothing\nelse prints.\n\nThe objects are worn on the HUD points, because nothing else wants\nthose and they are no part of how the avatar looks.  There are only\neight of them, so the first eight objects have one each and the rest\ndouble up.  That is what the listing after `-n` is for: past the\neighth slot the only way to see the pool is arranged as intended is\nto read which point each one landed on.  Twelve is this avatar's\nwhole share.\n\nOne object runs one script at a time, so what is worn is what can\nrun.  A benchmark's width is slbench's own and moves with its flags;\nthere is nothing here to work a count of benchmarks out from.\n\nThe pool they come out of is the daemon's rather than this avatar's.\nTwelve is what each hosted avatar contributes, and a request for\nmore is answered out of as many avatars as it takes.  What this\ncommand reports is one avatar's share, and naming an avatar confines\na run to that share.\n\nA run asks for a number of objects and gets that many or none.  One\nthat cannot be served waits for enough of them to come free rather\nthan starting narrower than it asked for.
auto:example:1	auto	example		auto
auto:option:-n COUNT	auto	option	-n COUNT	Set up this many of the objects, rather than only reporting.  Without\nit, how many are worn is printed.  The listing after it says which\nobject went to which slot.
auto:section:Setting up while something is running	auto	section	Setting up while something is running	Setting up takes this avatar's whole share before it does anything,\nand refuses if any of it is busy.  Wearing things is not something to do\nunderneath a benchmark: an attach replaces what is on the point, and\na run whose object went away reports nothing useful about why it\nfailed.\n\nGetting fewer objects than were asked for is not a failure.  Fewer\nobjects means less at once, and the report says so in those words.
auto:section:Examples	auto	section	Examples	    auto\n    auto -n 12\n\nSee also: `worn` for the same objects as attachments, `wear` and\n`detach` for moving one by hand, and `agents` for which avatar this\nis being asked of.
auto:example:2	auto	example	Examples	auto -n 12
caps	caps	line		caps [TEXT]\nthe capabilities this session was granted\nhelp groups: simulator\nkeywords: capabilities services http urls session granted grid what is allowed
caps:intro	caps	intro		`caps` lists the capabilities the simulator granted this session.  A\ncapability is an HTTP service the session may use alongside the\nmessage circuit -- fetching inventory, uploading a script, searching\nfor somebody by part of their name, asking what the region supports\n-- and what is on this list decides which of those are possible at\nall.\n\n    caps\n\nWith a word after it, only the capabilities whose names contain that\nword are printed, matched without regard to case.  `status` counts\nthe same list without naming it.
caps:example:1	caps	example		caps
caps:section:What is on the list and what is missing from it	caps	section	What is on the list and what is missing from it	The list is the answer to one question asked at login: the session\nnames the capabilities it wants, and the simulator replies with the\nones it serves.  A name that is absent is absent for one of two\nreasons that look identical here -- either nothing asked for it, or\nthe region does not offer it -- and asking for one that does not\nexist is harmless, because the reply simply omits it.\n\nThat makes this the first place to look when a command fails in a\nway that has nothing to do with permissions or with the object it\nnames.  Whole paths through the protocol are shut when their\ncapability is not there, and what the simulator says at that point\nis usually about something else.\n\nWhat is printed is the names alone.  Each one is served by a URL,\nand the URL stays with whatever holds the session; nothing about it\nwould be usable from here.
caps:section:It describes this session where it is standing now	caps	section	It describes this session where it is standing now	Capabilities are granted by the simulator, to one session, for one\nregion.  Two avatars in the same region can have different lists,\nthe same avatar logged in again may too, and a listing saved from an\nearlier session says what was true then.\n\nA teleport replaces them.  The URLs served the region the avatar\nleft, so they are dropped on arrival and the whole list is asked for\nagain, from the new region's own seed.  What is printed after a\nteleport is the new simulator's answer rather than a carried-over\none, which is a change nothing on the screen shows: the names may\nlook the same.  It is the URLs behind them that moved.
caps:section:Examples	caps	section	Examples	    caps\n    caps inventory\n\nSee also: `features` for what the simulator says it supports, `lsl`\nfor the language it implements, and `status`.
caps:example:2	caps	example	Examples	caps inventory
cat	cat	line		cat [--in OBJECT] PATH\nprint a notecard or a script; --in reads one inside a rezzed object\nhelp groups: inventory\nkeywords: read view show print display contents text source code script notecard lsl inside object download to disk file
cat:intro	cat	intro		`cat` prints a notecard or a script.\n\n    cat /Scripts/greeter > greeter.lsl\n\nThe argument is an inventory path, relative to the folder the shell\nis in, or the uuid of an item.  Redirection is the other half of it:\nthat line is how a script gets onto the disk.  `save` writes the\ntext back.\n\nWith `--in` it reads one from inside a rezzed object instead:\n\n    cat --in lantern greeter\n\nThe object is named by the word the region calls it or by its uuid, and\nthe item by its name, exactly and in the case it has, or by the id\n`ls -l --in` prints for it.  The name is one argument, so one with a\nspace in it is quoted.  What is read is the object's own copy, which\nneed not be what is in inventory under the same name: a script edited\nin the object, or a notecard saved there, has parted company with the\nitem it was dropped in from, and the copy is the one the object runs\nor reads.  The region decides whether it may be read, and a script\nthis avatar may not modify is refused there.\n\nA notecard that may not be copied cannot be read at all, in inventory\nor in an object: the region refuses it, and a viewer does not even\nask, saying "You do not have permission to view this notecard".\n`cat` says which permission it is.\n\nA notecard and a script, and nothing else.  A texture, a sound, an\nanimation and the rest are not text; `get` is the command for a\ntexture.\n\nWhat is printed is the text.  A notecard's wrapper is not shown.
cat:example:1	cat	example		cat /Scripts/greeter > greeter.lsl
cat:example:2	cat	example		cat --in lantern greeter
cat:option:--in OBJECT	cat	option	--in OBJECT	Read it from inside a rezzed object, not from inventory.
cat:section:Examples	cat	section	Examples	    cat Notecards/README\n    cat /Scripts/greeter > greeter.lsl\n    cat --in lantern greeter\n    cat --in 88fa7e57-... "read me"
cat:example:3	cat	example	Examples	cat Notecards/README
cat:example:4	cat	example	Examples	cat --in 88fa7e57-... "read me"
cat:section:A link is followed	cat	section	A link is followed	An outfit folder holds links rather than items.  A link carries the\nsame name as the thing it points at, and a listing tells the two apart\nonly by the word `link` in the type column of `ls -l`, so a path taken\nfrom `My Outfits` names a link almost every time.\n\n`cat` follows one to the item at the other end, which is what the id\nit sends has to be: the grid has no object for a link's id and answers\nan id it does not recognise with silence rather than with a refusal.\n\nA link this inventory cannot follow is refused, naming the id it\nlooked for.  A link outlives what it pointed at, so an outfit put\ntogether years ago may name things that have since been deleted.\n\nSee also: `save` for writing one back, `new` for making one that\ndoes not exist yet, `get` for a texture, `ls` for what is there\nto read, and `fetch` for copying one out of an object.
cd	cd	line		cd [PATH]\nchange folder; no path goes to the root, .. goes up\nhelp groups: inventory\nkeywords: change folder directory enter go into inventory navigate browse up
cd:intro	cd	intro		`cd` changes which inventory folder the shell is in.  Every command\nthat takes a path takes it relative to that folder unless the path\nbegins with a slash, and `pwd` says which folder it is.\n\n    cd Objects/lanterns\n\nThe argument is a folder path: a leading slash starts from the root,\n`..` goes up a level, `.` stays, and the names between the slashes\nare walked one at a time.  With no argument it goes to the root.\nThe empty path would otherwise mean wherever the shell already is,\nso a bare `cd` would do nothing.
cd:example:1	cd	example		cd Objects/lanterns
cd:section:Only a folder, and only by name	cd	section	Only a folder, and only by name	An item is not somewhere to be.  A path naming one is refused as "no\nfolder", word for word what a name that is nothing at all gets: `cd`\nasks for a folder of that name and there is not one either way.\n`cat` and `ls` say "nothing called that here" instead, so which\nsentence appears says which command was typed rather than which kind\nof thing the name turned out to be.\n\nA uuid does not work here.  `cd` walks names; a uuid in a path is\nread as a folder called that, which nothing is.  `ls -l` prints the\nid beside the path for the commands that do take one.
cd:section:Case, and names that are not unique	cd	section	Case, and names that are not unique	The comparison ignores case, as every path through inventory does.\nThe grid keeps whatever case a name was given, but two names that\ndiffer only in case are the same name at this prompt.  Inside an\nobject it is the other way about: `rm --in` and `mv --in` want the\nname exactly as it is spelled there.\n\nWhere two folders in one place share a name, a path means the first\nof them and there is no way to say the other.  Moving or renaming\none is the way out.\n\nA name may contain a slash.  A backslash in front of it says so:\n`cd Notecards/2026\\/07` enters a folder called `2026/07`.
cd:section:Examples	cd	section	Examples	    cd Objects/lanterns\n    cd ..\n    cd\n\nSee also: `pwd`, `ls`, `mkdir`, and `find` for looking without going.
cd:example:2	cd	example	Examples	cd ..
cd:example:3	cd	example	Examples	cd
chat	chat	line		chat [WHO]\nenter chat mode; with WHO, in an instant message session\nhelp groups: people\nkeywords: talk conversation chat mode typing messages local nearby private switch keyboard
chat:intro	chat	intro		chat turns the keyboard over to talking.  Command mode is the outer\none, because a shell is wanted for listing and moving things far more\noften than for conversation, and this is the way in; the escape key is\nthe way back out.\n\n    chat\n\nWith nobody named it enters chat mode on whichever conversation is\ncurrent.  With somebody named -- a name, a key, or a number from the\nlast listing -- it opens the conversation with that person first and\nswitches to it, which is the same thing `im` does when there is nothing\nto say.  Everything after `chat` is the name, not a message.  Naming\nsomebody opens a conversation; `im` with words after the name sends\none.\n\nThe prompt is where the next line will go: `Local` for the region's\nopen chat, or somebody's name for an instant message to them.  A count\nin brackets may stand in front of it, as in `(2) Local>`, and that half\nbelongs to `waiting` rather than to the conversation: it is how many\nthings are waiting for an answer, and it stands in front of the command\nprompt in the same way.
chat:example:1	chat	example		chat
chat:section:A half-typed line is put aside, not lost	chat	section	A half-typed line is put aside, not lost	Leaving a mode puts the line being typed aside and brings back the line\nthat was put aside on the way in, so an interrupted sentence is still\nthere afterwards.  Nothing is sent or run by the switch.\n\nThere is one place to put a line rather than one for each mode, and the\nmulti-line answer a text box takes uses it too, so a sentence left half\ntyped in chat can come back at the `1 text>` prompt.  It comes back on\nthe line to be edited, and nothing is sent until it is entered.
chat:section:What the keys do here	chat	section	What the keys do here	Tab moves to the next conversation, rather than completing a word as it\ndoes at a command prompt, and it does nothing at all while something is\nhalf typed -- moving then would send the line somewhere nobody meant.\nCtrl-C on an empty line returns to command mode rather than leaving the\nshell.\n\nUp and down walk what has been said, which is a history of its own: a\ncommand is never offered at a chat prompt, where it would be said out\nloud rather than run, and a remark is never offered at a command one.\nA line of nothing, a repeat of the line before it, and a line abandoned\nrather than entered are left out.  A line the grid refused is kept,\nsince that is the one most wanted back.\n\nThe history belongs to the shell rather than to the conversation, and\nthat is what makes a line said in the wrong place recoverable: tab to\nthe conversation it was meant for, press up until it comes back, and\npress return to send it there.  What comes back lands on the line to be\nedited, and nothing is sent until it is entered.\n\nThe escape key is what leaves, and slsh takes a key of its own if\nescape is awkward on a particular terminal: `slsh --escape=^G` for one\nrun, or `set escape ^G` to have it remembered.  Escape is read when the\nshell starts, so a setting changed at the prompt is the next shell's\nescape key and not this one's -- `set escape` says so, and says which\nkey is in force.
chat:section:Everything heard is printed in either mode	chat	section	Everything heard is printed in either mode	Arriving chat has nothing to do with what the keyboard is for, so\nremarks, instant messages and notices are printed above whatever is\nbeing typed whichever mode is in force.  Time-stamped, and then one\ncharacter that says which kind of line it is: `<` for heard, `>` for\nsaid, and `*` for a notice.  The first two are the pair that has to be\ntold apart, since a remark and a reply read the same in a column; the\nthird is the shell speaking rather than anybody in the world.
chat:section:Control characters are shown, not obeyed	chat	section	Control characters are shown, not obeyed	What is heard is somebody else's text, and a terminal acts on the\nescape sequences in whatever it is sent: they can clear the screen,\nwrite over the line that says who spoke, set the window title, or put\nsomething on the clipboard.  So slsh shows every control character in\ncaret notation instead of sending it -- ESC is `^[`, BEL `^G`, DEL\n`^?` -- with `M-` in front for the eight-bit ones, `M-^[` for U+009B.\nThe same goes for everything else the grid names and slsh prints:\navatars, objects, parcels, groups, inventory, the prompt when it names\nsomebody, and the transcript.\n\nA newline is still a line break and a tab still a tab.  A carriage\nreturn on its own would put the cursor back over the start of the\nline, so it is shown as `^M`; one immediately before a newline is\ntaken with it as the one line break it was meant as.\n\nOutput redirected to a file is the one thing left as it arrived, since\na file is what a name is copied out of to be typed back in, and `^[`\ntyped back is not the name.  Such a file is data rather than a screen:\nread it with `less`, which shows control characters rather than\nsending them, and not with `cat`, which sends them.
chat:section:Examples	chat	section	Examples	Enter chat mode and talk to the room:\n\n    chat\n\nEnter it already talking to somebody:\n\n    chat Example Resident\n\nSee also: `talk` for the conversations tab moves between, `im` for one\nmessage without leaving command mode, and `say` for one remark out\nloud.
chat:example:2	chat	example	Examples	chat Example Resident
cp	cp	line		cp PATH NAME\ncopy an inventory item under a new name\nhelp groups: giving inventory\nkeywords: copy duplicate item clone inventory second copy
cp:intro	cp	intro		`cp` duplicates one inventory item under a new name.  `mv` is the\nother half of the pair: `mv` leaves one item where `cp` leaves two.\n\n    cp probe probe, the second\n\nThe first argument is what to copy -- a path relative to the folder\nthe shell is in, or a uuid -- and the rest of the line is the new\nname, so a name with spaces in it needs no quoting.  A folder is\nrefused; only items are copied.\n\nThe copy lands here, in the folder the shell is in.  There is no\ndestination folder to give: copying something into another folder\nis `cp` and then `mv`.\n\nCopying asks the land nothing.  An avatar that may not rez on this\nparcel cannot make an object in the world, and can still copy one\nit already owns.
cp:example:1	cp	example		cp probe probe, the second
cp:section:A no-copy item never appears	cp	section	A no-copy item never appears	The grid makes the copy when it gets round to it, so this waits for\nit to show up in the folder and prints its id when it does.  An\nitem whose permissions forbid copying produces nothing at all\nrather than a refusal, and the wait ends after a minute saying so.\nThat is the usual reason a `cp` seems to hang: it is not the shell\nbeing slow, it is an item that was never going to be duplicated.\n\nNothing here says what an item's permissions are beforehand: `ls -l`\ngives the kind, the date, the id and the path, and `perms` works on an\nobject standing in the region rather than on an item in inventory.  The\ncopy not arriving is how a no-copy item is found out.
cp:section:Examples	cp	section	Examples	    cp probe probe, the second\n\nInto another folder, which is two steps:\n\n    cp probe spare\n    mv spare Objects/lanterns
cp:example:2	cp	example	Examples	cp probe spare
cp:example:3	cp	example	Examples	mv spare Objects/lanterns
cp:section:A link is not followed	cp	section	A link is not followed	A link is an inventory entry in its own right, and `cp` acts on the\nentry.  Copying a link makes another link.  An item may have links to\nit from several outfits, and following one here would reach past all\nof them to the item they share.\n\nSee also: `mv`, `mkdir`, `rm`, `place` for putting a copy out into\nthe world, and `give` for handing one to somebody else.
decline	decline	line		decline [WHO|NAME]\nrefuse one\nhelp groups: giving people waiting\nkeywords: refuse reject deny no friendship request item offer gift
decline:intro	decline	intro		`decline` refuses an offer of friendship, or an item somebody has\nhanded over, and tells them so.  It is `accept` answered the other\nway, and it names what it means in exactly the same fashion.\n\nWith nothing after it, it refuses the only offer waiting.  With\nsomething after it, that names one: part of the name of whoever\noffered friendship, or part of the name of the item offered, or the\nwhole name of whoever sent it.  Where one of each is waiting the bare\ncommand means the friendship offer, and the item has to be named.\n\n    decline\n    decline lantern\n\nThe traps are `accept`'s traps, since it is the same picking: what is\ntyped is tried against the items first, so a word that could be either\ntakes the item; and a word that matches two of anything is refused\nwith a count, so nothing is answered by accident.
decline:example:1	decline	example		decline
decline:example:2	decline	example		decline lantern
decline:section:Saying no is not the same as saying nothing	decline	section	Saying no is not the same as saying nothing	The refusal is a message, and it has to be sent or the offer stays\npending on the other side with nobody told anything.  That is the\ndifference between this and simply leaving an offer alone.\n\nIf another client on the same avatar -- slbotd, another shell -- has\nalready answered the offer, nothing is sent: slgod says who answered\nand how, and that is what is reported.\n\nThe person is told which way it went.  An item declined does not\narrive, and a friendship declined is not formed; neither can be undone\nfrom here, and the other side is free to offer again.
decline:section:Examples	decline	section	Examples	The only offer waiting:\n\n    decline\n\nOne of several, by who sent it or what it is called:\n\n    decline Another\n    decline lantern\n\nSee also: `accept`, `offers`, and the pair of `no` and `ignore` --\n`no` is this same refusal reached by the number `waiting` prints, and\nit answers these two kinds along with teleports, permissions and group\ninvitations, while `ignore` leaves the offer waiting and says nothing\nto anybody.
decline:example:3	decline	example	Examples	decline Another
detach	detach	line		detach [-w SECONDS] NAME|PATH|UUID\ntake a worn object off, by the name of the item it was worn from\nhelp groups: region\nkeywords: remove take off unwear attachment clothing hat hair worn object
detach:intro	detach	intro		`detach` takes a worn object off.\n\n    detach hat\n\nWhat it names is different from what `wear` names.  `wear` names\nsomething in inventory, because that is where the thing to be put on\nis.  `detach` names something worn, and matches the word against what\nis actually on the avatar -- so a name that is in inventory and not on\nthe avatar is a sentence saying so rather than a detach that reports\nsuccess and does nothing.\n\nA path is allowed and only its last name is used: nothing about a worn\nobject says which folder its item came from.  A uuid is looked for as\neither the inventory item or the worn object, since the region's answer\nholds both.
detach:example:1	detach	example		detach hat
detach:option:-w, --wait SECONDS	detach	option	-w, --wait SECONDS	How long to give the region to agree the thing is off.  Without it,\nfifteen.
detach:section:What `worn` can and cannot see	detach	section	What `worn` can and cannot see	The region describes an attachment when it goes on, and again at login,\nand never otherwise.  An avatar dressed before this session began can\ntherefore be wearing something nothing here has ever heard of, and\n`detach` will say it is not worn.  It is not a denial that the thing is\non; it is this session not having been told.  `worn` lists what can be\nseen.\n\nA name that is worn twice is refused with the points\nprinted.  `worn -l` gives the item ids, which are what tells the two\napart.
detach:section:It waits	detach	section	It waits	Nothing replies to a detach.  The request goes out, and what says it\nworked is the object no longer being among what is worn -- which\narrives some time later.  `detach` polls until the region agrees before\nit says the thing is off, and a wait that runs out is reported as not\nknowing rather than as failing.
detach:section:Clothing comes off; a body part does not	detach	section	Clothing comes off; a body part does not	`detach` takes off a system wearable too, and works out which it is\nfrom the name: a shirt, an alpha, a tattoo layer are not worn on a\npoint, so taking one off is dropping its link out of the Current\nOutfit folder and asking for a rebake.  The report says the slot.\n\n    detach a blue shirt\n    a blue shirt is no longer worn as shirt\n\nAttachments are looked for first, since that is the common case and\nthe wearables are one more read.  A name that is neither is the\nordinary refusal.\n\nA body part is refused.  An avatar is never without a shape, a skin,\nhair or eyes -- there is nothing to fall back to -- so the way out of\none is `wear` on another, which replaces it.  A viewer draws the same\nline without saying so: its menu offers Take Off for clothing and\nleaves it out for a body part.
detach:example:2	detach	example	Clothing comes off; a body part does not	detach a blue shirt
detach:section:Examples	detach	section	Examples	    detach hat\n    detach badge\n    detach --wait 30 Objects/hat\n\nSee also: `wear`, `worn`.
detach:example:3	detach	example	Examples	detach badge
detach:example:4	detach	example	Examples	detach --wait 30 Objects/hat
dress	dress	line		dress [-w SECONDS]\nput on everything the Current Outfit folder names that is not on\nhelp groups: region\nkeywords: outfit current outfit wear everything put on clothes restore appearance
dress:intro	dress	intro		`dress` puts on everything the Current Outfit folder names that the\navatar is not wearing.\n\n    dress
dress:example:1	dress	example		dress
dress:section:What it is for	dress	section	What it is for	The simulator puts most of an avatar's attachments back by itself when\nit logs in, but not all of them.  Measured on Agni over eight logins of\ntwo avatars: wherever a point held several attachments, exactly one of\nthem came back -- one of three on a chest, which one varying from login\nto login, and one HUD on each of eight points where twelve were worn.\nEvery point with a single attachment was restored every time.  It looks\nas though the simulator restores one attachment per point, as it did\nwhen a point could hold only one; that is inferred from the pattern.\n\nA viewer covers the gap.  Once the Current Outfit folder has loaded,\nit puts on whatever the folder names and is not on, and nobody sees it\nhappen.  Nothing here did, so an avatar could come back from a login\nmissing a head of hair or a dress and stay that way.  `worn` shows the\ndifference, and this is what closes it.\n\nOnly attachments.  Clothing and body parts are not attached and do not\ngo missing at a login: the folder is what the baking service reads, and\nit has been read by the time the avatar is standing up.
dress:section:It adds rather than replaces	dress	section	It adds rather than replaces	A point holds more than one attachment and an ordinary outfit uses\nthat: a mesh body, a dress and a pair of arms all sit on the chest.\nSo this adds, which is what a viewer does down the same path.\n\nIt was written the other way round first, because replacing is\nself-limiting -- this cannot be sure what is on already, since what\nsays an attachment is worn is the region's description of it and that\ndescription can be missing.  That is right about the risk and wrong\nabout the cost: restoring with replace puts the chest items on one\nafter another and each knocks the last one off.  Seen, on an avatar\nrestored that way: she came back in her boots and her hair and nothing\nelse.\n\nA duplicate is visible and costs a `detach`.  A garment silently lost\nis neither.\n\nSo the duplicate is reported rather than prevented.  Anything the\nregion describes twice is named at the end:\n\n    worn more than once, which detach undoes: a hat\n\nTwo attachments from one item agree in every field a person could name\none by, so nothing else is going to mention it.
dress:example:2	dress	example	It adds rather than replaces	worn more than once, which detach undoes: a hat
dress:section:It waits for what the simulator says is on	dress	section	It waits for what the simulator says is on	After a login the simulator is putting the outfit back itself, and the\nregion describes each piece as it arrives.  For a while, then,\nsomething can be on and not yet described -- and asking for it again\nwould be asking for a thing that is already there.\n\nThe simulator's own list of what is attached settles it.  It names\nevery attachment on the body by its object, and a viewer draws an\navatar as a cloud until what it has been shown matches that list.\nWhile the list names more than the region has described, this waits,\nfor up to `--wait` seconds, before deciding anything is missing.\n\nThe list has two limits.  It never includes HUDs, not even in the copy\nan avatar is sent about itself.  And an avatar is sent its own list\nwhen it is baked, not when it logs in -- measured, none arrived in the\ntwenty seconds after a login until a bake was asked for.  So when there\nis no list baked from the outfit as it is now, this asks for a bake\nfirst, as a viewer does once the outfit folder has loaded, and gives\nthe answer a few seconds to arrive.  `wear` and `detach` ask for a bake\nafterwards too, which is what keeps it current.
dress:section:The report has three parts	dress	section	The report has three parts	    dress\n    put on a mesh body, a dress, a hat\n    asked for and not described: a HUD\n\nWhat was **already on** is counted rather than named, so that running\nthis twice does not read as having done nothing.\n\nWhat **went on** is the answer.\n\nWhat was **asked for and not described** is neither a success nor a\nfailure.  The confirmation is the region describing the new object, and\nthat description is the thing most likely to be lost, so a name here\nmeans "asked for, and nothing came back about it" -- which is weaker\nthan "not worn".  `worn` a minute later is the way to settle it.\n\nWhen the simulator lists attachments that the region has still not\ndescribed once the waiting is over, that is said too:\n\n    the simulator lists 1 attachment that nothing here has described\n\nThat is the case in which "asked for and not described" may well be\non.
dress:example:3	dress	example	The report has three parts	put on a mesh body, a dress, a hat
dress:option:--wait SECONDS	dress	option	--wait SECONDS	How long to give the region to describe what it was asked to rez,\ntwenty seconds by default.  The wait is one wait for the whole outfit\nrather than one per item: the requests go out together and the\nconfirmations arrive together, which is what a viewer does too.\n\nThe same limit bounds the waiting beforehand, for what the simulator\nsays is on already, so a run can take up to twice this.
dress:section:Examples	dress	section	Examples	    dress\n    dress --wait 40\n\nSee also: `worn` for what is on and what the outfit says should be,\n`wear` and `detach` for one thing at a time, and `ls -L "/Current\nOutfit"` to read the folder itself.
dress:example:4	dress	example	Examples	dress --wait 40
drop	drop	line		drop OBJECT PATH\nput an inventory item inside a rezzed object\nhelp groups: region\nkeywords: put add insert copy item script notecard into inside object prim contents load
drop:intro	drop	intro		`drop` puts an inventory item inside a rezzed object.  It is not\nputting something down in the world -- that is `place` -- and it is\nnot picking something up -- that is `take`.  `fetch` is what brings\none back out into inventory, `rm --in` deletes one where it is, and\n`ls --in` is what says which are in there.  Nothing\nis offered and nobody is asked: this is the avatar's own object, so it\nhappens at once, where `give` offers an item to a person and waits for\nthem to accept it.\n\n    drop lantern Scripts/greeter\n\nThe object comes first and the item takes the rest of the line, so an\nobject whose name has a space in it has to be quoted and an item whose\nname has one need not be.  The object is named by the word the region\ncalls it or by its uuid; where several objects answer to one word it is\nrefused, with their uuids printed, since editing the wrong one is not\nsomething that can be taken back.  The item is a path relative to the\nfolder the shell is in, or a uuid, and a folder is refused -- `drop`\ntakes one item.
drop:example:1	drop	example		drop lantern Scripts/greeter
drop:section:It is a copy, and the object keeps every copy	drop	section	It is a copy, and the object keeps every copy	The item stays in inventory and a copy of it goes in.  An object\nrenames a newcomer whose name it already has rather than replacing it,\nso dropping the same item in twice leaves two entries of nearly one\nname, and it is worth listing the object rather than assuming the\nsecond drop overwrote the first.\n\nAsk twice.  A listing run straight after a drop is often the listing\nfrom before it.  `ls --in` forgets what it was told last and asks the\nobject again every time, so the answer without the item in it is the\nregion's own answer, freshly given.  Nothing has gone wrong when it\ndoes: the object has the item, and what is being said about it has not\ncaught up.\n\nWhat goes in is the whole item, permissions and all, so something whose\npermissions were set carefully is inside the object set that way.
drop:section:A script dropped in does not run	drop	section	A script dropped in does not run	Copying a script into an object leaves it there uncompiled, and\nnothing about it will change that: `start` has nothing to start,\nbecause there is nothing compiled to start.  Putting a script into an\nobject by hand looks like one action and is really two -- the copy, and\nthe save that compiles it inside the object.  `new --in OBJECT` does\nboth, and is the way in for anything that has to run.  Notecards,\ntextures and everything else that only has to be there are what this\ncommand is for.
drop:section:Examples	drop	section	Examples	    drop lantern Scripts/greeter\n    drop "brass lantern" Notecards/README
drop:example:2	drop	example	Examples	drop "brass lantern" Notecards/README
drop:section:A link is followed	drop	section	A link is followed	An outfit folder holds links rather than items.  A link carries the\nsame name as the thing it points at, and a listing tells the two apart\nonly by the word `link` in the type column of `ls -l`, so a path taken\nfrom `My Outfits` names a link almost every time.\n\n`drop` follows one to the item at the other end, which is what the id\nit sends has to be: the grid has no object for a link's id and answers\nan id it does not recognise with silence rather than with a refusal.\n\nA link this inventory cannot follow is refused, naming the id it\nlooked for.  A link outlives what it pointed at, so an outfit put\ntogether years ago may name things that have since been deleted.\n\nSee also: `fetch` for the other direction, `new`, `rm`, `ls`, `start`,\n`stop`, `place` for putting an\nobject into the world, `take` for bringing one in, and `give` for\nhanding an item to somebody else instead.
dump	dump	line		dump [-o FILE] [-w SECONDS] NAME|UUID\ndescribe an object as the simulator's JSON\nhelp groups: region\nkeywords: export save describe object json file backup prims linkset
dump:intro	dump	intro		`dump` writes an object out as JSON.  It is the reading half of three\nverbs that share one file format: this says what is there, `rez` builds\nwhat a file describes, and `reform` makes what is there match a file.\n\n    dump lantern\n\nThe object is named by the word the region calls it or by its key, and\nthe whole linkset is described, root first -- so naming any prim of an\nobject describes all of it.
dump:example:1	dump	example		dump lantern
dump:option:-o, --out FILE	dump	option	-o, --out FILE	Write here, rather than to the terminal.  Without it the JSON goes to\nthe terminal, which is what makes this usable in a pipe; with it, the\nfile is written and one line says how many prims went into it.
dump:option:-w, --wait SECONDS	dump	option	-w, --wait SECONDS	How long to let the region describe itself.  Without it, thirty.
dump:section:What is in the file, and what is not	dump	section	What is in the file, and what is not	Positions are region coordinates.  A child prim's update describes it\nas an offset from its root; the file writes both the root and the\nchildren in the region, because that is what somebody reading the file\nexpects and what building from it takes.\n\nA prim's description, its creator and its permission masks are asked\nfor one prim at a time, which is most of what the waiting is for.  A\nprim that will not answer is still described, with whatever arrived on\nits update.\n\nTextures are left out.  `texture` with no flags reads them instead.\n\nEvery prim is written with a type, and where the form is not known the\nword written is `box`: a prim nothing has described comes out as a\nbox, and so do meshes and sculpts, whose form this shell cannot name\nat all.  Everything else about such a prim is recorded truthfully, but\n`rez` and `reform` build the type they are given, so a file holding a\nmesh rebuilds it as a box.
dump:section:Examples	dump	section	Examples	Look at a thing:\n\n    dump lantern\n\nWrite it down, edit the file, and put the edit back:\n\n    dump --out lantern.json lantern\n    reform lantern lantern.json\n\nSee also: `rez`, `reform`, `texture`, and `objects` for finding the\nname to give it.
dump:example:2	dump	example	Examples	dump --out lantern.json lantern
dump:example:3	dump	example	Examples	reform lantern lantern.json
echo	echo	line		echo [text ...]\nprint the arguments, which is how to write a note into a file\nhelp groups: shell\nkeywords: print write text line note into file comment
echo:intro	echo	intro		`echo` prints its arguments.  In a shell with no scripting in it\nthat sounds like nothing, and it is there for one thing: with a\nredirection after it, it is how a line gets into a file.  A listing\nwritten out with `>` is the raw material of a file of commands, and\n`echo` is what adds to it the lines no listing produced -- a note at\nthe top, a command the listing could not have known about, a blank\nline between two batches.\n\n    echo cd Objects >> tidy\n\nThe arguments are joined with a single space, so a run of spaces\nbetween two words closes up unless the words are quoted together.\nQuoting and backslashes follow the same rules as anywhere else at\nthis prompt: quotes group, and a backslash is left alone, because an\ninventory path uses it to escape a separator and a shell that ate it\nwould make those paths untypeable.
echo:example:1	echo	example		echo cd Objects >> tidy
echo:section:It has no flags at all, on purpose	echo	section	It has no flags at all, on purpose	Every other command here answers `--help` with a usage message.\nThis one prints the words, because the whole point of it is to put a\nline into a file and a line may perfectly well be about a flag.  A\ncommand that refused to print the one thing somebody asked for would\nbe a command refusing to do its only job.\n\nIt follows that there is nothing to ask it for: whatever comes after\nit is text.
echo:section:Examples	echo	section	Examples	Build a file of commands and run it:\n\n    echo "# clear out the probes left over from yesterday" > tidy\n    echo cd Objects >> tidy\n    echo rm probe-1 >> tidy\n    echo rm probe-2 >> tidy\n    . tidy\n\nNote that `>` replaces a file and `>>` adds to it, which is what\nmakes the first line above start afresh and the rest accumulate.\n\nSee also: `.`, which runs the file this builds, and `help all`,\nwhere the redirection itself is written down.
echo:example:2	echo	example	Examples	echo "# clear out the probes left over from yesterday" > tidy
echo:example:3	echo	example	Examples	echo rm probe-1 >> tidy
echo:example:4	echo	example	Examples	echo rm probe-2 >> tidy
echo:example:5	echo	example	Examples	. tidy
emptytrash	emptytrash	line		emptytrash\nthrow away everything in the trash, permanently\nhelp groups: inventory\nkeywords: empty trash delete purge permanently clear deleted items
emptytrash:intro	emptytrash	intro		`emptytrash` throws away everything in the trash, permanently, and\nleaves the folder itself standing.  `rm` is the other way of getting\nrid of something and does not go through the trash at all: what is\nin there was put there by a viewer, and `rm` deletes outright.\n\n    emptytrash\n\nIt takes no argument.  There is one trash and nothing to say about\nit.
emptytrash:example:1	emptytrash	example		emptytrash
emptytrash:section:Found by what it is, not by what it is called	emptytrash	section	Found by what it is, not by what it is called	The grid marks a folder with the kind of thing it prefers to hold, and\nthe trash is the folder carrying the mark for it.  Its name is not the\ntest, and cannot be: the folder can be renamed, and an inventory\nmade through a viewer in another language never called it `Trash` in\nthe first place.  An inventory with no such folder is an error\nrather than a silent success.
emptytrash:section:The count is one level	emptytrash	section	The count is one level	What it prints is how many entries were directly in the trash,\ncounted before the purge.  A folder in there counts as one entry and\ngoes with everything inside it, so a trash of a few folders can\nreport a small number for a great many things.  An empty trash says\nso and does nothing.\n\nNothing is examined on the way out.  There is no undo, no list of\nwhat went, and no check that the trash held nothing wanted.  `ls` on\nthe trash before running it is the whole of the safety there is.
emptytrash:section:Examples	emptytrash	section	Examples	    ls /Trash\n    emptytrash\n\nSee also: `rm`, `mv` for taking something out of the trash rather\nthan losing it, and `ls`.
emptytrash:example:2	emptytrash	example	Examples	ls /Trash
features	features	line		features [TEXT]\nwhat the simulator says it supports\nhelp groups: simulator\nkeywords: simulator region supports mesh upload attachment limit group limit voice server
features:intro	features	intro		`features` prints what the simulator says about itself, in one\nrequest: whether mesh may be rezzed and uploaded here, how many\nattachments an avatar may wear, how many groups it may join, which\nvoice server the region runs, and the id of the LSL syntax it\nimplements.  Where `caps` says which services this session may use,\nthis says what the region on the other end of them will do.\n\n    features\n\nWith a word after it, only the features whose names contain that\nword are printed, matched without regard to case.\n\nEvery one of these is otherwise discovered by doing something and\nbeing refused, and a refusal names what failed rather than why the\nregion was never going to allow it.  The answer is also per region\nand regions differ -- a test grid answers differently from a main\ngrid one.\n\nThe id of the LSL syntax is the one to know about: it changes when\nthe language changes, which is how a cached copy of the language can\nbe told to be stale without fetching half a megabyte to find out.\n`lsl` uses it for exactly that.
features:example:1	features	example		features
features:section:What is printed, and what a list in the value means	features	section	What is printed, and what a list in the value means	The names are the simulator's and so are the values, printed as they\narrived.  Where a value is itself a set of settings rather than a\nnumber or a flag, its keys are printed instead of its contents,\nwhich keeps a line a line; that a name has keys under it is the\nuseful half, and what is under them is a question for whatever needs\nit.\n\nThe set of names is not ours and has grown several times, so a name\nnothing here has ever heard of is still printed rather than dropped.
features:section:It describes the region, not the parcel	features	section	It describes the region, not the parcel	A feature the region allows can still be refused by the land under\nthe avatar, which grants building and object entry parcel by parcel.\nA region that says it will rez mesh is saying nothing about whether\nthis particular parcel will.
features:section:Examples	features	section	Examples	    features\n    features mesh\n\nSee also: `caps`, `lsl`, and `look` for the rest of what the region\nsaid.
features:example:2	features	example	Examples	features mesh
fetch	fetch	line		fetch [--into FOLDER] [--move] [-w SECONDS] OBJECT NAME\ncopy an item out of a rezzed object into inventory, which is what drop undoes\nhelp groups: region\nkeywords: copy get retrieve item out of object contents inventory extract script notecard
fetch:intro	fetch	intro		`fetch` brings an item out of a rezzed object and into inventory.  It\nis `drop` the other way round, and what a viewer does when something\nis dragged out of an object's Contents tab or its Copy to Inventory\nbutton is pressed.  It is not picking the object itself up -- that is\n`take`.\n\n    fetch lantern greeter\n\nThe object comes first and the item takes the rest of the line, as in\n`drop`, so an object whose name has a space in it has to be quoted and\nan item whose name has one need not be.  The object is named by the\nword the region calls it or by its uuid.  The item is named exactly,\nin the case it has, or by the id `ls -l --in` prints for it, which is\nhow one of two items of the same name is chosen.\n\nIt lands in the folder the shell is in, where `new` and `mkdir` would\nmake something.  A viewer would put it in the folder for its kind\ninstead -- a notecard in Notecards -- and `--into` says so when that is\nwhat is wanted.
fetch:example:1	fetch	example		fetch lantern greeter
fetch:section:Copied, or moved out	fetch	section	Copied, or moved out	The simulator decides which, by the item's permissions, and nothing in\nthe request can overrule it.  An item this avatar may copy is copied\nand the object keeps it.  One it may not copy is moved: it comes into\ninventory and the object does not have it any more.\n\nSo an item that would be moved is refused unless `--move` says that is\nmeant, and the refusal says why.  An item that is somebody else's and\nmay not be copied is refused whatever is said, since only the object's\nowner can take it out.  One that everyone may copy -- the contents of a\nbox of freebies -- is copied from anybody's object.
fetch:option:--into FOLDER	fetch	option	--into FOLDER	The folder it lands in, as a path or as the folder's uuid.  Without\nit, the folder the shell is in.
fetch:option:--move	fetch	option	--move	Bring out an item that may not be copied, which leaves the object\nwithout it.
fetch:option:-w, --wait SECONDS	fetch	option	-w, --wait SECONDS	How long to let the region describe itself before giving up on finding\nthe object.  Without it, thirty.
fetch:section:Nothing answers	fetch	section	Nothing answers	The region does not reply to the request, so the new item is looked for\nin the folder it was sent to, and after forty seconds a fetch that\nnever arrived says so.  A refusal the region makes looks the same as\none that is slow: the item never appears.
fetch:section:Examples	fetch	section	Examples	    fetch lantern greeter\n    fetch --into /Notecards "brass lantern" README\n    fetch --move lantern only copy\n\nSee also: `drop` for putting an item in, `ls --in` for what an object\nholds, `cat --in` for reading a notecard or a script where it is, and\n`take` for bringing in the object itself.
fetch:example:2	fetch	example	Examples	fetch --into /Notecards "brass lantern" README
fetch:example:3	fetch	example	Examples	fetch --move lantern only copy
find	find	line		find [-Ll] TEXT [PATH]\nlook for names containing TEXT, from here down\nhelp groups: inventory\nkeywords: search locate look for item name inventory folder path where is
find:intro	find	intro		`find` looks for names containing a piece of text, from a folder\ndownwards, and prints the full path of everything that matches.\n\n    find lantern\n\nThe first argument is the text and the second is where to start,\nwhich defaults to the folder the shell is in.  The paths printed are\nabsolute, and they are paths other commands take.
find:example:1	find	example		find lantern
find:option:-l	find	option	-l	The columns `ls -l` prints: what kind of thing it is, when it was\nacquired, its id, and the path.  The same row in the same shape, so\na search and a listing can be read the same way.\n\n    find -l "a hat"\n    object  2025-01-08T14:02:55  9b457e57-...  /Objects/a hat\n    link    2025-03-04T11:20:08  45557e57-...  /My Outfits/Sunday/a hat\n\nThe columns answer what bare paths cannot.  Names are not unique, so\na search that turns up four things of one name gives four identical\nlines without them; and a link carries the same name as the item it\npoints at, so `link` in the first column is the only thing that tells\nthem apart.\n\nThe id is there to be copied into another command.  Every command\nthat takes a path takes an id instead, which is the way to name one\nof several things sharing a name.\n\nWithout `-l`, one bare path per line, which is what makes a search\neditable into commands that take paths.
find:option:-L	find	option	-L	The same columns, with a link shown as the item it points at: its\nkind, its date and its id, under the path where the link was found.\nImplies `-l`.\n\nAn outfit folder holds nothing but links, every one named after the\nitem at the far end, so `-l` there gives a column of `link` and a\ncolumn of ids that name nothing outside this inventory.  `-L` turns\nthe same listing into what is actually worn:\n\n    find -L hat "/Current Outfit"\n    object     2025-04-14T17:44:09 cfb57e57-...  /Current Outfit/a hat\n\nThat is also the way to see clothing and body parts as against\nattachments, which `worn` cannot show: `worn` lists objects on\nattachment points, and a skin or a shape is not one.\n\nA link that cannot be followed keeps the word `link`, which says what\nhappened -- this is a link and following it got nowhere.  The id\ncolumn is the id it points at either way, since the link carries that\nalready.\n\nFollowing costs one walk of inventory for the whole listing, and only\nwhere the listing holds a link at all.
find:example:2	find	example	Options	find -l "a hat"
find:example:3	find	example	Options	link    2025-03-04T11:20:08  45557e57-...  /My Outfits/Sunday/a hat
find:example:4	find	example	Options	find -L hat "/Current Outfit"
find:section:A substring, not a pattern	find	section	A substring, not a pattern	The text matches anywhere in a name and case is ignored.  There is\nno wildcard.  A star at this prompt means what `rm` means by one,\nnot anything to do with searching.  `find lantern` finds "brass\nlantern" and "Lantern notes" alike, and there is no way to say\n"names beginning with".
find:section:What is searched, and how deep	find	section	What is searched, and how deep	Names, and only names.  Nothing here looks inside a notecard or a\nscript, and nothing looks at descriptions.\n\nFolders match as readily as items.  A folder is an entry with a\nname like any other.\n\nThe descent goes four levels below the folder it starts in.  Deeper\nthan that is not searched and is not reported as unsearched, so a\nresult of nothing from the root is not proof that nothing is there.\nStarting further down is the way to reach it.
find:section:Examples	find	section	Examples	    find lantern\n    find greeter /Scripts\n    find -l lantern\n    find -L hat "/Current Outfit"\n\nSee also: `ls`, `cd`, and `cat` for reading what a search turned up.
find:example:5	find	example	Examples	find greeter /Scripts
find:example:6	find	example	Examples	find -l lantern
friends	friends	line		friends [-a]\nfriends who are online, or -a for all of them\nhelp groups: people\nkeywords: online friend list buddies contacts who is on logged in
friends:intro	friends	intro		friends lists the people this avatar is friends with who are logged in.\nIt is the narrow list on purpose: a friend list of four hundred people,\nof whom two are online, answers a question nobody asked.\n\n    friends\n\nEach line is a number, a name and a key, and the listing is what the\nnext command means by a number -- `friends` then `im 2` is the ordinary\nway round, and the same number works for `profile`, `offer` or `give`.\n\nA word after it is dropped rather than refused: `friends someone`\nprints the whole online list, exactly as `friends` does, because there\nis nothing here to search.  The search is `lookup`, and it goes to the\ngrid rather than to this list.
friends:example:1	friends	example		friends
friends:option:-a	friends	option	-a	List everybody rather than the ones online.  Without it, only whoever\nis online.  The whole list is the one to use when the question is who\nsomebody has ever befriended, or when a name is wanted for a key.
friends:section:What the list is, and what it is not	friends	section	What the list is, and what it is not	The list is the session's, kept from login and updated as friends come\nand go and as friendships form.  It is not asked of the grid each time\nthis command is typed.\n\nOnline is what the other side granted this avatar the right to see, and\nno more than that.  It is not a promise that somebody is awake or\nwilling to be written to.
friends:section:Examples	friends	section	Examples	    friends\n    friends -a\n\nWrite to the second person listed:\n\n    friends\n    im 2 are you still at the build\n\nSee also: `offer` for making a friendship, `offers` for one waiting to\nbe answered, `who` for everybody in the region whether or not they are\nfriends, and `lookup` for everybody else.
friends:example:2	friends	example	Examples	friends -a
friends:example:3	friends	example	Examples	im 2 are you still at the build
get	get	line		get [-r] [-o FILE] PATH|UUID\nsave a texture as a PNG, by inventory path or by asset id\nhelp groups: inventory\nkeywords: download save texture image picture png export disk file
get:intro	get	intro		`get` saves a texture to disk as a PNG.  `put` is the other\ndirection.  Between them a texture can be fetched, edited and\nuploaded again -- though the upload is a new item and costs L$,\nwhich this does not.\n\n    get Textures/lantern\n\nThe argument is an inventory path, relative to the folder the shell\nis in, or a uuid.  Anything that is not a texture is refused, a\nfolder included.  A folder of textures is still a folder.
get:example:1	get	example		get Textures/lantern
get:option:-o, --out FILE	get	option	-o, --out FILE	Write here rather than to a file named after the texture.  Used\nexactly as given.
get:option:-r, --raw	get	option	-r, --raw	Write the JPEG 2000 codestream exactly as the grid stores it, in a\nfile ending `.j2c`.  That is the form to keep for putting the same\ntexture back, and it is also what to fall back on when a texture\nwill not decode -- the refusal says so.
get:section:A uuid here is an asset id	get	section	A uuid here is an asset id	Not an item id.  Textures are served by asset id alone, so a texture\non somebody else's object -- named nowhere in this avatar's\ninventory -- is fetchable the moment its id is known.  That id comes\nfrom wherever the object was inspected, not from a listing here: no\ninventory command prints an asset id, and the third column of\n`ls -l` is the item id, which the network does not answer to.  A\ntexture that is in inventory is named by its path instead, and `get`\nlooks its asset up itself.
get:section:PNG, or the codestream	get	section	PNG, or the codestream	The grid stores JPEG 2000.  What is written is a PNG, and the report\nsays the size it decoded to and how many bytes that came from.\n\nThe file is the item's name, with separators replaced, and `.png` or\n`.j2c` on the end.  A texture asked for by uuid has no name here, so\nthe file is called after the uuid.
get:section:Examples	get	section	Examples	    get Textures/lantern\n    get -o /tmp/wall.png d8467e57-...\n    get --raw Textures/lantern
get:example:2	get	example	Examples	get -o /tmp/wall.png d8467e57-...
get:example:3	get	example	Examples	get --raw Textures/lantern
get:section:A link is followed	get	section	A link is followed	An outfit folder holds links rather than items.  A link carries the\nsame name as the thing it points at, and a listing tells the two apart\nonly by the word `link` in the type column of `ls -l`, so a path taken\nfrom `My Outfits` names a link almost every time.\n\n`get` follows one to the item at the other end, which is what the id\nit sends has to be: the grid has no object for a link's id and answers\nan id it does not recognise with silence rather than with a refusal.\n\nA link this inventory cannot follow is refused, naming the id it\nlooked for.  A link outlives what it pointed at, so an outfit put\ntogether years ago may name things that have since been deleted.\n\nSee also: `put`, `cat` for a notecard or a script, and `ls` for what\nis there.
give	give	line		give WHO PATH\noffer an inventory item to somebody\nhelp groups: giving\nkeywords: send hand transfer offer item object gift inventory to person friend somebody
give:intro	give	intro		`give` offers an inventory item to somebody.  It is the other end of\n`accept`: what this sends arrives on their side as a question, and\nwhat they send arrives here as one.\n\nTwo things are named, the person and the item, in that order.  The\nperson is a name, a key, or the number beside a name in the last\nlisting; the item is an inventory path, relative to the folder the\nshell is in unless it begins with a slash, or the key of an item.\n\n    give Example Resident Objects/lantern\n\nThe key is the form to reach for where a folder holds two items of one\nname.  A path takes the first of them, in listing order, and says\nnothing about the other -- which is a worse bargain here than\nelsewhere, because this hands the thing to a person rather than\nprinting it.  `ls -l` puts the key beside each duplicate for exactly\nthis.\n\nA person is resolved the way `im` resolves one: the session's cache is\nasked, then whoever is standing in the region, and last the grid's own\nsearch, which reaches somebody who is nowhere near.  What the search\nfinds is taken only when it is the whole name -- `Example Resident`, or\n`example.resident` -- because it matches part of a name too, and a name\nguessed at wrong offers an item to a stranger.  Anything less is listed,\nnumbered and refused rather than guessed at, and the number is what to\ngive this.
give:example:1	give	example		give Example Resident Objects/lantern
give:section:An offer, not a transfer	give	section	An offer, not a transfer	Nothing moves until the other side accepts.  The report says "offered"\nrather than "gave" because that is all that has happened: the offer\ntravels as a message to them, and their answer travels back as\nanother, printed on this side as a notice saying the offer was\naccepted or declined.  Whether the item itself arrived is the part\nnobody is told.\n\nAn offer nobody answers stays pending, and nothing is told to either\nside.
give:section:How much of the line is the person	give	section	How much of the line is the person	A name has two words in it, so the person is the longest run of one or\ntwo words at the front that answers to somebody, and the rest of the\nline is the path.  Read as one word, a two-word name leaves its last\nword at the front of the path, and what follows is either an item that\ncannot be found or a different item, offered to the right person and\nreported as a success.\n\nEverything after the person is the path, spaces and all, so a name\nwith a space in it needs no quoting -- though quoting does no harm and\nsays plainly where the person ends and the item begins.
give:section:A folder goes as a folder	give	section	A folder goes as a folder	A folder may be given, and arrives as a folder rather than as its\ncontents.
give:section:Examples	give	section	Examples	    give Example Resident Objects/lantern\n\nQuoting a path that has a space in it:\n\n    give Another Resident "Notecards/build notes"
give:example:2	give	example	Examples	give Another Resident "Notecards/build notes"
give:section:A link is followed	give	section	A link is followed	An outfit folder holds links rather than items.  A link carries the\nsame name as the thing it points at, and a listing tells the two apart\nonly by the word `link` in the type column of `ls -l`, so a path taken\nfrom `My Outfits` names a link almost every time.\n\n`give` follows one to the item at the other end, which is what the id\nit sends has to be: the grid has no object for a link's id and answers\nan id it does not recognise with silence rather than with a refusal.\n\nA link this inventory cannot follow is refused, naming the id it\nlooked for.  A link outlives what it pointed at, so an outfit put\ntogether years ago may name things that have since been deleted.\n\nSee also: `accept` and `decline` for offers arriving, `offers` for the\nones waiting, `ls` and `cd` for finding the path, `lookup` or `who`\nfor finding the person, and `cp` for making a copy to give away rather\nthan the one being used.
group	group	line		group [NAME|UUID|none]\nwhat this avatar is acting as, and what it could act as; land rights hang on it\nhelp groups: region\nkeywords: active group title tag land rights acting as switch groups joined list which
group:intro	group	intro		group says which group this avatar is acting as, lists the ones it has\njoined, and with an argument makes one of them active.  It is not about\njoining or leaving: an invitation into a group arrives in `waiting` and\nis answered there.\n\n    group\n\nWith nothing after it, the listing marks the active one in a column of\nits own and gives each group's key beside its name.  With a name, a key\nor the word `none`, that becomes the active group and the command waits\nfor the simulator to agree before saying so.  A name with a space in it\nneeds no quoting: the rest of the line is the name.
group:example:1	group	example		group
group:section:Why this matters more than it looks	group	section	Why this matters more than it looks	Land usually grants building to a group rather than to individuals, so\nwhether an avatar may rez something somewhere depends on which group it\nis acting as and not on who it is.  An avatar acting as nobody is\nrefused by land that would otherwise allow it, and the refusal talks\nabout the land rather than about the group, then sends the reader to\nthe land tool, which has nothing to show.\n\nA viewer remembers the active group from one session to the next and a\nheadless login does not: the login itself always comes up acting as\nnobody, and a refusal that blames the land is the first sign of it.\n\nWhen slgod brings a session up it settles the group as well.  A profile\nunder `~/.config/slgo` may carry\n\n    group = Example Builders\n\nby name or by key, and an avatar that has joined exactly one group and\nsaid nothing about it gets that one.  So an avatar the daemon brought\nup may well be acting as something already, and this is the command\nthat says which.
group:example:2	group	example	Why this matters more than it looks	group = Example Builders
group:section:Acting as nobody is spelled none	group	section	Acting as nobody is spelled none	The way back out is the word, because the thing it stands for is a key\nof thirty-two zeros and nobody would guess that from a listing that\nsays "acting as no group".  The word wins over a group that happens to\nbe called it; such a group is still reachable by its key, which the\nlisting prints beside every name.
group:section:An empty list is not the same as no groups	group	section	An empty list is not the same as no groups	Nothing asks for the list: it arrives on its own shortly after login,\nand again whenever the membership changes.  So an empty one means "not\ntold yet" exactly as much as it means "belongs to none", and the\nlisting says both rather than picking one.  Trying again in a moment is\nwhat settles it.\n\nA name is matched against that list, whole and without regard to case,\nand nothing else.  A key is sent as typed, even if the list has not\narrived yet.  An avatar that is not a member is what a key that never\nbecomes active usually means, and that is what the timeout says.  Two\ngroups of one name are refused with their keys rather than guessed\nbetween.
group:section:Examples	group	section	Examples	    group\n    group Example Builders\n    group none\n\nSee also: `place` and `rez` for the commands land refuses when this is\nwrong, `waiting` and `answer` for an invitation into a group, and\n`profile` for the groups somebody else has chosen to publish, which is\na different list from this one.
group:example:3	group	example	Examples	group Example Builders
group:example:4	group	example	Examples	group none
help	help	line		help [GROUP|all]\nthe command groups; "help GROUP" for one, "help all" for everything\nhelp groups: shell\nkeywords: commands list groups overview what can do index usage
help:intro	help	intro		`help` is the index.  Commands are gathered into groups: a name,\nwhat the group is for, and how many commands are in it.  Naming one\nlists its commands with the line that says how each is typed, and\n`help all` is every command at once.\n\n    help\n\nThe groups are the questions somebody has -- how do I look at\ninventory, how do I talk to somebody, which avatar am I driving.\n\nA command may be in more than one group.  Copying an item is an\ninventory operation, and it is also how an avatar that may not rez\ngets another object, so it is listed under both.  Looking in either\nplace finds it.
help:example:1	help	example		help
help:section:It never describes a single command	help	section	It never describes a single command	That is the command's own business, and there are two ways to ask\nit: the command itself says what it takes, and `man` says what it is\nfor and the traps.  Asking `help` about a command is\nanswered by pointing at those two rather than by refusing, and a\nword that is neither a group nor a command is an error, since it is\na typo.\n\nA name can be both a group and a command without ambiguity: here it\nalways means the group.  `waiting` is both, and `help waiting` is\nthe group.
help:section:Two names for one command are listed once	help	section	Two names for one command are listed once	Some commands answer to two names -- `quit` and `exit`, `.` and\n`source`, `stand` and `unsit` -- and they are not two entries but\none entry reached twice.  `help all` walks the commands rather than\nthe names, so each of those appears once, under whichever of its\nnames sorts first: `exit`, and `.`, and `stand`.  The other name is\nneither missing nor discouraged.\n\nA group, on the other hand, lists whichever name its own line names,\nso `help shell` says `quit` where `help all` says `exit`.  Anyone\nsearching a listing for a name they know is there wants `man`'s: it\nis the one that lists every name rather than every command.
help:section:Examples	help	section	Examples	    help\n    help region\n    help all > commands\n\nSee also: `man`, and the command itself, which answers what it\ntakes.
help:example:2	help	example	Examples	help region
help:example:3	help	example	Examples	help all > commands
how	how	line		how [-ir] QUESTION...\nwhich command does what you describe, with a line to type and its man page's words\nhelp groups: shell\nkeywords: question ask which command does what describe find command search suggest words
how:intro	how	intro		`how` answers "which command does this?" for somebody who knows what\nthey want to do and not what slsh calls it.  The question is the\nwhole line, `how` included, in ordinary words, and needs no quoting:\n\n    how do I make where I am standing my home\n\nThe answer is a line to type, why it does what was asked, and the\nsentence from that command's man page that says so:\n\n    to type:  landmark --set-home\n      it sets home to where the avatar is standing\n      "Make where this avatar is standing the place home is." -- man landmark\n\nNothing is run.  The line is for you to read, change and type.
how:example:1	how	example		how do I make where I am standing my home
how:option:-i, --index	how	option	-i, --index	Answer from the index alone, without asking the model, even when one\nis set up.  Quicker, and the same answer `how` gives when there is no\nmodel to ask.
