how:option:-r, --rejected	how	option	-r, --rejected	Also list what the model suggested that the checks refused, each with\nthe reasons.  Those lines are printed quoted and under a heading that\nsays they are not commands; they are there to show what was thrown\naway, not to be typed.
how:section:How it answers	how	section	How it answers	`how` looks the question up in an index of every command's usage\nline, description and man page, built into slsh when it was compiled,\nso it is never out of step with the commands.  The commands that\nmatch best are shown to a language model, with the parts of their\npages that matched, and it is asked which of them does what was asked.\n\nWhat the model says is then checked before any of it is printed:\n\n- the line must run a real command, with flags that command takes,\n  parsed the way the shell would parse it when typed;\n- where a command takes a word from a list slsh keeps -- a setting's\n  name for `set`, a rating for `maturity`, a group for `help` -- the\n  word must be on that list, or be the capitalised placeholder the\n  usage line writes, like NAME;\n- the quotation must be found, word for word, in that command's man\n  page, usage line or description.\n\nA suggestion that fails any of these is dropped.  If every suggestion\nwas dropped, the model is told why, once, and asked again.  Nothing\nelse the model writes is printed, because nothing checks it; only the\nlines that passed, their reasons and their quotations are.\n\nWhen nothing passes, `how` says plainly that it found no command for\nthat, and names the nearest commands with their descriptions, so you\ncan read their pages yourself.
how:section:Without a model	how	section	Without a model	With no `how_url` set, with `--index`, or when the server does not\nanswer, `how` says which of those it is and answers from the index\nalone: the best-matching commands, each with its description and the\nexample lines from its own page.  Those are lines a person wrote in\nthe page, not guesses.\n\n    From the index alone (how_url is not set); the commands whose pages match best:\n\n    landmark -- ...\n        landmark --home\n        ...
how:example:2	how	example	Without a model	landmark -- ...
how:example:3	how	example	Without a model	landmark --home
how:section:Setting up a model	how	section	Setting up a model	Any server that speaks the OpenAI-style `/v1/chat/completions`, such\nas llama-server or Ollama, running a small model.  Five settings, set\nwith `set` like any other:\n\n    how_url        where the server is, with http:// in front; empty is no model\n    how_model      the model's name; llama-server ignores it, Ollama needs it\n    how_slot       a llama-server slot to use; empty is whichever is free\n    how_timeout    how long to wait, like 45s; empty is two minutes\n    how_extra      a JSON object added to every request\n\nFor Ollama on the same machine, with a model it has pulled:\n\n    set how_url http://127.0.0.1:11434\n    set how_model qwen3.5:4b\n\n`how_extra` is for what one model needs and another would refuse.  A\nmodel that reasons at length before it answers should be told not to.\nUnder Ollama, for Qwen3 and Qwen3.5:\n\n    set how_extra '{"reasoning_effort": "none"}'\n\nUnder llama-server, whose documentation gives this form:\n\n    set how_extra '{"chat_template_kwargs": {"enable_thinking": false}}'\n\nThe single quotes matter.  The shell takes double quotes off a word\nthe way it does anywhere else, and JSON without its double quotes is\nnot JSON, so `set` would refuse it.\n\nWhich options a model takes is the model's and the server's business,\nso nothing of the kind is built in.  The slsh guide says which models\nwere measured, and how to share a llama-server that slbotd uses.
how:example:4	how	example	Setting up a model	set how_url http://127.0.0.1:11434
how:example:5	how	example	Setting up a model	set how_model qwen3.5:4b
how:example:6	how	example	Setting up a model	set how_extra '{"reasoning_effort": "none"}'
how:example:7	how	example	Setting up a model	set how_extra '{"chat_template_kwargs": {"enable_thinking": false}}'
how:section:Your own words	how	section	Your own words	A file called `how-hints.tsv`, beside the settings file, teaches `how`\nthe words you use.  One hint per line: the words, a tab, and the\ncommands they mean.\n\n    # my words for things\n    snapshot picture\tlook texture\n    stash\tput\n\nA question containing every word on the left brings the commands on\nthe right to the top.  Blank lines and lines starting with `#` are\nskipped.  A line without a tab is an error naming the line; a command\nname slsh does not have is left out, and `how` says so each time it\nreads the file.
how:section:Examples	how	section	Examples	    how do I make where I am standing my home\n    how can I see which avatars are near me\n    how -i do I take off an attachment\n    how -r do I list what is in a folder\n\nSee also: `man` for a command's whole page, `help` for the commands\nby group, and `set` for the settings.
how:example:8	how	example	Examples	how can I see which avatars are near me
how:example:9	how	example	Examples	how -i do I take off an attachment
how:example:10	how	example	Examples	how -r do I list what is in a folder
ignore	ignore	line		ignore N\nleave it waiting, but stop counting it at the prompt\nhelp groups: waiting\nkeywords: dismiss skip hide set aside waiting count prompt later
ignore:intro	ignore	intro		`ignore` sets one of the things `waiting` lists aside: it stays\nwaiting, and the prompt stops counting it.\n\nNothing is said to whoever asked.  That is the whole of the difference\nbetween `ignore` and `no` -- `no` sends a refusal and ends it,\n`ignore` is this shell's own note that this one is not being dealt\nwith now.  A script asking for a permission that is not wanted, a\ndialog from an object nobody is using: the count at the prompt is only\nuseful while it means things not yet looked at, and one of these left\nin it makes the count worth nothing.\n\n    ignore 4\n    waiting -a\n\nIt is this shell's, not the session's.  Another client attached to the\nsame avatar has its own idea of what it has dealt with, and ignoring\nsomething here does not reach it.\n\n`waiting -a` lists what has been set aside.  Answering or declining\none takes it out of the set again, and out of the listing in the same\nbreath.\n\nSee also: `waiting`, `answer`, `no`.
ignore:example:1	ignore	example		ignore 4
ignore:example:2	ignore	example		waiting -a
im	im	line		im WHO [TEXT]\nopen a conversation, or send one message to it\nhelp groups: people\nkeywords: private message instant message direct message send tell person somebody
im:intro	im	intro		im says something to one person.  A remark out loud reaches whoever is\nstanding near, and this reaches the person named and nobody else,\nwherever they are.\n\n    im Example Resident the lantern is rezzed\n\nWho is named by a name, by a key, or by the number beside a name in the\nlast listing of people, which is what `who`, `friends` and `lookup`\nprint -- and `profile` too, where the name it was given answered to\nseveral people and it listed them to be picked from.  With words after\nthe name they are the message, sent at once, and the shell stays in\ncommand mode.  With nothing after it the conversation is opened and the\nshell enters chat mode, which is what `chat` on somebody does.\n\nEither way the conversation joins the list that tab cycles between, and\nstays there for the rest of the session.
im:example:1	im	example		im Example Resident the lantern is rezzed
im:section:How much of the line is the name	im	section	How much of the line is the name	A Second Life name has two words in it, so the name is the longest run\nof one or two words at the front of the line that answers to somebody,\nand everything after that run is the message.  Two words are tried\nfirst: `im Example Resident hello` says "hello" to Example Resident,\nbecause the pair names them; `im Example hello there` says "hello\nthere", because "Example hello" names nobody and the run shortens.\n\nA name is matched on either half and on the start of it, so reading the\nfirst word as the name and the rest as the message would send the last\nname as the first word of what was said -- to the right person, reported\nas a success, with the mistake visible only to whoever received it.\n\nA name that could be several people is refused with all of them named\nrather than shortened, since every shorter run matches at least as\nmany.  Typing more of the name is the answer.
im:section:Who a name may be	im	section	Who a name may be	The session's own cache is asked first, then whoever is standing in the\nregion, and last the grid's own search -- the one `lookup` makes -- so\nsomebody on the other side of the grid can be named as plainly as\nsomebody standing here.  The search is made only when nothing nearer\nknew the name, which is a line that was about to be refused anyway.\n\nWhat the search finds is taken only when it is the name itself:\n`Example Resident` in any case, or `example.resident` with the dot a\nusername has.  The search matches part of a name, and display names\ntoo, so what it turns up is everybody the words resemble; taking one of\nthem would deliver a private message to a stranger.  So anything short\nof the whole name is listed, numbered, and refused, and the number is\nwhat to give this:\n\n    im Example the lantern is rezzed\n     1  Example Resident\n     2  Example Wanderer\n    slsh: im: nobody on the grid is called "Example", and the 2 listed\n    have names like it; a number picks one\n    im 1 the lantern is rezzed\n\nA first name alone is not a whole name here, even for somebody whose\nusername it is, and neither is one row that is merely the closest the\nsearch came.\n\nA search that could not be made -- the grid not answering, or a session\nnever given the search -- finds nobody, and the refusal says so, since\nthat is the one part worth trying again.\n\nNothing comes back to say a message was read, or that it was delivered\nat all.
im:example:2	im	example	Who a name may be	im Example the lantern is rezzed
im:example:3	im	example	Who a name may be	im 1 the lantern is rezzed
im:section:Examples	im	section	Examples	    im Example Resident the lantern is rezzed\n\nOpen the conversation and go on talking in chat mode:\n\n    im Another Resident\n\nSee also: `chat`, `talk`, `say`, and `lookup` or `who` for finding\nwhoever is meant.
im:example:4	im	example	Examples	im Another Resident
invite	invite	line		invite WHO GROUP\nask somebody into one of this avatar's groups; nothing answers, so it is an asking\nhelp groups: people region\nkeywords: invite ask join group member add somebody role
invite:intro	invite	intro		Asks somebody into one of this avatar's groups.  They get an instant\nmessage and decide for themselves; nothing here can make anybody a\nmember, and nothing here is told what they decided.\n\nThe person is read off the front of the line and the rest of it is the\ngroup, so neither name needs quoting:\n\n    invite Example Resident Example Builders\n    invite Example Resident 488f7e57-...\n\nThe person need not be anywhere near, which is often why they are being\ninvited.  A name nothing here has heard of is looked for with the\ngrid's own search, as `im` does it, and taken only when it is the whole\nname; anything less is listed, numbered and refused, and the number is\nwhat to give this.\n\nThe group is named every time and has no default.  It could have meant\nwhichever group this avatar is acting as, which would be shorter and\nwrong the first time somebody typed it after activating something\nelse -- and what this command does reaches a person who is not at this\nprompt.  There is no message that cancels an invitation, so the group\nbelongs on the line, where it can be read before pressing return.
invite:example:1	invite	example		invite Example Resident Example Builders
invite:example:2	invite	example		invite Example Resident 488f7e57-...
invite:section:Nothing answers an invitation	invite	section	Nothing answers an invitation	The request has no reply.  `InviteGroupResponse` exists in the message\ntemplate and is a trusted message -- simulator to simulator -- so a\nclient never sees one.  Measured on Agni on 2026-09-02: an invitation\nthat arrived at the far end within seconds produced nothing whatever\non the sender's circuit.\n\nSo the line printed here says what was sent, in those words, and never\nthat anybody was invited or joined:\n\n    asked Example Resident into Example Builders (488f7e57-...), in its\n    everyone role\n    nothing answers an invitation, so this says what was sent and not what\n    became of it; they see it as an instant message and it waits for them\n\nWhether they joined cannot be seen from this avatar at all.  The\nmembership list this shell reads is this avatar's own, and somebody\nelse's memberships are not in it; the answer to the invitation goes to\nthe group rather than to whoever sent it.  Asking the person is the\nway to find out.
invite:section:What the person on the other end sees	invite	section	What the person on the other end sees	An instant message the simulator composes, which their viewer shows as\na dialog and which this shell lists under `waiting`.  Measured, as the\ninvitee's session printed it:\n\n    1  group       quark.idlemind invites you into a group: "Quark Idlemind\n                   (quark.idlemind) has invited you to join a group.\\nThere is\n                   no cost to join this group.\\nGroup:\\nQuark Engineering\n                   Works: A group for testing slgo's group commands.\\n\\n" (no fee)\n                    answer, no, ignore\n\nThe group's name is inside the text the simulator wrote and nowhere\nelse in the message, which is why the invitation this shell receives\nprints as a paragraph rather than as fields.  The inviter appears as a\nusername -- `quark.idlemind` -- and not as the display name.\n\nIt waits for them whether or not they are logged in.  What it does not\ndo is wait for a shell: an invitation that arrives while no client is\nattached to that avatar's session is not in a listing made afterwards,\nbecause `waiting` is a client's own record of what it saw arrive.  Two\ninvitations sent while nothing was attached were invisible to the next\n`slsh` to start.
invite:example:3	invite	example	What the person on the other end sees	no cost to join this group.\\nGroup:\\nQuark Engineering
invite:section:What is checked before it goes	invite	section	What is checked before it goes	Two things, and they are checked because the grid answers both of them\nwith silence:\n\n- **that this avatar is in the group.**  Only a member invites.\n- **that its role there carries the power to invite.**  Membership is\n  not the power.  Measured: an ordinary member's everyone role carries\n  `0x0000080018010000`, which does not include it, where the group's\n  owner carries every bit.\n\nBoth refuse before anything is sent, and say which of the two it was:\n\n    slsh: invite: sl: this avatar cannot invite into that group: this avatar\n    is in "Example Builders" (488f7e57-...) and its role there does not carry\n    the power to invite\n\n    slsh: invite: sl: this avatar cannot invite into that group: this avatar\n    is not a member of 93787e57-..., and only a member invites\n\nThe membership list they are read out of is the one `group` prints.\nIt arrives unasked shortly after login, so an avatar that has not been\ntold yet is allowed to try rather than refused on a list that has not\ncome.\n\nA group named by a word has to be one this avatar has joined, since the\nlist is the only place a name can be looked up; a key is taken as\ntyped.  That is `group`'s rule and this follows it.
invite:section:The role	invite	section	The role	The invitation is into the group's everyone role, which every group has\nand which Linden Lab's viewer starts on.  Nothing here offers the\nothers: reading a group's roles is a request this shell does not make,\nand a role named by a key nobody could look up would be worse to type\nthan none.
invite:section:What it costs the person invited	invite	section	What it costs the person invited	A group may charge to join, and the person accepting pays it -- their\nmoney and their decision.  The fee is stated in the invitation they\nsee, and this shell says it back to them when they are the one being\ninvited.  Nothing on this side states it: the invitation is composed by\nthe simulator, and the sender is told neither the fee nor the answer.
invite:section:Examples	invite	section	Examples	    invite Example Resident Example Builders\n    invite Example Resident 488f7e57-...\n\nSee also: `group` for the groups this avatar has joined and which one\nit is acting as, `who` and `lookup` for finding somebody by name,\n`offer` for offering friendship rather than a group, and `waiting` for\nthe invitations that arrive at THIS avatar and how to answer them.
landmark	landmark	line		landmark [--go] [--home] [--make] [--set-home] [-w SECONDS] [NAME]\nthe landmarks in inventory, where one goes, going there, and going home or setting it\nhelp groups: region\nkeywords: home set home go home landmarks places favourite bookmark location teleport saved place create make
landmark:intro	landmark	intro		A place said by name, where `tp` is a place said in numbers.  A\nlandmark is an ordinary inventory item -- the grid's own way of writing\na place down -- and this lists the ones this avatar holds, says where\none of them goes, makes one of wherever the avatar is standing, and\ngoes to one.  Home is here too: going to it, and setting it.\n\nWith no arguments it lists them, as paths, so that two landmarks of one\nname can be told apart.  With a name it reads that one and prints where\nit goes, without moving.  Going there is asked for separately, and so\nis going home:\n\n    landmark\n    landmark Example Workshop\n    landmark --make Example Workshop\n    landmark --go Example Workshop\n    landmark --home\n    landmark --set-home\n\nThe avatar moves in `--go` and `--home` and in nothing else.  That is\ndeliberate.  If going somewhere were the bare form, then reading a\nlandmark and being somewhere else afterwards would be one typo apart.\nNone of the four verbs has a short letter, for the same reason: `-g`\nbeside `-m` is exactly the typo a whole word prevents.  `--set-home`\ncarries the word "set" for a sharper version of it: a `-h` that meant\n"make this home" beside a `-h` that meant "go home" is one keystroke\nbetween a journey and a rewritten account setting, and only one of\nthose is undone by teleporting back.\n\nA name with spaces in it needs no quoting: the words after the flags\nare joined back together, as `tp` joins a region name.\n\nThe whole of inventory is looked in, not just Landmarks, but only four\nlevels down.  A landmark buried deeper than that is not listed and is\nnot found by name, and what comes back is "no landmark called that"\nrather than anything about depth.  `find` counts the same four levels\nfrom wherever it is started, so it will not see it either; an `ls` of\nthe folder it is in will.
landmark:example:1	landmark	example		landmark
landmark:example:2	landmark	example		landmark Example Workshop
landmark:example:3	landmark	example		landmark --make Example Workshop
landmark:example:4	landmark	example		landmark --go Example Workshop
landmark:example:5	landmark	example		landmark --home
landmark:example:6	landmark	example		landmark --set-home
landmark:option:--make	landmark	option	--make	Make a landmark of where this avatar is standing, called *NAME*.\nMaking one, going to one, going home and setting home are four\ndifferent things; ask for one.
landmark:option:--go	landmark	option	--go	Go to the landmark *NAME* names.
landmark:option:--home	landmark	option	--home	Go to wherever this account's home is set.  It takes no name.
landmark:option:--set-home	landmark	option	--set-home	Make where this avatar is standing the place home is.  It takes no\nname and no position: home is set where the avatar stands, and\nnowhere else.  Nothing moves.
landmark:option:-w, --wait SECONDS	landmark	option	-w, --wait SECONDS	How long to wait for the avatar to arrive, on `--go` and `--home`.\nWithout it, thirty seconds -- `tp`'s wait, for the same kind of trip.\nIt is refused on the reading form and on `--set-home`, neither of\nwhich moves the avatar.
landmark:section:A landmark is a point and not a place	landmark	section	A landmark is a point and not a place	What the asset holds is a region id and three numbers.  It carries no\nregion name, no parcel, no owner and no description of anything.  A\nlandmark remembers where the avatar stood and nothing about what was\nthere.  `parcel` is the command that asks, after arriving.\n\nNothing keeps the far end still.  The parcel may have been sold, the\nplatform stood on taken away, the region emptied or removed.  A\nlandmark into what is now empty air is an arrival in empty air followed\nby a fall.
landmark:section:Arriving somewhere is not the same as staying there	landmark	section	Arriving somewhere is not the same as staying there	This command reports the arrival and stops watching.  Measured twice on\nAgni: a landmark into a parcel with a security orb on it succeeded --\nthe avatar arrived, the command printed the position, and about one and\na third seconds later the parcel's own object teleported it home.  So a\nposition printed here can be true when it is printed and false a moment\nafterwards.  If a landmark lands somewhere it should not be, run\n`where` again.\n\nThe narrower case is unmeasured: what a parcel that refuses this avatar\nby its access settings does to a landmark arrival, as against one that\nadmits everybody and then throws them out.\n\nThe name is printed before the waiting starts, and what is printed\nafter the arrival is where the avatar actually ended up, read back.\n\nEverything the shell knew about the region it left is dropped on the\nway, exactly as it is for `tp`.
landmark:section:Where it goes is an id	landmark	section	Where it goes is an id	The line the reading form prints as `region` is the region's grid-wide\nuuid, because that is what the asset says.  It is not a region name and\nit cannot be turned into one here: the only way this program has of\nfinding a region is the world map's search by name, and a landmark\ncarries no name to search for.  Going there needs no map lookup: the\nsimulator already has the region id.
landmark:section:An item has two ids and the grid takes only one	landmark	section	An item has two ids and the grid takes only one	Every inventory item has an item id and an asset id.  `ls -l` prints\nthe first, and the second is the one every landmark message takes.\nThey are both uuids and nothing about either says which it is.\n\nSent the wrong one, the grid says nothing at all -- no start, no\nprogress, no refusal -- so the wait is the only way to find out.  It\nruns to the end of `--wait`, thirty seconds unless another number was\ngiven, and what is printed then says that the item id where the asset\nid was wanted is the usual reason for the silence.\n\nThat is why a name here means something in inventory and never a bare\nuuid handed on to the grid: the asset id comes out of the listing,\nwhich is the one place the two are told apart correctly.  A uuid may be\ntyped, and it is looked up in that same listing as either of an item's\ntwo ids; a uuid this avatar does not hold is refused rather than sent.\n\nA landmark with no asset id in it is refused rather than being allowed\nto become a quiet trip home: the null id is the one wrong id the grid\nis not silent about, and it reads it as home.\n\nA link to a landmark is not a landmark, and is left out of the listing\nfor the same reason: a link's asset id is the item id of the thing it\npoints at, so following one would produce exactly that silence.  Name\nthe landmark itself.
landmark:section:What is in the trash is not reached from here	landmark	section	What is in the trash is not reached from here	A viewer's delete moves things to the trash rather than destroying\nthem, so a deleted landmark still has a name, still has an asset and\nwould still teleport.  The trash is left out.  Nothing in it is listed,\nand nothing in it can be named -- not by its name, not by its whole\npath, and not by either of its ids.  `mv` moves a landmark back out of\nthe trash, and then it is a landmark again.\n\nA name whose only answer is in the trash is told so, and told where it\nis.  A listing with nothing in it says how many of them are deleted.\n\nThe trash is found by its folder type and not by its name.  It can be\nrenamed, and an account made through a viewer in another language never\ncalled it "Trash" in the first place.
landmark:section:Making one	landmark	section	Making one	The position is written by the simulator out of where it believes this\navatar is at the instant the message lands, so for a walking avatar it\nis not quite the place it was asked from.  What is printed afterwards\nis read back out of the asset rather than assumed.\n\nNo folder is named on the way out.  The simulator files a new landmark\nunder Landmarks without being asked.  If the asset will not read, the\nitem is made anyway and the command says so.\n\nA name is not made unique by anything.  Two landmarks called the same\nthing are two landmarks called the same thing, and naming one of them\nafterwards is refused with both listed.  Where they are in different\nfolders the whole path chooses between them; where they are in the same\nfolder the path is the same for both and the id beside each is the only\nthing that tells them apart.  Making a landmark of a name that is\nalready taken says so at the time.
landmark:section:Home is the one landmark nobody has to own	landmark	section	Home is the one landmark nobody has to own	Going home is the same message with the null id in it, which the grid\nreads as home rather than as an error.  It costs no inventory lookup\nand needs no item.  `tp home` is the same trip said the short way.\n\nGoing home while standing at home is refused, and the refusal is the\none a landmark under your feet gets: `CouldntTPCloser`, the grid\ndescribing its own arithmetic.  The line says what it usually means.
landmark:section:Setting home	landmark	section	Setting home	`landmark --set-home` makes where this avatar is standing the place\nhome is.  It is not a landmark and no landmark is involved: it is one\nmessage that carries a position and the direction the avatar is\nfacing, and the region is not in it at all.  Whichever simulator\nreceives it is the region, which is why home can be set where the\navatar is and nowhere else.  To put home somewhere else, go there\nfirst and then set it.\n\nHome may be set on land this account controls and at a mainland\ninfohub.  Anywhere else it is refused, and both answers arrive the\nsame way -- as an alert, with no reply and no field that says home\nmoved.  So the grid's own sentence is what is printed, unchanged.\nMeasured on Agni on 2026-09-01, on two parcels a region apart:\n\n    setting home to Pelmar Reach at 26, 66, 24\n    Home position set.\n\nand, a region away and on land this account does not control:\n\n    setting home to Corvane at 44, 66, 24\n    slsh: landmark: sl: the grid would not set home here: You can only set your 'Home Location' on your land or at a mainland Infohub.\n\nThe position printed is the one the message carried, and not a second\nreading taken to print it.  The difference is real: an avatar that has\njust teleported is still settling, and two readings a second apart were\neight metres apart when this was written.  What the line says is where\nhome was set, whether the grid then agreed or refused.\n\nThat is also why setting home the moment after arriving somewhere can\nput it a metre or two above where the avatar comes to rest.  Home is\nset where the avatar is, and just after a teleport that is still\nfalling.\n\nWhere home is cannot be read.  Nothing in the protocol answers the\nquestion and nothing here keeps it, so the only way to find out is to\ngo there: `landmark --home`, and then `where`.  A home just set is\ntherefore worth checking by going to it if it matters, and that is\nalso the only way to see that a home set a month ago is still where\nyou think.
landmark:section:Examples	landmark	section	Examples	    landmark\n    landmark Example Workshop\n    landmark /Landmarks/Example Workshop\n    landmark --make Example Workshop\n    landmark --go Example Workshop\n    landmark --wait 60 --go Example Workshop\n    landmark --home\n    landmark --set-home\n\nSee also: `tp` for a place said in numbers, for `tp home`, and for what\na teleport costs and how it is refused, `where` for the position a\nlandmark is made from and the position home is set to, `parcel` for\nwhat the land at the far end turns out to be, and for whether it is\nland this account controls,\n`ls` for the items themselves and the two ids each of them has, `mv`\nfor taking one back out of the trash, and `regions` for finding a place\nby name when there is no landmark to it.
landmark:example:7	landmark	example	Examples	landmark /Landmarks/Example Workshop
landmark:example:8	landmark	example	Examples	landmark --wait 60 --go Example Workshop
link	link	line		link [-w SECONDS] ROOT CHILD...\njoin objects into one; the first is the root and its name is the object's\nhelp groups: region\nkeywords: join combine merge prims objects linkset connect
link:intro	link	intro		`link` joins objects standing in the region into one.  The first named\nis the root, its name becomes the object's, and `unlink` is the way\nback.\n\n    link chair "left leg" "right leg"\n\nEach argument is one object, named by the word the region calls it or\nby its key, so a name with a space in it has to be quoted.  That is the\nopposite of `unlink`'s rule, which joins its arguments into one name,\nand the two differ because the commands do: this one has a list to read\nand that one has a single thing to find.
link:example:1	link	example		link chair "left leg" "right leg"
link:option:-w, --wait SECONDS	link	option	-w, --wait SECONDS	How long to let the region describe itself.  Without it, thirty.
link:section:Naming one object twice is refused	link	section	Naming one object twice is refused	A prim cannot be linked to itself, and the simulator would take such a\nmessage without comment: nothing would happen, and the report would say\nso in numbers rather than in words.  So a repeated name is a typo and\nis treated as one.\n\nA name that several objects answer to is refused as well, with their\nkeys printed, since linking the wrong one is not something that undoes\nitself.
link:section:The report is read back, not counted	link	section	The report is read back, not counted	What is printed afterwards is what the region says the object is now,\nrather than a count of the arguments given.  Linking brings a linkset's\nprims along, so joining two things can make an object of seven -- and\nthat is exactly the case worth telling somebody about.\n\nIt waits for the linking to have happened rather than reporting the\nrequest and stopping.  What says it worked is the children naming the\nroot as their parent, which arrives when it arrives.
link:section:Examples	link	section	Examples	Three things into one, the chair first so the object is a chair:\n\n    link chair "left leg" "right leg"\n\nA thing whose name is shared, named by key instead:\n\n    link sign d8467e57-...\n\nSee also: `unlink`, `objects` for the names and keys to give it, `dump`\nfor what the linkset then looks like written down, and `take`, which\nbrings a linkset in as one item.
link:example:2	link	example	Examples	link sign d8467e57-...
login	login	line		login [-f] NAME\nbring an avatar up that is not running\nhelp groups: avatars\nkeywords: log in sign in bring up start avatar account bot session connect
login:intro	login	intro		`login` asks the daemon to bring an avatar up.  The argument is a\nprofile name from the first column of `agents`, not an avatar's name\nand not a uuid.  Flags come before the name.\n\n    login builder\n\nIt has to be named.  Logging an avatar in puts it in the world -- an\narrival, a presence, a notice to whoever watches for that name -- so\nthere is no default.\n\nAsking twice is safe.  An avatar already up is reported as already\nup and nothing else happens: logging it in again would kick the\nsession it has.  Two shells asking at the same moment produce one\nlogin between them for the same reason.
login:example:1	login	example		login builder
login:option:-f, --force	login	option	-f, --force	Accepted for symmetry with `logout`, and changes nothing here: naming\nan avatar IS asking for it back, so `login` always asks.  It does not\noverrule a login the daemon has already refused for a failed attempt;\nthe wait printed with that refusal is still the wait.
login:section:A session that is down and staying down	login	section	A session that is down and staying down	Something stopped it, and the likeliest something is a person now\nusing that avatar in a viewer.  `login` starts it anyway, because\ntyping the name is the deliberate act of asking for it back -- what\nthe daemon refuses unasked is a DAEMON retrying, which would undo the\nlogout by itself.  slbotd asks without forcing for exactly that reason\nand is exactly what should be refused.\n\nSo read the reason before typing it.  The check the refusal asks for\nwas never one the daemon could make: a stopped session has no clients,\nso nobody here can be using it, and what it warns about is somebody\nlogged in as that avatar in their own viewer -- which slgod cannot see\nand no flag here could have checked.\n\nThe refusal says why it went down rather than when, and that is the\npart worth reading before overruling.  `logged out` is somebody\nhaving asked for it here.  Anything beginning `ended by the grid` is\nthe avatar having been thrown off, and the grid's own words follow;\nthe commonest cause of that is the avatar being logged in somewhere\nelse, which means `-f` would take it back off whoever has it.\n\nA login that was refused is a different case.  The daemon remembers\nthe refusal and will not try again for a while, and says how long.\nA login server throttles whatever hammers it, and the throttling then\narrives as a quite different failure, so the wait is the difference\nbetween one clear error and an afternoon of confusing ones.
login:section:What the daemon has to have been told	login	section	What the daemon has to have been told	Only a daemon started with profiles to log in can start one on\ndemand.  One that was given no way to do it says so rather than\nfailing obscurely, and a name it has never heard of is an error and\nnot a silence.  A shell that logged in for itself has no daemon to\nask at all.
login:section:Examples	login	section	Examples	    login builder\n    login -f helper\n\nSee also: `logout`, `agents`, `status`, and `viewer` for putting a\nreal viewer on the session once it is up.
login:example:2	login	example	Examples	login -f helper
logout	logout	line		logout [-f] NAME\nlog NAME out until it is logged back in via login\nhelp groups: avatars\nkeywords: log out sign off stop avatar account session disconnect
logout:intro	logout	intro		`logout` puts one of the daemon's sessions down and keeps it down.\nThe argument is a profile name out of the `agents` listing, the same\nname `login` takes.  Flags come before the name.\n\n    logout builder\n\nThe keeping down is the part worth knowing.  The daemon brings a\nsession back when it drops, which is what it is for, and it must not\ndo that to an avatar somebody has taken away on purpose -- so a\nlogout is remembered.  Nothing will bring that avatar back on its\nown: it stays listed as stopped until `login` asks for it by name\nand with `-f`, since a deliberate stop is exactly what `login` will\nnot undo casually.\n\nThe session also gives up its place in the queue, so the avatar a\nbare command drives moves on to the next one up.  Logging it back in\nlater puts it at the end of that queue rather than back at the head.\n\nOnly a session the daemon is holding can be logged out.  A profile\nlisted as configured is one it has never been asked to bring up, so\nnaming it is answered with `no agent named ...` rather than with a\nshrug.  There is nothing there to put down; `login` is what that\nline is for.\n\nAsking for one that is already down is not an error: it reports the\nlogout as though it had just done it, because from the caller's side\nthe wanted state has been reached either way.
logout:example:1	logout	example		logout builder
logout:option:-f, --force	logout	option	-f, --force	Log out a session that has clients attached.  Without it a session\nin use is left alone.  It overrules only that refusal.
logout:section:It refuses while somebody is using it	logout	section	It refuses while somebody is using it	A session with clients attached is left alone and the refusal names\nthem: the programs that attached, under the names they authenticated\nwith, rather than a count of them.
logout:section:Nothing here is the way to end this shell	logout	section	Nothing here is the way to end this shell	A shell attached to the daemon leaves the avatar logged in when it\nquits; that is the arrangement, and logging out is a separate act\nagainst a named avatar.  The other way round, a shell that logged in\nfor itself holds the session in this process, so there is no daemon\nto ask and quitting is what logs the avatar out.  The line printed\nat startup says which of the two is in force.
logout:section:Examples	logout	section	Examples	    logout builder\n    logout -f helper\n\nSee also: `login`, `agents`, `quit`, and `viewer` for handing a\nsession to a real viewer instead of taking it down.
logout:example:2	logout	example	Examples	logout -f helper
look	look	line		look\nwhat the simulator said about the region\nhelp groups: region\nkeywords: region information describe owner water height rating access product estate
look:intro	look	intro		What the simulator said about the region when the avatar arrived: its\nname and key, who owns it, its access rating, the height of the water,\nand which product it runs as.  `where` is the same question asked about\nthe avatar rather than about the land, and `objects` is what is\nstanding on it.\n\n    look\n    Testville\n      id       d8467e57-...\n      owner    7e7c7e57-...\n      access   general\n      water    20.0m\n      product  Estate / Full Region\n      objects  195 described so far\n\nThe access rating is printed in the words a viewer shows -- general,\nmoderate, adult -- and as `access N` where the grid sends one nothing\nhere has a word for, which is the same rendering `regions` gives it.\nThe label and that rendering double up, so an unknown rating reads\n`access   access 21`.\n\nA region describes itself as the avatar arrives, and never again.  A\nshell attached to a daemon therefore gets the same answer as one that\nlogged in itself, and a shell that attached an hour late gets it too.\nA refusal here means the handshake has not arrived yet, which happens\nfor a second or two after a login and not otherwise.
look:example:1	look	example		look
look:example:2	look	example		objects  195 described so far
look:section:The count of objects is a count of what has been heard	look	section	The count of objects is a count of what has been heard	The last line is how many objects this session has been told about, and\nthat is not how many are in the region.\n\nThe simulator describes what is near the camera and nothing else, so an\nobject beyond the draw distance is not merely unnamed here, it is\nunknown.  The count grows as the avatar moves about, and it falls\nagain as things drift out of range -- about half a minute after they\ngo, since something out of range is kept that long in case the camera\nit was judged by was the moment's wrong one.  Read it as what the session is\nholding now rather than as a census.
look:section:Examples	look	section	Examples	    look\n\nSee also: `where`, `who`, `objects`, `regions` for the same question\nasked about somewhere else on the grid, `caps` and `features` for what\nthis simulator offers a client, and `lsl` for the language it\nimplements.
lookup	lookup	line		lookup [-l] TEXT\nsearch the grid for people by part of a name; -l for the keys\nhelp groups: people\nkeywords: search find person people avatar name username key resident
lookup:intro	lookup	intro		lookup searches the whole grid for people by part of a name.  `who`\nasks who is standing in this region and `friends` asks who is on the\nfriend list; this one reaches somebody who is nowhere near and has\nnever been mentioned here.\n\n    lookup example\n\nThe argument is whatever part of a name is remembered, joined with\nspaces.  A username typed with a dot -- `example.resident` -- is\nsearched as a name.  What comes back is a numbered listing in name\norder, with the display name in a second column where it differs from\nthe name.\n\nNo key is printed unless `-l` asks for one.  The search knows the key\nof everybody it found and hands it to whatever the number is typed at,\nso most of the time the key is machinery rather than an answer -- and\na hundred of them down the page would bury the names they belong to.
lookup:example:1	lookup	example		lookup example
lookup:option:-l	lookup	option	-l	The key as well as the name, between the name and the display name.\n\n    lookup -l example\n     1  Example Resident                  a4c67e57-...  Exemplary\n\nIt is the one handle that does not change.  A display name is today's,\na name is shared in part by everybody the search turned up, and every\ncommand here that takes a person takes a key instead -- so a key is\nwhat to write down, paste into something else, or put in a script that\ncannot type a number at a listing.\n\nThe columns before it are fixed width, so the key lands in the same\nplace on every row and can be cut out of a listing.  The display name\ncomes last because it is the one field with no width at all.\n\nThe listing is what the next command means by a number.  `lookup` then\n`profile 3` is the ordinary way round, and the same number works for\nanything else that takes a person.
lookup:example:2	lookup	example	Options	lookup -l example
lookup:section:A full page is not a complete answer	lookup	section	A full page is not a complete answer	The search answers with at most a hundred names and says nothing about\nhow many it left behind, so a page of a hundred would otherwise read\nexactly like the whole of the grid.  A listing that is a full page\ntherefore says so underneath.  Nothing here asks for the next page:\nsomebody who typed too little of a name wants to type more of it rather\nthan read a hundred more names.\n\nA listing short of a hundred is not a complete answer either.  The way\nto find a particular person is more of their name rather than more of\nthe listing.
lookup:section:Too little of a name is an error, not a short list	lookup	section	Too little of a name is an error, not a short list	One or two letters often come back as a failure from the search rather\nthan as names.  More of the name is the answer.\n\nWhere the grid grants this session its name search, part of a name\nmatches, and so does a display name.  Where it does not, only a whole\nname matches -- so a search that finds nothing on a name typed in part\nis worth trying again in full before concluding nobody is there.\n\nA name found here is not a person who is online, in this region, or\nreachable; it is a name the grid knows, and a key to go with it.
lookup:section:Examples	lookup	section	Examples	Find somebody by as much of the name as is remembered, then read what\ntheir profile says:\n\n    lookup example res\n    profile 1\n\nOr take the key straight out of the listing:\n\n    lookup -l example res\n\nSee also: `profile`, `im`, `offer`, `give` and the other commands that\ntake a person, which make this same search for themselves when nothing\nnearer knows the name -- `profile` taking what it resembles, the rest\nonly the whole name -- `who` for the people in this region, and\n`friends` for the friend list.
lookup:example:3	lookup	example	Examples	lookup example res
lookup:example:4	lookup	example	Examples	profile 1
lookup:example:5	lookup	example	Examples	lookup -l example res
ls	ls	line		ls [-Llrt] [--in OBJECT] [PATH]\nlist a folder, or what a path names; -l for detail, -t newest first\nhelp groups: inventory\nkeywords: list show folder contents inventory directory items what have browse
ls:intro	ls	intro		`ls` lists a folder.  With no path it lists the folder the shell is\nin; with one it lists that folder instead, relative to here unless\nthe path starts from the root.\n\n    ls -l Objects\n\nA path that is not a folder lists what it names.  A name can mean\nseveral things, and this is how to see them.\n\nBy default it prints one bare path per line.  A listing written to a\nfile is then a list of paths, which can be edited into commands and\nrun.
ls:example:1	ls	example		ls -l Objects
ls:option:-l	ls	option	-l	The columns: kind, when it was acquired, id and path.  Without it,\none bare path per line.
ls:option:-L	ls	option	-L	The same columns, with a link shown as the item it points at: its\nkind, its date and its id, under the path where the link was found.\nImplies `-l`.\n\nAn outfit folder holds nothing but links, every one named after the\nitem at the far end, so `-l` there gives a column of `link` and a\ncolumn of ids that name nothing outside this inventory.  `-L` turns\nthe same listing into what is actually worn:\n\n    ls -L "/Current Outfit"\n    object     2025-04-14T17:44:09 cfb57e57-...  /Current Outfit/a dress\n    bodypart   2025-04-14T18:01:16 28d37e57-...  /Current Outfit/a shape\n\nThat is also the way to see clothing and body parts as against\nattachments, which `worn` cannot show: `worn` lists objects on\nattachment points, and a skin or a shape is not one.\n\nA link that cannot be followed keeps the word `link`, which says what\nhappened -- this is a link and following it got nowhere.  The id\ncolumn is the id it points at either way, since the link carries that\nalready.\n\nFollowing costs one walk of inventory for the whole listing, and only\nwhere the listing holds a link at all.
ls:option:-r	ls	option	-r	Descend four levels rather than listing only this folder.  Deeper\nthan that is not listed and is not reported as missing.
ls:option:-t	ls	option	-t	Newest first, in a flat order rather than a tree one.  Grouping by\nfolder would bury a thing made a minute ago under whichever folder\nit lives in.  Things made in the same second sort by name.  A folder\nhas no date and sorts as the oldest thing there is, which puts\nfolders at the end.
ls:option:--in OBJECT	ls	option	--in OBJECT	List what a rezzed object holds instead of a folder.  Takes no path:\nan object holds no folders.  A line is the kind and the name, and\nwith `-l` the kind, the id and the name.  There is no date, and no\npath.
ls:example:2	ls	example	Options	ls -L "/Current Outfit"
ls:section:The columns	ls	section	The columns	With `-l` a line is kind, when it was acquired, id, path, in that\norder and padded to fixed widths.\n\n    notecard   2026-08-03T21:26:43 d8467e57-...  /Notecards/readme\n\nThe date is one field, down to the second, with a `T` rather than a\nspace, so a listing is four fields and the id stays the third.  Two\nitems of one name, acquired a minute apart, are told apart by this\ncolumn and by the id.  `rm --newest` and `--oldest` pick between\nthem by exactly this number, so a listing is how to see what one of\nthose is about to take.\n\nA folder has no date and gets a dash, so the columns after it do not\nmove.
ls:section:Listing an item	ls	section	Listing an item	    ls -l /Scripts/greeter\n    script     2026-08-07T12:06:57 cb567e57-...  /Scripts/greeter\n    script     2026-08-07T11:26:01 e9d97e57-...  /Scripts/greeter\n\nSeveral things of one name, one line each.  The dates are what\n`--newest` and `--oldest` choose by, and the ids are what names one\nof them exactly.  A uuid works as the path too, and prints the one\nline it names.\n\nWithout `-l` the same listing is the same path several times over,\nwhich is honest and says nothing about which is which.\n\nA folder wins where a folder and an item share a name, as `cd` does;\nthe id names the item.  A path that is neither is reported as the\nname it is rather than as the folder it was tried as first.\n\nInventory names are not unique.  A path names the first of them.\nThe id names exactly one, and `cat`, `rm`, `mv`, `drop` and the rest\ntake an id anywhere they take a path.  `get` looks like it belongs\non that list and does not: the uuid it takes is a texture's asset\nid, which is not what this column prints.
ls:example:3	ls	example	Listing an item	ls -l /Scripts/greeter
ls:section:Inside a rezzed object	ls	section	Inside a rezzed object	The ids in a `-l` listing `--in` an object belong to the object's\nown copies, not to the inventory items they came from.  `rm --in`,\n`mv --in`, `cat --in` and `fetch` take either: the name, matched\nexactly and in the case it has, or one of these ids, which is how one\nof two items of the same name is chosen.
ls:section:Examples	ls	section	Examples	    ls -l Objects\n    ls -l /Scripts/greeter\n    ls -lt d8467e57-...\n    ls -rt\n    ls --in lantern\n\nSee also: `cd`, `find`, `cat`, `get`, `rm`, `drop` for putting\nsomething into the object `--in` lists, and `fetch` for bringing\nsomething out of it.
ls:example:4	ls	example	Examples	ls -lt d8467e57-...
ls:example:5	ls	example	Examples	ls -rt
ls:example:6	ls	example	Examples	ls --in lantern
lsl	lsl	line		lsl [-acefmt] [TEXT]\nthe LSL this simulator implements: functions, constants, events, types\nhelp groups: simulator\nkeywords: lsl scripting language functions constants events reference syntax script
lsl:intro	lsl	intro		`lsl` is the language this simulator implements, from the simulator\nitself: every function with its arguments, return type, energy and\nsleep, every constant with its type and value, every event with its\narguments, and the type names.  It is what a viewer's own script\neditor colours and completes from -- slsh has no editor -- and it\ncomes from the machine that will run the script rather than from a\npage somebody edited.\n\n    lsl llSetLinkPrimitiveParams\n\nA function this does not list does not exist here.  A constant whose\nvalue differs from what a compiler believes is a bug in the\ncompiler.  When the grid gains something, the difference can be read\noff rather than guessed at.\n\nWith nothing said it prints one line: the version of the format and\nhow many of each kind there are.  A word after it searches every\nkind for names containing that word, without regard to case.  A\nfunction's line carries its energy and sleep where either is not\nzero, and says where it is deprecated or needs god mode.\n\nThe document the simulator sends also names the control keywords --\nif, else, for, jump and the rest -- and nothing prints them.
lsl:example:1	lsl	example		lsl llSetLinkPrimitiveParams
lsl:option:-f, --functions	lsl	option	-f, --functions	List the functions.
lsl:option:-c, --constants	lsl	option	-c, --constants	List the constants.
lsl:option:-e, --events	lsl	option	-e, --events	List the events.
lsl:option:-t, --types	lsl	option	-t, --types	List the type names.  It is described as the types and keywords,\nwhich is what was meant rather than what happens: it prints the type\nnames alone.
lsl:option:-a, --all	lsl	option	-a, --all	List everything.  Without a word, that is the listing rather than\nthe one-line summary.
lsl:option:-m, --machine-readable	lsl	option	-m, --machine-readable	One record a line, tab separated, for a program.  Without it the\nlisting is aligned for reading.  A bare `-m` with no kind flag is\nevery kind, not the summary.
lsl:section:The form meant for a program	lsl	section	The form meant for a program	One record a line, tab separated, no alignment and no totals.  The\nfirst field always says which kind of record it is, so one loop\nreads whichever kinds were asked for, and a reader meeting a kind it\ndoes not know can skip the line rather than misread it.\n\n    function  NAME  RETURN  ENERGY  SLEEP  FLAGS  ARGTYPE,...\n    constant  NAME  TYPE    VALUE\n    event     NAME  ARGTYPE,...\n    type      NAME\n\nA function that returns nothing says `void` rather than leaving the\nfield empty.  The flags are a comma-separated set that may be empty,\nand so is a list of argument types, so a function of no arguments\nends its line with an empty field.  Fields are never reordered and\nnever removed: anything new goes on the end, so a reader that splits\nand takes the first few keeps working.  Nothing is quoted or escaped\nbecause nothing in the source contains a tab -- which is also why\nthe tooltips are not in it, since a tooltip could contain anything.
lsl:section:What it costs to ask	lsl	section	What it costs to ask	The whole language is about half a megabyte, so it is fetched once\nand kept.  Asking again costs one small request for the id that says\nwhether it has changed, and, where it has not, nothing else.  Kept\nby the session, which means for as long as this shell is running and\nno longer: a fresh slsh pays for the document again.  A script that\nwants the language more than once is a script to run in one shell,\nwhich is what `.` is for.
lsl:section:Examples	lsl	section	Examples	    lsl llSetLinkPrimitiveParams\n    lsl -m -f > functions\n\nSee also: `features`, where that id comes from, and `caps` for\nwhether the capability behind all this was granted at all.
lsl:example:2	lsl	example	Examples	lsl -m -f > functions
lure	lure	line		lure [-a] WHO [TEXT]\noffer somebody a teleport here, or --ask to be brought to them\nhelp groups: people\nkeywords: offer teleport bring summon invite somebody here to me teleport request
lure:intro	lure	intro		`lure` offers somebody a teleport to where this avatar is standing.  It\nis what a viewer's menu calls Offer Teleport; the grid's own word for\nit is a lure, which is what `waiting` already says when one arrives\nhere.\n\n    lure Example Resident\n    lure Example Resident come and look at the dock\n\nWho is named by a name, by a key, or by the number beside a name in the\nlast listing of people, exactly as `im` and `offer` name somebody.\nAnything after the name is the note that goes with it.
lure:example:1	lure	example		lure Example Resident
lure:example:2	lure	example		lure Example Resident come and look at the dock
lure:section:What the other side sees	lure	section	What the other side sees	The destination is not a field of the message.  What arrives is the\nnote, and a viewer shows that as the whole of the offer -- so an offer\nwith no note arrives with nothing to say where it goes.  For that\nreason the shell appends this avatar's position to the note as a map\nlink, which is what a viewer does with its own offers:\n\n    come and look at the dock\n    https://maps.secondlife.com/secondlife/Example%20Region/128/128/25
lure:option:-a, --ask	lure	option	-a, --ask	Ask to be brought to *them* instead of offering to bring them here.\nThat sends a teleport request, which is an ordinary instant message\ncarrying dialog 26 rather than a message of its own, and is answered by\nan offer coming back -- if they answer at all.\n\n    lure --ask Example Resident could you bring me over
lure:example:3	lure	example	Options	lure --ask Example Resident could you bring me over
lure:section:Nothing comes back	lure	section	Nothing comes back	An offer is sent and that is the end of what can be known here.  Being\naccepted arrives as its own instant message and being ignored arrives\nas nothing at all, so `lure` reports what was sent rather than what\nhappened.  Whether they came is a question for `who`.
lure:section:When somebody asks this avatar	lure	section	When somebody asks this avatar	A request arriving here is listed by `waiting` as a teleport request,\nand `answer N` sends them an offer -- which is the only thing that\nanswers it, since a request carries no id of its own.  `no N` merely\ndrops it from the listing: the grid has no message for refusing one,\na viewer's own No button sends nothing, and the person who asked is\nnever told either way.\n\nBefore this existed such a request arrived as `* dialog 26 from\nSomebody`, which named neither what it was nor what to do about it.\n\nSee also: `tp`, `waiting`, `answer`, `offer`, `im`.
man	man	line		man [NAME]\nthe long description of one command; no name lists the ones that have one\nhelp groups: shell\nkeywords: manual documentation page long description explain command details
man:intro	man	intro		`man` prints the long description of a command.\n\n    man place\n\nIt displays the name, a one-line brief and the usage line, then the page:\nwhat the command does, what the arguments mean, and the traps that\n`--help` has no room for.  A command with flags lists them on its page\ntoo, under Options, a paragraph each rather than the column\n`place --help` prints.  A command whose only flag is `--help` -- this\none -- has no such section, and then the flags are only the column.\n\nThe only argument is a command name.  One at a time.
man:example:1	man	example		man place
man:section:The listing	man	section	The listing	With no name, `man` lists every command that has a page.  Aliases are\nlisted on their own -- `quit` and `exit`, `source` and `.`, `stand` and\n`unsit` -- even though each pair shares a page.  `help` groups commands\nby topic and shows one name each; this list is every name you can type.
man:section:Missing pages, missing names	man	section	Missing pages, missing names	If a command has no page yet, `man` still prints the brief and usage,\nand says the page is unwritten.  If the name is not a command at all,\nthat is an error -- `help all` is the full set.
man:section:Width	man	section	Width	The page wraps to this terminal.  Redirected to a file, it still wraps\nto the window you ran it in, and the escape sequences a terminal uses\nfor bold are left out -- they are not text.  Unicode (bullets, rules)\nstays.
man:section:Paging	man	section	Paging	On a terminal, a page longer than the screen is shown a screenful at a\ntime.  Redirected to a file, or short enough to fit, the whole page is\nprinted and nothing waits.\n\n| key | action |\n| --- | --- |\n| space, `f` | forward one screen |\n| Enter | forward one line |\n| `b` | back one screen |\n| `d` | forward half a screen |\n| `u` | back half a screen |\n| `p` | the top of the page |\n| `/text` | the next line containing text |\n| `/` | the next match of the last search |\n| `?text` | the previous line containing text |\n| `?` | the previous match of the last search |\n| `n` | the next match in the same direction as `/` or `?` |\n| `q` | leave |\n\nAt the end of the page, space and Enter leave as well.  Type the\nsearch, then Enter.\n\nNothing is erased and the screen is never cleared.  Each screenful is\nprinted under the last, so whatever was on the terminal before the page\nbegan is still above it, and the page itself is still in the scrollback\nafter the pager has gone.  A key that moves backwards therefore prints\nthat part of the page again, underneath, rather than carrying the\ndisplay back up to it.
man:section:Examples	man	section	Examples	    man place\n    man\n    man wear > wear.txt\n\nSee also: `help`, and `COMMAND --help` for the same flags in a\ncolumn, without the paragraphs.
man:example:2	man	example	Examples	man
man:example:3	man	example	Examples	man wear > wear.txt
map	map	line		map [-r] [--rows N] [--span METRES]\na picture of who is around this avatar, or of the whole region\nhelp groups: region\nkeywords: picture radar minimap avatars around nearby positions direction draw
map:intro	map	intro		Draws the other avatars as characters on a grid.  It is the half of the\nquestion `who` cannot answer: a listing says somebody is twenty metres\naway and nothing at all about which way, and three people at twenty\nmetres are a crowd in one direction and a scattering in another.\n\nThere are two pictures.  By default it is the ground around this\navatar, sixty-four metres across, with the avatar in the middle of it.\nThe other is the whole region -- 256 metres -- with the avatar\nwherever in it the avatar really is.\n\n    map
map:example:1	map	example		map
map:option:-r, --region	map	option	-r, --region	Draw the second of those two pictures rather than the first.
map:option:--rows N	map	option	--rows N	How many rows to draw, for this picture only, from 2 to 64.  Without\nit, `map_rows`, which is sixteen.
map:option:--span METRES	map	option	--span METRES	How much ground the close picture covers, in metres across.  Without\nit, `map_span`.  Less than 2 is refused, being narrower than the avatar\nin the middle of it.  `--region` has no span to change and says so rather than\nignoring one.
map:section:The picture is wider than it is tall, which is what makes it look square	map	section	The picture is wider than it is tall, which is what makes it look square	A character cell is taller than it is wide, so a grid drawn with as\nmany columns as rows comes out on the screen taller than it is wide,\nand a square region looks like a doorway.\n\nThe rows are what is chosen and the columns follow: as many as it takes\nfor a square of ground to look square.  How much taller than wide a\ncell is belongs to the font, so it is a setting -- `map_ratio` --\nwritten height first.  The default is 7:3.  `set map_ratio 2:1` is\ntwice as tall as wide.  `set map_ratio auto` asks the terminal for the\ncell size and writes down what came back, which is where to start\nrather than the answer: what a terminal reports is the box it hands the\nfont and not the shape the letters look.  `man set` has the rest of it.\n\nA picture is sixteen rows unless something says otherwise, and at 7:3\nsixteen rows is 37 columns.  Those are the numbers that turn a mark on\nthe picture back into metres: in the region view a row is 256/16 = 16\nmetres and a column 256/37, a shade under 7; in the close view a row is\n4 metres and a column about 1.7.\n\nThe line under the grid says how much ground the picture covers --\n`64m x 64m` -- because that is the number somebody reads a map for.\nMore rows buy detail rather than reach, so drawing it taller does not\nmake it cover more ground.
map:section:The marks	map	section	The marks	    *   this avatar\n    o   somebody within three metres of this avatar's height\n    ^   somebody higher than that\n    v   somebody lower than that\n\nThree metres is about one storey: an `o` is somebody who could be\nwalked over to and a `^` is somebody on the roof.  It is the\n`map_level` setting, and whatever it is set to is the number in the\nline under the grid.  The height is compared with this avatar's in both\nviews, so a picture of the whole region still says who is up in a\nskybox.\n\nA friend's mark is drawn in colour.  Two or more people in one cell are\ndrawn as the count -- 2 to 9 -- and ten or more as a `+`.  Somebody\nstanding in the same cell as this avatar is covered by the star, which\nwins its own cell: a picture whose reader cannot find themselves in it\nis a picture of nowhere.  The line under the grid says how many are\nunder the star.\n\nA picture with no star in it says so.  Where there is nobody else at\nall there is no count either, only "nobody else is in range", which is\nthe wording `who` uses for the same state.
map:section:A friend is picked out in colour	map	section	A friend is picked out in colour	Green unless `map_friend_colour` says otherwise.  The colours are named\nrather than spelled as escape sequences -- black, red, green, yellow,\nblue, magenta, cyan, white, and "bright" before any of them.  The line\nunder the grid says the colour's own name, in that colour, so the\npicture always names the word to type to change it.\n\nA cell with several people in it is drawn as a count, and the count is\ncoloured when any one of them is a friend.  Somebody outside the\npicture is coloured in the line that names them underneath.  The star\nis never coloured.\n\nThe colour only ever reaches a terminal.  A picture written to a file\n-- `map > listing` -- and a picture from `slsh -c` or `slsh -f` or a\npiped session have no escape sequences in them at all.  `NO_COLOR` is\nhonoured: set it to anything at all, `0` included, and nothing is\ncoloured.  When there is no colour there is no line explaining one\neither.
map:section:North is up and east is right	map	section	North is up and east is right	Which is what the region's own coordinates already mean: y grows\nnorthward and x eastward, and the line under the grid says so as well.\n\nAnybody the picture does not reach is counted and named underneath it,\nwith how far away they are, rather than being drawn at the edge.  An\navatar drawn at the edge is a claim about where they are standing, and\nthat claim would be false.  The names stop at eight and the rest are a\ncount, because naming forty people under a grid is `who` printed twice.
map:section:Scale	map	section	Scale	Nothing chooses the region view's scale -- a region is 256 metres --\nbut the close view has to cover something, and 64 metres is a choice:\ncomfortably more than the twenty metres ordinary chat carries, and\nrather less than the draw distance usually is.
map:section:What can be set	map	section	What can be set	`map_rows` is how many rows a picture is drawn in.  `map_span` is how\nmuch ground the close picture covers, in metres.  `map_ratio` is the\nshape of a character cell, height first.  `map_level` is how far above\nor below still counts as level.  `map_friend_colour` is the colour a\nfriend is drawn in.\n\n`set` lists them with the values they have now, and `set NAME VALUE`\nchanges one and writes it to the settings file, so that the next\npicture and every picture after it is drawn that way.
map:section:It numbers nobody	map	section	It numbers nobody	Every listing that prints a number down the left -- `who`, `friends`,\n`lookup` -- replaces what a number means for the commands that take\none.  A picture cannot be numbered usefully, since two people in one\ncell are one character, so this deliberately leaves the last listing\nalone: after a map, `im 2` still means the second line of whatever\nprinted one.
map:section:What it cannot show	map	section	What it cannot show	The avatars are the ones the simulator has described to this session,\nwhich is what is within the draw distance and not everyone in the\nregion.  A picture of the whole region can only mark the people this\nsession has been told about: an empty corner is a corner nobody has\ndescribed, not a corner with nobody in it.  The count under the grid,\nand `where`, are what to believe; the blank cells are not.
map:section:Examples	map	section	Examples	    map\n    map --region\n    map --rows 24\n    map --span 24\n\nSee also: `set` for the settings above and everything else slsh keeps,\n`who` for the same people as a listing with their distances and the\nnumbers other commands take, `where` for this avatar's own position,\n`look` for what the region says about itself, `parcel --map` for the\nland rather than the people, and `tp` for going to a position rather\nthan reading one.
map:example:2	map	example	Examples	map --region
map:example:3	map	example	Examples	map --rows 24
map:example:4	map	example	Examples	map --span 24
maturity	maturity	line		maturity [RATING]\nwhat land this avatar is shown and may be; with a RATING, ask for that one and print what was granted\nhelp groups: avatars region\nkeywords: rating adult moderate general mature pg content access preference region refused
maturity:intro	maturity	intro		Says what rating of land this avatar is shown and how high the\naccount may go.  Given a rating -- general, moderate or adult -- it\nasks for that one instead, and prints what the grid granted, which is\nnot always what was asked for.\n\n    maturity\n    maturity adult\n    maturity moderate\n    maturity general\n\nThe letters the protocol uses are taken as well, so `maturity a` and\n`maturity pg` mean the same as the words.  "Mature" is read as\nmoderate, which is what that rating used to be called.
maturity:example:1	maturity	example		maturity
maturity:example:2	maturity	example		maturity adult
maturity:example:3	maturity	example		maturity moderate
maturity:example:4	maturity	example		maturity general
maturity:section:Two numbers wear this name	maturity	section	Two numbers wear this name	An account carries both of these, and only the first is anybody's to\nset:\n\n- **The preference.**  The highest rating this avatar has asked to be\n  shown.  That is what this command sets, and it is the same setting a\n  viewer's maturity menu changes.\n- **The ceiling.**  The highest the account is *permitted* to ask for.\n  It comes from age verification, it is `agent_access_max` in the login\n  response, and no client can raise it -- there is no message for it,\n  and Linden Lab's own viewer offers only what the account already\n  allows and sends you to the website for the rest.\n\nA bare `maturity` prints both; see "Reading it" below.\n\n`maturity RATING` asks, and the grid answers with what it granted.\nWhere they agree there is nothing more to say, and this is what was\nmeasured on Agni on 2026-09-02:\n\n    /$ maturity adult\n    this avatar is shown land rated adult and below\n\nWhere they differ, the answer is the ceiling, and the line says so.\nThat case has NOT been seen here -- both accounts this was run against\nwere granted what they asked for -- so what follows is what the\ncommand would print and not a transcript:\n\n    /$ maturity adult\n    asked for adult and was granted moderate\n    moderate is as high as this account may go, which is age verification\n    rather than a setting: no client can raise it, and it is changed on the\n    account page and nowhere else\n\nBeing granted what was asked for does not mean anything changed:\nasking for a preference already in force is answered the same way.
maturity:section:Why you would want it	maturity	section	Why you would want it	An avatar refused entry to a region is told this, on the teleport:\n\n    RegionTPAccessBlocked: "You aren't allowed in that Region due to your\n    maturity Rating. You may need to validate your age and/or install the\n    latest Viewer. Please go to the Knowledge Base for details on accessing\n    areas with this maturity Rating."\n\nThat sentence names both causes at once and picks neither, so there is\nno telling from it whether the preference is too low or the account is\nnot verified.  This command tells them apart in one call, either\nway round: a bare `maturity` prints the two numbers without changing\nanything, and asking for adult makes the grid answer with which of the\ntwo you are looking at -- and fixes the preference, if it can be.\n\nMeasured on Agni on 2026-09-02, in that order and on one avatar:\n\n    /$ tp Nightmire Sea 128 128 25\n    teleporting to Nightmire Sea at 128, 128, 25\n    slsh: tp: sl: the grid refused the teleport: ... RegionTPAccessBlocked:\n    "You aren't allowed in that Region due to your maturity Rating. ..."\n    /$ maturity adult\n    this avatar is shown land rated adult and below\n    /$ tp Nightmire Sea 128 128 25\n    teleporting to Nightmire Sea at 128, 128, 25\n    Nightmire Sea at 128, 128, 26\n\nSo the block was the preference and not the account, and one command\ncleared it.  A second avatar on the same grid walked into that region\nthroughout, which is what made the difference visible in the first\nplace.\n\nA headless login starts with whatever the account already had, and\nthese avatars are usually set up through a viewer once and never again\n-- so a preference somebody set years ago on one account and never on\nanother is the ordinary reason two avatars behave differently on the\nsame land.
maturity:example:5	maturity	example	Why you would want it	maturity Rating. You may need to validate your age and/or install the
maturity:section:Reading it	maturity	section	Reading it	A bare `maturity` asks the grid nothing.  Both numbers arrive in the\nlogin response -- the preference as `agent_region_access`, the ceiling\nas `agent_access_max`, which is how Linden Lab's viewer reads them --\nand the daemon keeps that response and hands the two on with the\navatar's presence, which is where this reads them.\nFor an account shown moderate that may go to adult it prints\n\n    this avatar is shown land rated moderate and below, and this account may go as high as adult\n\nand for one already at its ceiling\n\n    this avatar is shown land rated adult and below, which is as high as this account may go\n\nThose are the lines the command prints and not a transcript: this\nform needs a daemon built with it, and had not been run against the\ngrid when this was written.\n\nA number the login did not carry is said to be missing rather than\nguessed at, and so are both when the daemon is older than this form\nand passes neither on.  Missing is never printed as general.\n\nThe field called `agent_access` is not the preference, though its name\nmakes it look like the one.  It is older than the adult rating, and a\nlogin response from an account shown adult still carries M there.\n\nThe capability `maturity RATING` uses could not have been the reading\nform.  Its only question is "set it to this", and the only rating\nalways safe to ask for is general -- which would quietly *lower* a\npreference somebody had set higher.\n\n### What it does not see\n\nWhat a bare `maturity` prints is the login's, followed by whatever\n`maturity RATING` has been granted since through the same daemon.  A\nchange made anywhere else is not seen until the next login: on the\naccount's web page, or in a viewer attached to the session, whose\nrequest goes to the simulator directly rather than through the daemon.\nNo message a simulator sends to a client carries the preference, and\nthe viewer's own source learns it from its login and from the answer to\nits own request and nowhere else -- which is read from that source\nrather than watched, but it leaves nothing for the daemon to hear.
maturity:section:The rating is per account, not per session	maturity	section	The rating is per account, not per session	It is remembered by the grid and outlives the session, so setting it\nonce is setting it for good -- an avatar logged out and back in is\nstill shown what it was last granted.  It is not part of the profile\nthis shell keeps and it is not affected by `logout`, `login`, or the\ndaemon being restarted.\n\nSee also: `regions` and `look`, which print the maturity rating of land\nrather than of an avatar, and `tp` for the refusal that sends most\npeople here.
mkdir	mkdir	line		mkdir PATH\nmake a folder\nhelp groups: inventory\nkeywords: create make new folder directory
mkdir:intro	mkdir	intro		`mkdir` makes one folder.  `rm` is what takes one away, and takes\neverything inside it at the same time.\n\n    mkdir lanterns\n\nThe argument is a path.  The last name in it is the folder that gets\nmade, and everything before it has to be there already: nothing here\nmakes a chain of folders, so a path through a folder that does not\nexist is refused rather than filled in.  A path with no slashes in\nit makes the folder here, in the folder the shell is in.\n\nWhat it prints is the new folder's id.
mkdir:example:1	mkdir	example		mkdir lanterns
mkdir:section:Nothing stops two folders sharing a name	mkdir	section	Nothing stops two folders sharing a name	Asking twice makes two folders with one name.  A path then means\nthe first of them, and the second can only be named by its id, which\nis what `ls -l` prints ids for.  Where that has happened by\naccident, `mv` renames one of them and the ambiguity is gone.
mkdir:section:What a name may contain	mkdir	section	What a name may contain	Nearly any printable character.  A slash inside a name is written\nwith a backslash in front of it, and a backslash is doubled.  The\ngrid trims a leading or trailing space, so a name cannot begin or\nend with one however carefully it is quoted.
mkdir:section:Examples	mkdir	section	Examples	    mkdir lanterns\n    mkdir /Objects/lanterns/brass\n\nSee also: `cd`, `ls`, `mv`, and `rm`.
mkdir:example:2	mkdir	example	Examples	mkdir /Objects/lanterns/brass
move	move	line		move NAME X Y Z\nmove a rezzed object to a position\nhelp groups: region\nkeywords: position reposition shift object coordinates xyz relocate rezzed object
move:intro	move	intro		`move` shifts an object that is already standing in the region to a\nposition.  It changes nothing else: the object keeps the rotation and\nthe scale it had.\n\n    move probe 128 128 25\n\nThe object is named by the word the region calls it, and a name with a\nspace in it has to be quoted.  A name that several objects answer to is\nrefused rather than guessed at, since moving the wrong one is a thing\nsomebody then has to find.  The position is three numbers -- X Y Z,\nseparated by spaces, in this region's metres.\n\nA key will not do in place of the name.  `take`, `touch`, `dump` and\nthe rest accept a uuid where a name would go; `move` looks its argument\nup as a name and as nothing else.  A uuid typed there is refused with\n`no object named` and the uuid back again, which reads like the thing\nhas gone rather than like the form is wrong.  The way past a name\nseveral objects answer to is the one the refusal itself names -- rename\none of them -- and not the key every neighbouring command would have\ntaken.\n\nNothing answers a move: the region reports the new position when it\ngets round to it, so this asks again until the object is where it was\nsent rather than reporting the request and stopping.  A position read\nback by hand without that wait is the one the object had before,\nprinted with total confidence.  A move that has gone nowhere after twenty seconds\nis reported as that, and the likeliest reason is the one the message\nnames: a parcel that will not have objects moved refuses in silence.
move:example:1	move	example		move probe 128 128 25
move:section:This is not `place`	move	section	This is not `place`	`place` puts a thing into the world out of inventory.  `move` shifts\none that is already there.  They were the other way round until\nrecently -- `place` meant this command -- so a script written before\nthe change may say `place NAME X Y Z`, and that line is now refused\nrather than obeyed.
move:section:Putting something back where it was	move	section	Putting something back where it was	Second Life does not remember where an object stood before it was\ntaken, so a take-and-place round trip lands the object beside the\navatar and not where it started.  Writing the position down first and\nmoving it back afterwards is the whole of the fix:\n\n    objects probe\n    take probe\n    place Objects/probe\n    move probe 33 73 1000
move:example:2	move	example	Putting something back where it was	objects probe
move:example:3	move	example	Putting something back where it was	take probe
move:example:4	move	example	Putting something back where it was	place Objects/probe
move:example:5	move	example	Putting something back where it was	move probe 33 73 1000
move:section:Examples	move	section	Examples	    move probe 128 128 25\n    move "big sign" 33.5 73 1000.2\n\nSee also: `place`, `take`, `objects` for what is in the region and\nwhere, and `tp` for moving the avatar rather than an object.
move:example:6	move	example	Examples	move "big sign" 33.5 73 1000.2
