mv	mv	line		mv [--in OBJECT] PATH DEST\nmove into a folder, or rename if DEST is a plain name; folders too\nhelp groups: inventory\nkeywords: move rename folder item organise organize relocate inventory
mv:intro	mv	intro		`mv` moves something into another folder, or renames it where it is.\n\n    mv probe Objects/lanterns\n\nThe first argument is what to move -- a path relative to the folder\nthe shell is in, or a uuid, an item or a folder alike.  The second\nis where it goes.
mv:example:1	mv	example		mv probe Objects/lanterns
mv:option:--in OBJECT	mv	option	--in OBJECT	Rename something inside a rezzed object rather than in inventory.\nOnly a rename: an object holds no folders, so there is nowhere in\none to move anything to.  The object is named by the word the region\ncalls it or by its uuid, and a name several objects answer to is\nrefused with their uuids rather than guessed at.  The thing inside\nis named by its name, matched exactly and in the case it has.  The\nid beside a line of `ls -l --in` belongs to the object's own copy,\nand is not something to give this.
mv:section:What decides between moving and renaming	mv	section	What decides between moving and renaming	The destination.  If it names a folder, this is a move and the name\nis kept.  If it is a plain name that is no folder, this is a rename\nand the place is kept.  If it is neither -- a path through folders\nthat do not exist -- it is refused, since a rename cannot be to a\npath.\n\nThe trap is in the first of those.  A destination is tried as a\nfolder first, so renaming something to a word that happens to be a\nfolder here moves it into that folder instead, quietly and\nsuccessfully.  `mv README Notecards` beside a folder called\nNotecards does not make a notecard called Notecards; it files the\none there already.\n\nMoving and renaming at once is two commands, in either order.\n\nA folder can hold several things of one name, and a path means the\nfirst of them.  Where that is not the one wanted, `ls -l` prints the\nid beside each and an id may be given wherever a path is.
mv:section:Examples	mv	section	Examples	    mv probe Objects/lanterns\n    mv probe "probe, the second"\n    mv --in lantern hello.lsl greeter
mv:example:2	mv	example	Examples	mv probe "probe, the second"
mv:example:3	mv	example	Examples	mv --in lantern hello.lsl greeter
mv:section:A link is not followed	mv	section	A link is not followed	A link is an inventory entry in its own right, and `mv` acts on the\nentry.  Renaming or moving a link renames or moves the link.  An item\nmay have links to it from several outfits, and following one here\nwould reach past all of them to the item they share.\n\nSee also: `cp`, `mkdir`, `rm`, `ls`, and `drop` for putting an\ninventory item inside an object in the first place.
neighbours	neighbours	line		neighbours [on|off]\nthe circuits held to the regions around this one, which walking over a border needs\nhelp groups: region\nkeywords: neighbors border crossing adjacent regions circuits walk cross region edge
neighbours:intro	neighbours	intro		Says whether this avatar holds a circuit to the regions beside the one\nit is standing in, and lists what it holds.  With `on` or `off` it\nchanges that first and then says the state it ended up in.\n\nThe circuits are what a border crossing needs.  A simulator will not\nhand an avatar over the edge of a region to a client that holds no\ncircuit to the region on the other side -- it does not refuse, it\nsimply does not do it -- so with neighbours off the avatar walks to the\nborder and stops there.  With a circuit open, the same walk is over the\nborder in a couple of seconds.
neighbours:section:Why it is off unless somebody asks	neighbours	section	Why it is off unless somebody asks	Each neighbour costs a socket and a share of the traffic, for as long\nas it is held, and the number of them is however many regions surround\nthis one -- up to eight on the open grid.  That is a price worth paying\nfor an avatar somebody is walking about with, and a waste for one\nrunning a benchmark in a single region, which is why this is a property\nof an avatar and not of the daemon holding it.\n\nOff drops what is held rather than merely refusing the next offer.\nNothing is listed after it: one line says they are off.\n\nA word that is neither `on` nor `off` is a usage error, and the session\nis left as it was.
neighbours:section:On, with nothing held, is the ordinary state	neighbours	section	On, with nothing held, is the ordinary state	Turning them on asks for nothing and opens nothing.  The simulator\noffers a neighbour of its own accord and repeats the offer for as long\nas it goes untaken, so the circuits arrive by themselves; the way to\nsee them is to run `neighbours` again.\n\nThey take their time, and they do not arrive together.  Measured on\nAgni on 2026-08-23, timed from turning them on: the first circuit\nopened between twenty and sixty seconds later, and more followed over\nthe minute after that -- at one position three opened together and two\nmore forty seconds behind them.  An empty listing in the first few\nseconds is the offer not having come round yet.\n\nBeing away from a border is not what empties it.  Standing in the\nexact middle of a 256-metre region, 128 metres from all four edges,\nfour circuits were held: one across each edge, and none across a\ncorner.  Twenty-eight metres in from the west edge and seventy-two\nfrom the south, the near three came first -- those two edges and the\ncorner between them -- and the far two followed.  Height does not\ndecide it: that second position was 2001 metres up, and the middle was\nmeasured both at 2001 and at ground level.\n\nThe set changes as the avatar moves.  The circuits belong to the\nregion the avatar is in, so a teleport to another region or a crossing\nthrows them away; a teleport within the region leaves them alone.
neighbours:section:The listing	neighbours	section	The listing	One region to a line, in the shape `regions` prints: what the region\ncalls itself, the square it occupies on the grid, the address of the\nsimulator, and how many packets have arrived on the circuit.\n\n    neighbours are on, 2 circuits held\n    Example Landing                   43647, 43648   203.0.113.9:13009  412 heard\n    Example Shallows                  43648, 43647   203.0.113.28:13006 87 heard\n\nThe handle is not in the line, where `regions` prints it, because\nnothing is addressed by a neighbour's handle: a border is walked over\nrather than typed at.\n\nA name arrives in the region's handshake, so a line that says\n`(no handshake yet)` is a circuit that has not been sent one.  It does\nnot follow that the offer came to nothing.  On 2026-08-23, three of\nthe five simulators around one region sent no handshake through four\nopenings each, while the packet count beside them climbed into double\nfigures -- and the same three had answered on an earlier day, so it is\nthe simulator's mood rather than the circuit's health.  The count is\nwhat says a circuit is alive; the name only says the simulator\nintroduced itself.\n\nThe count is what the neighbour has said, counted and dropped.  Nothing\nthis shell can ask about lives in another region: what is over the\nborder is not in `objects`, not in `who` and not in the object cache.\nThe count is there to say the circuit is alive.
neighbours:example:1	neighbours	example	The listing	neighbours are on, 2 circuits held
neighbours:section:It belongs to the session, not to this shell	neighbours	section	It belongs to the session, not to this shell	Turning neighbours on turns them on for the avatar, in whatever is\nholding the session, and it stays on after this shell exits.  Another\nclient attached to the same daemon sees the same answer and can change\nit too.  Quitting does not put it back.\n\nA profile can start an avatar with them on, which is the right place\nfor one that is always driven by a person:\n\n    neighbours = yes\n\nin the profile under ~/.config/slgo.  The profile spells it `yes`; this\ncommand spells it `on`.  slgod's `-neighbours` flag is what profiles\nthat say nothing get, and a profile that says either wins over the\nflag.
neighbours:example:2	neighbours	example	It belongs to the session, not to this shell	neighbours = yes
neighbours:section:Examples	neighbours	section	Examples	    neighbours\n    neighbours on\n    neighbours off\n\nSee also: `tp` for crossing the grid rather than a border, `regions`\nfor finding a region by name and for what its handle is, `look` for\nwhat the region underfoot says about itself, and `where` for the\nposition inside it.
neighbours:example:3	neighbours	example	Examples	neighbours
neighbours:example:4	neighbours	example	Examples	neighbours on
neighbours:example:5	neighbours	example	Examples	neighbours off
new	new	line		new [--from FILE] [--in OBJECT] [--kind KIND] [-w SECONDS] PATH\nmake a notecard or a script; --in puts a script in an object and starts it\nhelp groups: inventory\nkeywords: create make new notecard script blank empty item write
new:intro	new	intro		`new` makes a notecard or a script that did not exist.  `save` writes\ninto one that already does.\n\n    new Notecards/README\n\nThe argument is where it lands: a path relative to the folder the\nshell is in, whose last name is the item and whose folders must\nalready be there.  What is printed is the name and the new item's\nid.\n\nBoth kinds cost nothing.  Uploading a texture costs L$; this does\nnot.\n\nA script made with no source is given a skeleton -- an empty default\nstate -- because a script with none at all is a compile error the\nmoment anything runs it.  A script that did not compile says so and\nis in inventory all the same.
new:example:1	new	example		new Notecards/README
new:option:--kind KIND	new	option	--kind KIND	`notecard` or `script`.  Without it, a notecard.  With `--in` and\nno `--kind`, a script, because a notecard cannot be made inside an\nobject.
new:option:--from FILE	new	option	--from FILE	Its contents.  Without this a notecard is empty and a script is\ngiven a skeleton.
new:option:--in OBJECT	new	option	--in OBJECT	Make the script inside a rezzed object, where it is compiled and\nstarted.  Takes a name and not a path, because an object holds no\nfolders.
new:option:-w, --wait SECONDS	new	option	-w, --wait SECONDS	How long to let the region describe the object `--in` names.\nWithout it, thirty seconds.
new:section:Inside a rezzed object	new	section	Inside a rezzed object	Dropping a script into an object does not compile it: the copy sits\nthere uncompiled, and `start` cannot start what was never compiled.\n\nReusing a name replaces that script rather than adding another.  An\nobject otherwise keeps every copy it is given and renames the\nnewcomer, so a command that added would leave two scripts of nearly\none name both running.\n\nA notecard cannot be made inside an object at all.  Asking for one\nwith `--in` is refused rather than quietly made in inventory\ninstead.  A notecard is made with a plain `new` and put in with\n`drop`.
new:section:Examples	new	section	Examples	    new Notecards/README\n    new --from notes.txt Notecards/README\n    new --kind script --from hello.lsl /Scripts/greeter\n    new --in lantern --from hello.lsl greeter\n\nSee also: `save`, `cat`, `drop`, `start`, `stop`, and `put` for an\nimage.
new:example:2	new	example	Examples	new --from notes.txt Notecards/README
new:example:3	new	example	Examples	new --kind script --from hello.lsl /Scripts/greeter
new:example:4	new	example	Examples	new --in lantern --from hello.lsl greeter
no	no	line		no N\ndecline one, and tell whoever asked\nhelp groups: waiting\nkeywords: decline refuse reject deny turn down offered teleport request permission dialog invitation waiting
no:intro	no	intro		`no` declines one of the things `waiting` lists, by its number, and\ntells whoever asked.\n\nThat last part is the difference between `no` and `ignore`.  A declined\nteleport, item, friendship, permission or group invitation sends a\nrefusal, so the person or script on the other end stops\nwaiting.  `ignore` says nothing to anybody.\n\n    no 2\n\nA dialog is the exception, and it says so when it happens.  There is\nno way to refuse a blue menu -- it can be answered or left -- so `no`\non a dialog or a text box drops it here and lets it expire where it\nwas raised.\n\nA teleport request is the other exception.  Nothing can be sent to\nrefuse one -- a viewer's No button sends nothing either, and the person\nwho asked is never told -- so `no` tells slgod instead, and the request\nleaves the listing of every other client on the avatar as well as this\none.\n\nDeclining something that was being ignored also stops it being\nignored, since it is dealt with now either way.\n\nSee also: `waiting`, `answer`, `ignore`.
no:example:1	no	example		no 2
objects	objects	line		objects [-c] [--owner WHO] [TEXT]\nthe objects the region has described; -c for the prims inside each\nhelp groups: region\nkeywords: list objects prims nearby around region scan search rezzed things owner who owns
objects:intro	objects	intro		`objects` lists what the region has described so far: one line per\nobject, its key, its name and where it is, gathered under whoever owns\nit.  It is the listing to look at when `take`, `move`, `touch` and the\nrest say they cannot find a thing.\n\n    objects lantern\n\nWith no argument it is everything in range.  A word matches any part of\na name, without regard to case.  Quote a name that has a space in it:\nonly the first word is searched otherwise.\n\nThe name printed is the one `take` and `move` want typed back, in\nfull.  A search for `lantern` will find `brass lantern`; `take\nlantern` will not.
objects:example:1	objects	example		objects lantern
objects:option:-c, --children	objects	option	-c, --children	The prims of each object as well, indented under it.  Without it, the\nlisting is the roots.
objects:option:--owner WHO	objects	option	--owner WHO	Only one owner's things: a uuid, or a pattern for the name.  Somebody\nnobody has named cannot be matched by a pattern about names, so those\nare counted at the foot instead of being quietly dropped.
objects:section:Roots, and the prims inside them	objects	section	Roots, and the prims inside them	What a person means by an object is the root of a linkset, so the roots\nare the listing.  Printing every prim would turn a hundred things into\na thousand lines, most of them called `Object` and placed at an offset\nfrom something the listing did not name.\n\nA search is treated differently from browsing.  A root matches on its\nchildren's names as well as its own, because the name somebody remembers\nis often on a prim inside; and a prim whose own name was asked for is\nprinted even when the listing is only roots.  The foot of the listing\nsays how many prims were held back.\n\nA prim whose root nothing has described is listed on its own, saying\nso, rather than dropped.
objects:section:What has been described, and what has not	objects	section	What has been described, and what has not	The simulator describes what is near the camera and nothing else.  An\nobject beyond the draw distance is not unnamed here, it is unknown, and\na region with nothing of that name in it reads exactly the same as one\nwhere the thing is too far away.\n\nThe first run is slow: names are asked for one by one.  The answers are\nkept, so the next run is quick.
objects:section:Whose things these are	objects	section	Whose things these are	The owner heads each group.  Attachments are in the listing too.  Their\nlast column says which point they are worn on, and names the wearer\nwhen it is not the owner.  Avatars themselves are not listed: `who` is\nthe listing for people.
objects:section:Examples	objects	section	Examples	    objects lantern\n    objects -c "garden chair"\n    objects --owner "^Example"\n\nSee also: `who`, `look`, `worn` for this avatar's attachments alone,\nand `take`, `move`, `dump` and `unlink` for the things done to what it\nfinds.
objects:example:2	objects	example	Examples	objects -c "garden chair"
objects:example:3	objects	example	Examples	objects --owner "^Example"
offer	offer	line		offer WHO [TEXT]\noffer friendship\nhelp groups: people\nkeywords: friendship add friend befriend request friend ask to be friends
offer:intro	offer	intro		offer asks somebody to be a friend.  It is the other end of the offers\nthat arrive here: what this sends lands in their viewer as a question,\nto be accepted or refused, exactly as theirs lands in `waiting` and\n`offers` on this side.\n\n    offer Example Resident\n\nWho is named by a name, by a key, or by the number beside a name in the\nlast listing.  Anything after the name is the note that goes with the\noffer, and with nothing there the shell writes one -- "Would you be my\nfriend?" -- because an empty note would arrive as a blank line.  That\nsentence is what the other side reads, so a note of one's own is\nusually worth typing.\n\nAs with `im`, the name is the longest run of one or two words at the\nfront that answers to somebody, and the rest is the note.  A name with\na space in it is therefore taken whole rather than leaving its last\nword at the front of the greeting.
offer:example:1	offer	example		offer Example Resident
offer:section:Nothing here says whether it was accepted	offer	section	Nothing here says whether it was accepted	The offer goes out and the command reports that it was offered.  When\nit is answered, the answer arrives as a notice on the screen, whichever\nmode the shell is in -- and the friend list is the other place to look,\nsince a friendship formed while the session is up is added there as it\nhappens.\n\nAn offer nobody answers stays pending on their side, and sending a\nsecond one does not cancel the first.
offer:section:Who a name may be	offer	section	Who a name may be	The session's cache is asked, then whoever is standing in the region,\nand last the grid's own search, the way `im` does it.  This reaches out\nto somebody, and a name guessed at wrong offers friendship to a\nstranger, so what the search finds is taken only when it is the whole\nname -- `Example Resident`, or `example.resident` -- and anything less\nis listed, numbered and refused.  The number is what to give this.
offer:section:Examples	offer	section	Examples	    offer Example Resident\n\nWith a note, which is what the other side reads:\n\n    offer Another Resident we met at the build last night\n\n`offer` is friendship and only friendship.  A teleport offered to\nsomebody is `lure`, which is the grid's word for it and the one the\nshell's own listings use.\n\nSee also: `offers`, `accept` and `decline` for the offers coming the\nother way, `friends` for the list an accepted one joins, `lure` for\noffering a teleport, and `lookup` for somebody known by part of a name.
offer:example:2	offer	example	Examples	offer Another Resident we met at the build last night
offers	offers	line		offers\nfriendship and inventory offers waiting for an answer\nhelp groups: giving people waiting\nkeywords: pending incoming friendship requests items offered gifts received list
offers:intro	offers	intro		`offers` lists the friendship and inventory offers waiting for an\nanswer.  `accept` and `decline` are what answer them, and `waiting` is\nthe listing that has these in it along with everything else somebody\ncan be asked.\n\nIt takes nothing.  Both kinds are in one list.  Each line begins with\nwhat it is, and the two kinds fill the rest of the line differently:\n\n    friendship  Example Resident             12s ago\n    object      a lantern                    4s ago   from Another Resident\n\nA friendship offer has no name of its own, so the second column is\nwhoever made it; an item has one, so the second column is what it was\ncalled and the sender comes at the end.  The first column of an item\nis the kind -- object, notecard, script -- rather than the word\ninventory.\n\nNothing is answered by being looked at.  An offer nobody answers\nsimply stays pending, and the other side is told nothing either way.
offers:section:What arrived before this shell	offers	section	What arrived before this shell	slgod keeps every friendship and item offer made to the avatar until\nit is answered, whether or not any client is attached, and a shell is\nhanded what is still waiting when it starts.  Those are listed with the\nrest, marked `(from before this shell)`.\n\nAn empty list says what it is an account of: since when slgod has been\nkeeping them, or, against a slgod too old to keep a record, that\nanything offered before this shell started is not known here -- and it\nis still open on the other side, waiting, with nothing here to show it.\n\nThe grid cannot be asked for an offer a second time.  The id that\nanswers it arrives once, so what is listed is what slgod or this shell\ncaught.
offers:section:Two shells on one avatar	offers	section	Two shells on one avatar	Both are handed the offer, and the first to answer it is the only one\nthat does.  The other drops it from its list the moment slgod says so,\nwith a line saying who answered and how, and an `accept` or `decline`\ntyped there a moment too late is refused rather than sent twice.
offers:section:Examples	offers	section	Examples	    offers\n\nAnswer one of them by name:\n\n    offers\n    accept lantern\n\nSee also: `accept` and `decline` for answering, `waiting` for the same\noffers alongside teleports, dialogs, permissions and group\ninvitations, and `offer` or `give` for the ones going the other way.
offers:example:1	offers	example	Examples	offers
offers:example:2	offers	example	Examples	accept lantern
parcel	parcel	line		parcel [-mr] [--rows N] [X,Y]\nthe land under this avatar, or under a point in this region\nhelp groups: region\nkeywords: land parcel owner who owns plot property prim allowance permissions build fly scripts stream
parcel:intro	parcel	intro		The land underfoot: its name, who owns it, how much of its prim\nallowance is used, and what it permits.  A region is one place to the\ngrid and several to everybody in it -- `where` gives the region and\nthis gives the other half.  Reach for it when building, flying, running\na script or a stream has stopped working for no reason the region\nexplains.\n\nWith a point it describes any parcel in this region rather than the one\nunderfoot.  The avatar does not move.  The point is metres from the\nregion's south-west corner, the same numbers `where` prints, 0 to 256\neach way:\n\n    parcel\n    parcel 60,60
parcel:example:1	parcel	example		parcel
parcel:example:2	parcel	example		parcel 60,60
parcel:option:-r, --region	parcel	option	-r, --region	List every parcel the session has a map of.  It takes no point: the\nwhole region is what is asked about.
parcel:option:-m, --map	parcel	option	-m, --map	Draw the region's parcels in the shape `map` draws a region: one\ncharacter to a cell, a different letter for each parcel, and a key\nunder it naming them.  It takes no point.  `--region` counts them and\nthis draws them; ask for one.
parcel:option:--rows N	parcel	option	--rows N	How many rows that picture is drawn in, from 2 to 64.  Without it,\n`map_rows`.  Refused without `--map`.
parcel:section:What is true now	parcel	section	What is true now	The parcel is asked for, not remembered, so the answer is what is true\nnow -- a sale, a rename or a split since arrival is in it.\n\nIf the ask goes unanswered, the bare command falls back to what the\nsession was told when it arrived, and says so.  That fallback is only\ngood for the parcel underfoot.  `parcel 60,60` has nothing to fall back\non and reports the failure instead.
parcel:section:The whole region's layout	parcel	section	The whole region's layout	The region's parcels can be listed or drawn, and both need the parcel\noverlay, which arrives when the avatar does and cannot be asked for\nagain.  A shell attached to a daemon that has been up for hours usually\nhas it; a session that has just logged in and not moved may not, and\nthe command says so rather than drawing an empty region.  The only way\nto fetch it again is to arrive again.\n\nThe overlay carries the shapes and no names at all, so `--region` and\n`--map` both ask about a point inside each piece, one after another,\nabout a tenth of a second each: a dozen parcels is a second or two of\nnothing on the screen before either appears.  A piece nothing answers about is\nstill listed, by its shape, with `(no answer)` where the name would be,\nand after three unanswered in a row the rest are not asked about\neither; how many went unnamed is said at the foot.  The listing itself\nstops at thirty parcels and says how many more there were.\n\nOne parcel can be two pieces of ground -- land bought either side of a\nroad is still one parcel -- and the listing says how many pieces the\nparcels were cut into.\n\nLinden's protected land is drawn as ground: a blank for the roads and\nwaterways, and a `.` for the rez zones inside them.  A `?` is a part of\nthe overlay that never arrived.  Each other parcel is a letter, in a\ncolour: the colour is what tells two parcels apart at a glance, and the\nletter is what tells them apart at all when the picture is written to a\nfile or `NO_COLOR` is set.
parcel:section:A local id is not a name	parcel	section	A local id is not a name	Every report has a `local` line, which is the region's own numbering\nfor that parcel.  It belongs to that region and nowhere else: the same\nnumber in the region next door is a different piece of land.
parcel:section:Asking about a parcel selects it	parcel	section	Asking about a parcel selects it	A viewer attached to this session through slgod will notice: its About\nLand floater redraws to show the parcel that was asked about, which is\nnot the one under its avatar.  That is the grid working as designed.\nSomebody watching the screen while somebody else types `parcel 200,200`\nwill see the panel change under them.
parcel:section:Examples	parcel	section	Examples	    parcel\n    parcel 60,60\n    parcel --region\n    parcel --map --rows 24\n\nSee also: `where` for the region and the position, `look` for what the\nsimulator says about the region itself, `group` for the membership that\nparcel permissions usually hang on, and `map` for who is standing here.
parcel:example:3	parcel	example	Examples	parcel --region
parcel:example:4	parcel	example	Examples	parcel --map --rows 24
perms	perms	line		perms [--everyone LETTERS] [--group LETTERS] [--next LETTERS] [--owner LETTERS] [-w SECONDS] NAME|UUID\nset what others may do with a rezzed object: c copy, m modify, t transfer, v move\nhelp groups: giving region\nkeywords: permissions next owner copy modify transfer move owner group everyone allow others rights
perms:intro	perms	intro		`perms` sets what somebody other than this avatar may do with an\nobject standing in the region.  It is the question `take` and `place`\nleave open: an object can be moved into inventory and back out again\nwithout anybody ever having said who else may copy it.\n\nThe object is named by the word the region calls it or by its key.  A\nname with a space in it has to be quoted, and a name several objects\nanswer to is refused with their keys rather than guessed at.  At least\none of the four groups has to be named, because a command that changes\nthings and was told to change nothing has been mistyped rather than\nasked for a report.\n\n    perms --next cm lantern\n\nThe flags go in front of the name.  Option parsing stops at the first\nargument that is not a flag, so `perms lantern --next cm` is a line\nwith an object named and no group named at all, and what comes back is\na complaint that none of four flags was given, to somebody who has\njust typed one of them.  Read that message as saying the flag is in\nthe wrong place.
perms:example:1	perms	example		perms --next cm lantern
perms:section:The letters	perms	section	The letters	Every one of the four flags takes the same *LETTERS*: `c` copy, `m`\nmodify, `t` transfer and `v` move, or `all` for the four of them, or\n`none` for a bare refusal of everything.  What is set is said back in\nwords, so a line that was typed as letters can be read without knowing\nthem.  Those four are all there are here: export and the damage bit are\nreal permission bits and are deliberately not among them.
perms:option:--owner LETTERS	perms	option	--owner LETTERS	What the owner may do now.
perms:option:--group LETTERS	perms	option	--group LETTERS	What this avatar's group may do now.
perms:option:--everyone LETTERS	perms	option	--everyone LETTERS	What passers-by may do now.
perms:option:--next LETTERS	perms	option	--next LETTERS	What the next owner may do.  It is the set that travels: it is what\nthe object carries when it changes hands, and it is what somebody\nmeans by full permissions on a thing they were given.
perms:option:-w, --wait SECONDS	perms	option	-w, --wait SECONDS	How long to let the region describe itself before giving up on finding\nthe object.  Without it, 30 seconds.
perms:section:A set of letters replaces, and does not add	perms	section	A set of letters replaces, and does not add	Each flag says what that group may do afterwards, in full.  A letter\nleft out is a letter turned off, so asking for modify alone on\nsomething that could be copied has taken copying away.
perms:section:Nothing here confirms it	perms	section	Nothing here confirms it	The request goes out and nothing answers it, so the line printed is\nwhat was asked for rather than what the region did.  Where that\nmatters -- a parcel or an object that will not have its permissions\nchanged refuses in silence -- `dump` is the way to look: the masks are\namong what it writes out for each prim.
perms:section:Examples	perms	section	Examples	Let anybody who gets it copy and modify it, but not pass it on:\n\n    perms --next cm lantern\n\nTake everything back from everyone but the owner:\n\n    perms --group none --everyone none lantern\n\nSee also: `take` and `place`, `give` for handing an item to somebody,\nand `dump` for reading back what an object's masks actually are.
perms:example:2	perms	example	Examples	perms --group none --everyone none lantern
place	place	line		place [--at X,Y,Z] PATH|UUID\nput an inventory object into the world, which is what take undoes\nhelp groups: region\nkeywords: rez put out down object from inventory world ground put back set down
place:intro	place	intro		`place` takes one object out of inventory and puts it into the\nworld.  It is what take undoes: take brings a rezzed object in, place\nputs one back out, and between them an object can be picked up and set\ndown again.\n\n    place Objects/probe\n\nThe argument is an inventory path, relative to the folder the shell is\nin, or the uuid of an item.  A folder is refused: `place` puts out one\nobject, not the contents of a folder.\n\nThe active group matters more than it looks.  A parcel usually grants\n"create objects" to a group rather than to a person, so an avatar with\nthe wrong group active is refused by the land even where it plainly has\npermission, and the refusal talks about the land rather than about the\ngroup.  `group` says which is active, and sets it.
place:example:1	place	example		place Objects/probe
place:option:--at X,Y,Z	place	option	--at X,Y,Z	Where to put it, in this region.  Without it, a metre east of the\navatar: the avatar's own position with one metre added to X, near\nenough to hand for something that is about to be looked at.  East and\nnot in front -- nothing here asks which way the avatar is facing, so a\nthing put down behind somebody goes down east of them all the same.\n\nTwo or three numbers with commas between them and no spaces.  A pair\nis accepted rather than refused, and the Z that was not given is zero,\nwhich is the bottom of the region and not the height the avatar is\nstanding at.  `rez`'s `--at` is spelt the same and insists on three.
place:section:One object, and only one	place	section	One object, and only one	`place` takes exactly one argument, and a name with a space in it has\nto be quoted.  Extra words are not a position: `place probe 10 20 30`\nis refused rather than treated as a move.  Moving something already in\nthe world is `move`.
place:section:Examples	place	section	Examples	Put a thing down beside the avatar:\n\n    place Objects/probe\n\nPut it somewhere named, quoting the name because it has a space in it:\n\n    place --at 128,128,25 "Objects/big sign"
place:example:2	place	example	Examples	place --at 128,128,25 "Objects/big sign"
place:section:A link is followed	place	section	A link is followed	An outfit folder holds links rather than items.  A link carries the\nsame name as the thing it points at, and a listing tells the two apart\nonly by the word `link` in the type column of `ls -l`, so a path taken\nfrom `My Outfits` names a link almost every time.\n\n`place` follows one to the item at the other end, which is what the id\nit sends has to be: the grid has no object for a link's id and answers\nan id it does not recognise with silence rather than with a refusal.\n\nA link this inventory cannot follow is refused, naming the id it\nlooked for.  A link outlives what it pointed at, so an outfit put\ntogether years ago may name things that have since been deleted.\n\nSee also: `take`, `move`, `group`, `rez` for building an object out of\na JSON file rather than out of inventory, and `perms` for what others\nmay do with it once it is standing there.
profile	profile	line		profile [-w SECONDS] WHO\nwhat somebody's profile says: born, payment, partner, about, groups\nhelp groups: people\nkeywords: profile about bio born age partner groups picks information person avatar
profile:intro	profile	intro		profile prints what somebody's profile says about them: when the\naccount was made, what it says about payment, who its partner is, the\nweb page and the text they wrote, what they say they are looking for,\ncan do and speak, and the groups they chose to show.  It is a public\nquestion, answered by the grid to anybody who asks, and nothing about\nit reaches the person asked about.\n\n    profile Example Resident\n\nWho is named by a name, by a key, or by the number beside a name in the\nlast listing.  Empty fields say what they do not know rather than\nprinting a blank: a blank beside "born" reads as an answer that went\nmissing, where "not said" reads as a profile that has none.  Four are\nprinted only where there is something in them -- the web page, and the\n"wants", "skills" and "speaks" that viewers stopped showing years ago.\n\nThe keys stay in the lines.  A key is what everything else here takes,\nincluding this command's own argument, so a partner or a group worth\nreading about is one more line to type rather than a search.
profile:example:1	profile	example		profile Example Resident
profile:option:-w, --wait SECONDS	profile	option	-w, --wait SECONDS	How many seconds to give the grid.  Fifteen when it is not said.
profile:section:It takes more from the grid's search than anything else does	profile	section	It takes more from the grid's search than anything else does	A name is matched against what the session has heard, then against\nwhoever is standing in the region, and then the grid's own search is\nmade, as it is for `im`, `offer`, `give` and the other commands that\nname somebody.  What differs is what this one takes from it.  Those\ntake only a name typed whole, since guessing wrong for them delivers\nsomething to a stranger; this one takes the only person the search\nturned up even when the name was only part of theirs, and a username\ntyped on its own, because a profile is exactly the question one asks\nabout somebody who is not here and guessing wrong costs nothing but a\nwasted listing.  Several people answering to the name are listed,\nnumbered, and a number picks one.  A name typed in full that matches\none row exactly is that row even when the search returned others.
profile:section:Two fields that are easy to misread	profile	section	Two fields that are easy to misread	"none on file" and "not revealed" are different facts about payment.\nAn account carrying a caption -- a Linden, or one of the special\naccounts -- has its payment information withheld rather than absent.\nWhere there is caption text, it is printed under the payment line as\n"account", so that it does not read as a title somebody chose.  Where\nthere is not, the payment line stands alone, and that is the answer\nrather than a field that went missing.\n\nThe groups are only the ones their owner published, which is why the\ncount says so: somebody may be in a dozen and show none.
profile:section:A key nobody knows is a sentence, not a failure	profile	section	A key nobody knows is a sentence, not a failure	The grid has no way of saying it has never heard of somebody.  The\nanswer for a made-up key is the wait running out, printed as the plain\nsentence it is -- nothing went wrong, and the question was answered.\nIt is also the only case where the wait is spent, since a profile that\nexists arrives in milliseconds.
profile:section:Examples	profile	section	Examples	    profile Example Resident\n    lookup another\n    profile 2\n\nSee also: `lookup` for the search this makes on its own, `who` for the\npeople in the region, and `group` for the groups this avatar is in,\nwhich is a different question from the ones a profile lists.
profile:example:2	profile	example	Examples	lookup another
profile:example:3	profile	example	Examples	profile 2
put	put	line		put [-lN] [-d TEXT] [--filter NAME] [--filters] [-f PATH] [-n NAME] [-o FILE] [--ratio N] [--round MODE] [--round-x MODE] [--round-y MODE] FILE\nupload an image as a texture; costs L$, so -N says what it would do\nhelp groups: inventory\nkeywords: upload image picture photo texture png jpeg import costs
put:intro	put	intro		`put` uploads a picture from this machine as a texture in inventory.\nIt costs L$ every time.  `get` is the other direction and does not.\n\n    put photo.jpg\n\nThe argument is a file.  PNG, JPEG, GIF, BMP and TIFF are read, and\nthe orientation a camera left in the file is applied.  A JPEG 2000\ncodestream (`.j2c` and its relatives) goes up untouched.\n\nEach side of the picture must be a power of two and at most 2048.\nAlmost nothing already is, so the picture is resampled and the report\nsays from what size to what.  A side above 2048 comes down to it\nwhatever the rounding says.  Nothing is resized when both sides are\nalready legal.\n\nThe fee is ten lindens up to a megapixel of area, 1024x1024 included.\nAbove that it is fifty -- but that larger figure belongs to the account\nrather than to the grid, and fifty is what a beta grid wanted on the\nday it was measured.  Nothing here reads the real price, so the figure\nis not sent hopefully: it travels with the upload, the simulator\ncompares it against its own, and a request naming the wrong one is\nrefused.  An account whose large-texture price is not fifty therefore\ngets a refusal naming the right number rather than a wrong bill.\n\nThe grid charges before it looks, so a refusal afterwards has still\ncost the fee.  Every check it would make is made here first; `-N` stops\nbefore anything is sent.
put:example:1	put	example		put photo.jpg
put:option:-n, --name NAME	put	option	-n, --name NAME	Call the item this instead of the file's name.
put:option:-d, --desc TEXT	put	option	-d, --desc TEXT	The item's description.
put:option:-f, --folder PATH	put	option	-f, --folder PATH	The inventory folder it lands in.  Without it, `Textures`.
put:option:-N, --dry-run	put	option	-N, --dry-run	Print what would go up, how many bytes it is, and the fee, then stop.
put:option:-o, --out FILE	put	option	-o, --out FILE	Write the converted picture here instead of uploading.  The extension\npicks the format, so the rounding and the filter can be looked at\nwithout paying.
put:option:--round MODE	put	option	--round MODE	How both sides round to a power of two: `nearest`, `up` or `down`.\nWithout it the rounding is the viewer's, biased downwards -- a side\ngoes up only past 1.75 times the power of two below it, so 800 becomes\n512 and 900 becomes 1024.
put:option:--round-x MODE	put	option	--round-x MODE	Round the width only.
put:option:--round-y MODE	put	option	--round-y MODE	Round the height only.
put:option:--filter NAME	put	option	--filter NAME	How the picture is resampled.  The default is `lanczos`.  Photographs\nwant that; pixel art and masks want `nearest`; diagrams want\n`catmullrom`.  The filter only matters when the picture is resized.
put:option:--filters	put	option	--filters	List the filters and what each is good for, then stop.
put:option:-l, --lossless	put	option	-l, --lossless	Store every pixel, at several times the size.  Below an area of\n128x128 nothing is compressed whether it was asked for or not, so a\n512x32 banner is lossless as surely as a 128x128 square is.
put:option:--ratio N	put	option	--ratio N	Compress about *N*:1 rather than the default 8.  Compression does not\nchange the fee; the price is settled by the dimensions.
put:section:Examples	put	section	Examples	    put -N photo.jpg\n    put --filter nearest --round up icons/heart.png\n    put --name "wall panel" -f Textures/panels panel.png\n\nSee also: `get`, `new` for a notecard or a script, which cost nothing,\nand `ls` for finding what has been uploaded afterwards.
put:example:2	put	example	Examples	put -N photo.jpg
put:example:3	put	example	Examples	put --filter nearest --round up icons/heart.png
put:example:4	put	example	Examples	put --name "wall panel" -f Textures/panels panel.png
pwd	pwd	line		pwd\nwhere in inventory we are\nhelp groups: inventory\nkeywords: current folder directory where am in inventory path
pwd:intro	pwd	intro		`pwd` prints the inventory folder this shell is in, as a path from the\nroot.\n\n    pwd\n    /Objects/lanterns\n\n`cd` is what changes it.  Every command that takes a path without a\nleading slash takes it relative to this.  The prompt already carries\nthe same path, so this is chiefly for a script or a line redirected\ninto a file.\n\nIt takes no argument.  A word given anyway is ignored rather than\nrefused.
pwd:example:1	pwd	example		pwd
pwd:section:The root is a slash	pwd	section	The root is a slash	Inventory has one root and no home.  A shell that has not been told\notherwise starts at `/`.
pwd:section:The folder belongs to this shell	pwd	section	The folder belongs to this shell	Nothing on the grid records where a shell was looking.  A second\nshell on the same avatar starts at the root however deep the first\nhas gone, and quitting forgets it.
pwd:section:A path is meant to be read back	pwd	section	A path is meant to be read back	An inventory name may hold nearly any printable character, including\na slash.  A slash in a name is printed with a backslash in front of\nit, and a backslash is doubled: the path that comes out is the path\nthat goes back in.  The grid trims a leading or trailing space, so a\nname cannot begin or end with one.
pwd:section:Examples	pwd	section	Examples	    pwd\n\nSee also: `cd`, `ls`, and `find` for looking below here rather than\nat where here is.
quit	quit	line		quit\nleave slsh\nalso called: exit\nhelp groups: shell\nkeywords: exit leave close end shell
quit:intro	quit	intro		`quit` leaves the shell, and `exit` is the same command under a\nsecond name.  What it does to the avatar depends entirely on how\nthis shell got its session, which is the one thing worth knowing\nbefore typing it.\n\n    quit\n\nAttached to the daemon -- the ordinary case -- the avatar stays\nexactly where it is, logged in, with the daemon holding the session\nfor whatever attaches next.  Started with `--direct`, this shell\nlogged in for itself and holds the session, so quitting logs the\navatar out of the grid.  The line printed at startup says which of\nthe two is in force.\n\nTaking a daemon-held avatar down is a separate act against a named\navatar, which is what `logout` is for; no way of ending a shell does\nit.\n\nNeither name is the deprecated one and neither is going away.\n`help all` prints each command once, under whichever name sorts\nfirst, so it shows `exit` and not `quit`, while `help shell` shows\n`quit`.  `man` answers to either and prints the same page.
quit:example:1	quit	example		quit
quit:section:The keys that also end it	quit	section	The keys that also end it	At an empty command prompt, either interrupt or end-of-input leaves.\nNeither does so while there is anything else for it to mean first:\nwith a line half typed, interrupt clears the line; in chat mode it\nreturns to the command prompt, so leaving chat and leaving the shell\nare the first and second press rather than one; and while a\nmulti-line answer is being typed, end-of-input finishes the answer\nand interrupt abandons it.
quit:section:It can be the last line of a file of commands	quit	section	It can be the last line of a file of commands	A file run with `.` may end by quitting, and the shell stops there\n-- which is the difference between a file that leaves a session open\nfor more work and one that is the whole session.  Lines after it are\nnot run, since there is nothing left to run them.
quit:section:Examples	quit	section	Examples	    quit\n\nSee also: `logout` for taking an avatar down rather than a shell,\n`agents` for what the daemon still holds afterwards, and `.` for\nrunning a file that ends this way.
reform	reform	line		reform [-w SECONDS] NAME|UUID FILE\nchange an object to match a JSON file; what it omits is left alone\nhelp groups: region\nkeywords: edit modify change object json file update apply properties
reform:intro	reform	intro		`reform` changes an object that is already standing so that it matches\na JSON file.  It is the third of the three verbs sharing one format:\n`dump` says what is there, `rez` builds what a file describes, and this\none edits.\n\n    reform lantern brighter.json\n\nIt takes the object -- named by the word the region calls it, or by its\nkey -- and then the file.  What the file omits is left exactly as it\nwas, which is what makes a two-line file a useful edit rather than a\ndemolition.
reform:example:1	reform	example		reform lantern brighter.json
reform:option:-w, --wait SECONDS	reform	option	-w, --wait SECONDS	How long to let the region describe itself.  Without it, thirty.
reform:section:It edits, and never builds or removes	reform	section	It edits, and never builds or removes	Prims are matched by position in the list: the first prim described is\nthe root, and the rest follow in the order `dump` writes them, which is\nthe order of the region's own ids for them rather than the link numbers\na script would count in.  The two orders need not agree, so a file that\ncame out of `dump` lines up prim for prim, and one written by hand\nagainst link numbers may not.  A file with fewer prims than the object\nleaves the rest of them alone.  A file with more is refused, because\nthe extra prims would have to be made and linked, and that is what\n`rez` is for.  Nothing here removes a prim either.\n\nPrims are the only thing it will not build.  Every prim the file\ndescribes has its inventory put in as `rez` puts it in, so a file\ncarrying script source installs that script, compiles it and sets it\nrunning unless the file says the script is disabled -- and a script\nthat will not compile fails the command.
reform:section:What "left alone" cannot quite mean	reform	section	What "left alone" cannot quite mean	Two things are sent whole, so for those the file's silence is filled\nin from what the prim is now rather than from nothing.\n\nPosition, rotation and scale travel in one message.  A file that gives\nonly a position is honoured by reading the prim's current rotation and\nscale and sending all three.\n\nThe shape is all or nothing: there is no message for "change the hollow\nand leave the rest of the shape", so a file that says anything about\nthe shape has the rest of it taken from what the prim is at that\nmoment.  A file that says nothing about the shape does not touch it.\n\nPositions in the file are region coordinates, as `dump` writes them,\nbut a child is moved in its root's frame, so the root's position and\nrotation are undone on the way in.  The linkset is read once, before\nanything is sent, and that reading is what the undoing uses: the root\nas it stood before the edit, not as the file is about to leave it.\n\nSo a file that moves the root and names a child absolutely does not\nmean what it says.  Moving the root carries its children with it, and\nthe child named absolutely is then placed against where the root used\nto be, which lands it displaced by exactly the root's movement.  An\nabsolute position for a child is only what it looks like where the root\nstays where it is.  Where both have to change, move the root in one run\nand `dump` again before writing anything about the children.
reform:section:Examples	reform	section	Examples	Write an object down, change the file, and put the change back:\n\n    dump --out sign.json sign\n    reform sign sign.json\n\nA file naming one prim and one field is a one-field edit:\n\n    reform lantern brighter.json\n\nSee also: `dump`, `rez`, `texture` for the faces the format leaves out,\nand `move` for shifting a whole object without a file at all.
reform:example:2	reform	example	Examples	dump --out sign.json sign
reform:example:3	reform	example	Examples	reform sign sign.json
regions	regions	line		regions [-w SECONDS] NAME\nfind regions on the grid's map by the start of a name\nhelp groups: region\nkeywords: search find region sim location grid map coordinates name
regions:intro	regions	intro		Asks the grid's map where a region is.  It is `look` pointed somewhere\nelse: `look` describes the region this avatar is standing in, and this\none reaches a region on the other side of the grid that nobody here has\nmentioned and this avatar has never been to.\n\nThe argument is the name, or the start of one, joined with spaces -- a\nname with a space in it needs no quoting.  Each line that comes back is\none region: its name, the square it occupies on the grid, its maturity\nrating, and its handle.\n\n    regions Example Landing
regions:example:1	regions	example		regions Example Landing
regions:option:-w, --wait SECONDS	regions	option	-w, --wait SECONDS	How long to give the map to answer.  Without it, fifteen seconds.
regions:section:Several matches is the ordinary case and not a mistake	regions	section	Several matches is the ordinary case and not a mistake	The search matches from the start of a name and ignores case.  A word\nfinds every region beginning with that word -- `Example` finds Example\nLanding, Example Landing North and Example Shallows alike -- and typing\na name out in full still finds it along with everything else that\nbegins with it.\n\nThere is no way to ask for an exact name, because the map has no such\nquestion in it.  So this prints what matched and picks none of it: the\nline to read is the one whose name is the name.  A short name is a long\nlisting rather than a refusal.\n\nA name that matches nothing is an error saying which name.
regions:section:How long the map is given	regions	section	How long the map is given	Fifteen seconds are allowed for the whole of the answer.  Running out\nis a real timeout and not "no such region": what arrived is a fragment\nof the list, and a fragment printed as the answer would be a shorter\ngrid than the real one.  The error says how many had come in by then.
regions:section:The handle is the number a teleport is addressed to	regions	section	The handle is the number a teleport is addressed to	A region's place on the grid can be said two ways.  The square -- 1200,\n980 -- is the readable one, and the handle is that square's south-west\ncorner in metres packed into a single number, which is how every\nmessage that names a region names it.\n\n`tp` takes a name rather than a handle, and looks it up through this\nsame search -- so the listing here is what `tp` will be choosing\nbetween, and running `regions` first is how to find out whether the\nname is enough.  A name that matches several is refused there, where\nhere it is the answer.
regions:section:What the map leaves out	regions	section	What the map leaves out	The reply has fields in it for how many avatars are in a region, what\nthe region allows and how high its water is, and Second Life sends all\nthree as zero for every region every time.  So this cannot say whether\na region is up, or busy, or empty; the position and the rating are the\nwhole of the answer.\n\nA region listed here is a region that exists.  It is not a region that\nwill have this avatar: an estate may refuse an arrival for reasons the\nmap has no field for.
regions:section:Examples	regions	section	Examples	    regions Example Landing\n    regions Example\n\nSee also: `look` for what the region underfoot says about itself,\n`where` for the position inside it, `tp` for going to one of these, and\n`neighbours` for the regions this one touches, which are the ones the\navatar can walk into.
regions:example:2	regions	example	Examples	regions Example
rez	rez	line		rez [-k] [--at X,Y,Z] FILE\nbuild the object a JSON file describes\nhelp groups: region\nkeywords: build create object json file prims make construct
rez:intro	rez	intro		`rez` builds the object a JSON file describes: the prims are made and\nlinked, named and described, and the scripts named in the file are\ninstalled in them.  `dump` is the other direction, and the file each of\nthem reads is the same file.\n\n    rez lantern.json\n\nThe argument is the path of a file describing exactly one object.  A\nfile with several in it is refused rather than built one after another,\nsince what should stand where would then be the file's business and not\nthe command's.  What is built is left standing: an object being built\nis usually about to be looked at, so nothing takes it into inventory.
rez:example:1	rez	example		rez lantern.json
rez:option:--at X,Y,Z	rez	option	--at X,Y,Z	Where the root lands; every other prim keeps the vector from the root\nthat the file gave it.  Without it, the positions the file records.\n\nThree numbers with commas between them and no spaces.  Two are refused\nwith a message naming all three.  `place`'s `--at` is the other way\nround: it takes two or three, and fills in a zero for the Z it was not\ngiven, so a pair of numbers that `place` accepts without a word is a\nusage error here.
rez:option:-k, --keep	rez	option	-k, --keep	Leave what was built standing even if a script will not compile.\nWithout it, a failure removes what it made and says it has: half a\nbuild is prims standing in the region that nobody asked for.  This is\nfor the case where the wreckage is the interesting part.
rez:section:This is not what everyone means by rezzing	rez	section	This is not what everyone means by rezzing	`Rez` is the word everybody uses for putting an inventory object into\nthe world, and that is `place`.  One word cannot mean both "make what\nthis file describes" and "put back what I took": the first invents an\nobject and the second restores one, and somebody who mixed them up\nwould be told their object was not valid JSON.
rez:section:Where it lands	rez	section	Where it lands	The file says where each prim goes, in region coordinates.  A file\nwritten for another region names coordinates that land somewhere else\nhere, so `--at` moves the whole thing without the file having to be\nedited: the root lands on the point given, and every other prim keeps\nthe vector from the root that the file gave it.
rez:section:What is made, and what is not	rez	section	What is made, and what is not	Only the scripts in a description are made.  The rest of what a file\nmay name is an asset this avatar may not have, and a texture named by\nkey is not something a client can conjure into an object it does not\nalready own a copy of.  A notecard is the one to watch, because it is\nwritten in the same list as a script and reads as though it would be\nmade too: a file describing a notecard with a body in it builds a prim\nwhose contents are empty, and says nothing about the notecard it did\nnot write.
rez:section:Examples	rez	section	Examples	Build what a file says, where it says:\n\n    rez lantern.json\n\nBuild one here rather than at the position the file records:\n\n    rez --at 128,128,25 probe.json\n\nSee also: `dump`, `reform`, `place` for putting an inventory object\ninto the world, `link` and `unlink`, and `take` for bringing what was\nbuilt in.
rez:example:2	rez	example	Examples	rez --at 128,128,25 probe.json
rm	rm	line		rm [--in OBJECT] [--newest] [--oldest] [--remove-all-copies] PATH ...\ndelete items, permanently; a name that means several is refused\nhelp groups: inventory\nkeywords: delete remove erase destroy item folder inventory permanently
rm:intro	rm	intro		`rm` deletes inventory, permanently and at once.  Nothing here moves\nanything to the trash: what `emptytrash` disposes of is what a\nviewer put there, and what `rm` names is simply gone.\n\n    rm Notecards/README\n\nThe arguments are paths, relative to the folder the shell is in, or\nuuids.  Several may be given on one line, and a folder goes with\neverything inside it.
rm:example:1	rm	example		rm Notecards/README
rm:option:--newest	rm	option	--newest	Of several of a name, delete the one acquired last, by the date\n`ls -l` prints.  What went is named on the way out, with its date\nand its id.
rm:option:--oldest	rm	option	--oldest	Of several of a name, delete the one acquired first.
rm:option:--remove-all-copies	rm	option	--remove-all-copies	Delete every one of the name.  It is the whole words; no shorter\nspelling of it is accepted.  The count printed at the end is the\nonly evidence it did what was wanted, and is printed only when the\nname meant more than one thing.
rm:option:--in OBJECT	rm	option	--in OBJECT	Delete from inside a rezzed object rather than from inventory.  It\nis a delete and not a take: the copy is gone, and anything wanted\nback has to come from the original in inventory.  `--newest` and\n`--oldest` do not apply with it: what an object holds has no dates\non it.  What follows is a name, matched exactly and in the case it\nhas.  The paths and the uuids above are inventory's.  The id beside\na line of `ls -l --in` is not something to hand back to this.
rm:section:One name can mean a dozen items	rm	section	One name can mean a dozen items	Inventory names are not unique, so a path may name several things.\nA plain `rm` refuses that and deletes nothing.\n\n    /Scripts$ rm greeter\n    slsh: rm: "greeter" is 3 things here: say --newest or --oldest\n    to delete one of them, --remove-all-copies for all 3, or name\n    one by its id, which ls -l prints beside the date\n\n`ls -l greeter` is what to look at then: a path that names items\nlists those items, one line each, with the dates and the ids that\ntell them apart.\n\n`--newest`, `--oldest` and `--remove-all-copies` are one choice\nrather than three options.  Any two of them together is a line that\nmeans two things, and is refused.
rm:section:When a date does not settle it	rm	section	When a date does not settle it	`--newest` and `--oldest` refuse rather than guess in two cases.\nInventory dates are whole seconds, so several items can be stamped\nalike, and there is no sense in which one of those is the newer.\nThe other case is something with no date at all -- a folder has\nnone -- and undated is not the same as old.\n\nBoth refusals point at the id.  The other end of the pile may still\nbe unambiguous: two tied at the newest date does not stop `--oldest`\nfrom having an answer.
rm:section:The star, which is not a glob	rm	section	The star, which is not a glob	A path of one star means every item in the folder the shell is in,\nand it is refused unless `--remove-all-copies` is given as well.\nThere is no other pattern.  Folders are never included in it:\nemptying a folder of its items is a thing to want, and taking its\nsubfolders with them is not.\n\nAn item may itself be named with a star.  Such an item can no longer\nbe named at a prompt; its id still names it.
rm:section:Hundreds of removals take minutes	rm	section	Hundreds of removals take minutes	Each one is a round trip to the grid.  A count climbs in place while\nit works, and it goes to the terminal rather than to the output: a\nline redirected to a file catches the result and not a flickering\ncounter.
rm:section:Examples	rm	section	Examples	    rm Notecards/README\n    rm --newest greeter\n    rm --oldest greeter\n    rm --remove-all-copies greeter\n    rm --remove-all-copies *\n    rm d8467e57-...\n    rm --in lantern hello.lsl
rm:example:2	rm	example	Examples	rm --newest greeter
rm:example:3	rm	example	Examples	rm --oldest greeter
rm:example:4	rm	example	Examples	rm --remove-all-copies greeter
rm:example:5	rm	example	Examples	rm --remove-all-copies *
rm:example:6	rm	example	Examples	rm d8467e57-...
rm:example:7	rm	example	Examples	rm --in lantern hello.lsl
rm:section:A link is not followed	rm	section	A link is not followed	A link is an inventory entry in its own right, and `rm` acts on the\nentry.  Deleting a link deletes the link, and leaves the item it\npointed at alone.  An item may have links to it from several outfits,\nand following one here would reach past all of them to the item they\nshare.\n\nSee also: `emptytrash`, `mv`, `ls`, and `drop` for putting something\ninto an object that `rm` can take out again.
save	save	line		save FILE PATH\nwrite a local file into a notecard or script that is already there\nhelp groups: inventory\nkeywords: upload write edit update notecard script local file disk contents replace
save:intro	save	intro		`save` writes a file from this machine into a notecard or a script\nthat already exists in inventory.  It is what `cat` reads back, and\nit is not what makes an item: `new` does that.\n\n    save notes.txt Notecards/README\n\nTwo arguments, the file first and the item second.  The item takes\nthe rest of the line, since a name with spaces in it is ordinary in\ninventory and rare on disk.\n\nThe item's own type decides which write happens.  A link is refused\nrather than followed: it carries the type of whatever it points at,\nso it looks exactly like the notecard it names, and the write would\ngo to the link's own item, which is not where the text lives.
save:example:1	save	example		save notes.txt Notecards/README
save:section:A script that does not compile is still saved	save	section	A script that does not compile is still saved	The item is written and then compiled, in that order, so the verdict\nis a clause on the same line rather than a failure.  The compiler\nreports the first error and stops.  The file goes up as it stands:\nunlike `new`, nothing here puts a skeleton in a script given no\nsource.
save:section:What it does not do	save	section	What it does not do	It does not start anything and does not touch the world.  A script\nin inventory is not running and cannot be made to run; putting one\nthere and starting it is `new --in OBJECT`.  Nor does it reach the\ncopies already inside objects: they were separate the moment they\nwere put there, and a save leaves every one of them as it was.
save:section:Examples	save	section	Examples	    save notes.txt Notecards/README\n    save hello.lsl /Scripts/greeter
save:example:2	save	example	Examples	save hello.lsl /Scripts/greeter
save:section:A link is followed	save	section	A link is followed	An outfit folder holds links rather than items.  A link carries the\nsame name as the thing it points at, and a listing tells the two apart\nonly by the word `link` in the type column of `ls -l`, so a path taken\nfrom `My Outfits` names a link almost every time.\n\n`save` follows one to the item at the other end, which is what the id\nit sends has to be: the grid has no object for a link's id and answers\nan id it does not recognise with silence rather than with a refusal.\n\nA link this inventory cannot follow is refused, naming the id it\nlooked for.  A link outlives what it pointed at, so an outfit put\ntogether years ago may name things that have since been deleted.\n\nSee also: `cat`, `new`, `drop`, `start`, and `put` for an image,\nwhich costs L$.
say	say	line		say [-c CHANNEL] TEXT ...\nsay one line without leaving command mode\nhelp groups: people\nkeywords: speak chat out loud local chat channel message nearby
say:intro	say	intro		say puts one line into open chat without leaving command mode.  `chat`\nis for a conversation, and this is for the single remark somebody wants\nto make in the middle of doing something else.\n\n    say the gate is open\n\nEverything after the flags is what is said, joined with spaces, so\nnothing needs quoting except a word the shell would otherwise take for\nsomething of its own.  It goes out at ordinary speaking volume and is\nheard by whoever is near enough, and the line is printed back marked\nwith where it went -- `Local` for open chat, `channel N` for any other\n-- since nothing else on the screen would show for it.\n\nNothing says whether anybody heard it.  Chat is not acknowledged by\nanything, so a line that went out is the whole of what can be reported,\nand a line the circuit refused is not printed as though it had been\nsaid.
say:example:1	say	example		say the gate is open
say:option:-c CHANNEL	say	option	-c CHANNEL	The channel to say it on.  Without it, open chat -- channel zero.  A\nnegative one reaches scripts and carries at most 254 bytes, refused\nrather than truncated.
say:section:The channel is what a script listens on	say	section	The channel is what a script listens on	Open chat is channel zero and is where people talk.  A script listens\non a channel of its own, and saying something there is how a shell\ndrives one: the object hears the text with this avatar named as the\nspeaker, exactly as it would hear a remark made out loud.\n\nA negative channel is the usual choice for a script, because a viewer\nhas no way to speak on one, so nothing a passer-by types can set the\nscript off by accident.\n\nThe reach is chat's reach either way.  A negative channel is a private\nword rather than a region-wide one: heard nearby, not heard a hundred\nmetres up.
say:section:Examples	say	section	Examples	Say something out loud:\n\n    say the gate is open\n\nStart a script that is listening on a channel of its own:\n\n    say -c -4242 start\n\nSee also: `chat` and `im` for talking to one person rather than to the\nroom, `waiting` for what a script puts up when it wants an answer back,\nand `touch` for the other way of setting one off.
say:example:2	say	example	Examples	say -c -4242 start
set	set	line		set [NAME [VALUE]]\nthe settings and their values; "set NAME VALUE" changes one and remembers it\nhelp groups: shell\nkeywords: settings preferences configuration option config change setting value
set:intro	set	intro		`set` is the settings, from inside the shell.  There are three\nforms, told apart by how many words follow:\n\n    set                     every setting, its value, and what it is for\n    set NAME                one of them\n    set NAME VALUE          change it, and write it to the settings file\n\nThe value is the rest of the line, joined with single spaces, so\nnothing has to be quoted to be typed.  That matters for the settings\nwhose value is a command line of its own:\n\n    set viewer_launch open -a {app} --args --grid {grid}\n\nThe one exception is `>`, which is a redirection wherever it is\ntyped unquoted, with or without a space in front of it:\n`set viewer_grid a>b` sets the setting to `a` and puts set's own\nreport into a new file called `b`.  Quote a value with one in it --\n`set viewer_grid "a>b"`.
set:example:1	set	example		set                     every setting, its value, and what it is for
set:example:2	set	example		set NAME                one of them
set:example:3	set	example		set NAME VALUE          change it, and write it to the settings file
set:example:4	set	example		set viewer_launch open -a {app} --args --grid {grid}
set:section:It writes the file, and does nothing else to it	set	section	It writes the file, and does nothing else to it	Every change is written to the settings file -- `config` in the\ndirectory `$SLSH_CONFIG_DIR` names, otherwise `slsh/config` under\n`$XDG_CONFIG_HOME`, otherwise `~/.config/slsh/config` -- and the\nfile is created, with its directory, if it is not there yet.  The\npath is printed every time, since the file is the thing that will\nstill be true tomorrow.\n\nWhat changes in that file is the one line for the one setting, and\nnothing else.  Everything up to and including the `=` is left\nexactly as it stands, so the indentation, the column the equals\nsigns are lined up in, and whichever spelling of the name is already\nthere all survive.  A setting the file does not mention is appended,\none line, at the end.  Comments on lines of their own survive\nuntouched.\n\nA `#` after a value is part of the value rather than a note about\nit: a line reading `map_ratio = 7:3  # measured here` is a\n`map_ratio` of `7:3  # measured here`, which is not a ratio, and\nsince a value the setting will not take is refused when the file is\nread, it is a shell that will not come up until the line is edited.\nA comment has to be on a line of its own.\n\nIf the file names a setting twice, it is the last of the two that is\nin force, since the file is read from the top and each line\noverwrites what came before; that is the line `set` changes, for the\nsame reason.
set:section:Some of it cannot take until slsh is started again	set	section	Some of it cannot take until slsh is started again	Three settings are used before there is a prompt to type `set` at:\nthe address, because the session was attached through it; the\nprofile, because it says which avatar this is; and the escape key,\nbecause the terminal was already in raw mode reading for it.  A flag\nmay have overridden the file for this run as well.\n\nThey are written to the file all the same, and both the listing and\nthe answer to a change say so outright.  The listing ends their line\nwith `at startup only`, and changing one prints the sentence about\nthe next slsh under it.  The three are `addr`, `agent` and `escape`,\nand they are the only three.\n\nEverything else takes at once.  `set map_ratio 2:1` and then `map`\ndraws the new shape.
set:section:Where the settings come from and what beats what	set	section	Where the settings come from and what beats what	A flag on the slsh command line beats the file, and the file beats\nthe defaults.  So a shell started with `--escape ^G` is running with\n`^G` whatever the file says, which is the other half of why the\nthree above are left for the next run.\n\nThe names are the names in the file, and the file has always taken\nanother spelling of some of them -- `server` for `addr`, `profile`\nfor `agent`, `prefix` or `prefix_key` for `escape`.  Those still\nload, and `set` answers to them as well: `set server` prints the\nsetting under the name `addr`.\n\nA value a setting will not take is refused, with the setting named,\nand neither the shell nor the file is touched.  The same refusal\nhappens when the file is read at startup, where it stops slsh coming\nup rather than leaving somebody with a setting that silently does\nnothing.
set:section:What there is	set	section	What there is	`set` is the listing, and it is the one place they are all written\ndown, so this page does not copy it.  They fall into three:\n\n    addr, agent, escape             slsh itself\n    log, log_dir                    the transcript\n    viewer_*                        what "viewer --launch" starts\n    map_*                           how "map" draws its picture\n\nThe map's are the ones most worth knowing about, because the picture\nis drawn to the shape of the font it is being read in: `map_ratio`\nis how tall a character cell is against how wide, height first, and\n`7:3` is a measurement of one font rather than a fact about all of\nthem.  See `man map`, which says what each of the map's five does.
set:section:The transcript	set	section	The transcript	`log` is on to begin with, and what it keeps is what you saw: every\ncommand you ran, everything each one printed, everything heard and\neverything said.  One file per avatar, named after the avatar --\n`example-resident.log` -- in `$XDG_DATA_HOME/slgo`, or\n`~/.local/share/slgo` where that is not set.  `log_dir` puts it\nsomewhere else; empty is the default place.\n\n    2026-08-26 15:56:29 $ where\n    2026-08-26 15:56:29   Testville at 33, 70, 2001\n    2026-08-26 15:56:33 < [IM Example Resident] are you still at the build\n    2026-08-26 15:56:41 > [IM Example Resident] on my way\n\nA command is written down before it runs, so a command that hung is\nin the file that says what happened.  `set log off` stops it, and\nleaves the file where it is.\n\nControl characters are written the way the screen showed them, `^[`\nfor ESC and so on -- see `man chat` -- so that reading the file with\n`cat` or `tail` later does not hand a stranger's escape sequences to\nthat terminal instead.\n\nTwo things are deliberately not in it.  Output that was redirected --\n`ls > listing` -- is a listing you did not see, so the command is\nrecorded and the listing is not; it is in the file you sent it to. A\nman page read at a terminal goes through the pager rather than to the\nscreen a line at a time, and is not recorded either.\n\nWhat no shell can record is what happened while none was running.\nslgod stays logged in and goes on hearing, but a transcript is what\nsomebody saw, and there is nobody there to see it.
set:section:Measuring the font	set	section	Measuring the font	Some terminals will say, and slsh will ask them:\n\n    set map_ratio auto\n\nThat asks for the size of one character cell in pixels, and writes\nback whatever comes.  The numbers written are the ones reported, not\nreduced -- `18:10` rather than the `9:5` that means the same shape\n-- because the file is where the tweaking happens next and `18:9` is\none digit away from `18:10` in a way it is not away from `9:5`.\n\nWhat the terminal reports is the cell it hands the font, which is\nnot always the shape the letters look.  Take what `auto` writes as\nwhere to start, draw a region with `map --region`, and change the\nratio until a square region looks square -- that is the only test\nthat matters.\n\n`auto` needs a terminal to ask.  It is refused where there is none\n-- `slsh -c`, `slsh -f`, and a session with its input coming from a\npipe -- and refused again, naming what it tried, by a terminal that\nwill not answer.  Both of those refusals mean measuring it by hand,\nwhich is the same question sent the same way:\n\n    printf '\\033[16t'\n\nThe answer comes back as ESC [ 6 ; HEIGHT ; WIDTH t -- so `6;18;10`\nis a cell eighteen pixels tall and ten wide, and `18:10` is the\nratio to try.  A terminal that answers nothing at all to that is one\nthat does not know the question, and then it is by eye: start from\n`2:1`, draw a region, and go from there.\n\n`map_ratio` is the only setting that takes `auto`, and every other\none takes its value as it stands.  A word that might mean "work it\nout" in any row would be a word to check the meaning of in every\nrow.
set:example:5	set	example	Measuring the font	set map_ratio auto
set:section:Examples	set	section	Examples	    set\n    set map_span\n    set map_ratio 2:1\n    set map_ratio auto\n    set map_friend_colour bright cyan\n\nThe last of these cannot take until the next slsh, and says so:\n\n    set addr lab.local:7807\n    addr = lab.local:7807\n    written to /home/somebody/.config/slsh/config\n    this shell keeps the old value; the new one is for the next slsh\n\nSee also: `map` for what the map settings do to the picture,\n`viewer` for what the viewer settings start, and `chat` for the key\nthe escape setting names.
set:example:6	set	example	Examples	set
set:example:7	set	example	Examples	set map_span
set:example:8	set	example	Examples	set map_ratio 2:1
set:example:9	set	example	Examples	set map_friend_colour bright cyan
set:example:10	set	example	Examples	set addr lab.local:7807
sit	sit	line		sit [-w SECONDS] [NAME|UUID]\nsit on an object; with nothing named, sit on the ground\nhelp groups: region\nkeywords: sit down seat chair ground pose furniture
sit:intro	sit	intro		Sits the avatar down: on an object when one is named, and on the ground\nwhen nothing is.  It waits for the simulator to say what became of the\nrequest, and what it prints is what happened -- what the avatar is now\nsitting on, and where that left it.\n\n    sit                     on the ground, where the avatar is\n    sit greeter             on the object called greeter\n    sit d8467e57-...          on the object with that key
sit:example:1	sit	example		sit                     on the ground, where the avatar is
sit:example:2	sit	example		sit greeter             on the object called greeter
sit:example:3	sit	example		sit d8467e57-...          on the object with that key
sit:section:A sit moves the avatar	sit	section	A sit moves the avatar	This is the part to know before anything else.  A sit is not a request\nto sit on something already within reach: the simulator picks the\navatar up and puts it on the seat, over whatever is in the way, from\nseveral metres off.  A box seven metres away seats as readily as one\nhalf a metre away.  Eleven metres is refused.  A near thing is sat on\nand a far one is refused in words.\n\nStanding up afterwards does not undo the journey.  The avatar seated on\nthat box seven metres away is left about six metres from where it had\nbeen standing.  That is why a second line says where the avatar now is,\nin the words `where` and `tp` use:\n\n    sit greeter\n    sat on "greeter" d8467e57-... (local 8360)\n    Testville at 35, 72, 2001\n\nThe local id in that line is the simulator's answer rather than an echo\nof the question.  An object is a linkset, and which prim of one it\nseats an avatar on is its business.\n\nA ground sit moves nothing, so the position line after one says the\navatar is where it was.
sit:example:4	sit	example	A sit moves the avatar	sit greeter
sit:option:-w, --wait SECONDS	sit	option	-w, --wait SECONDS	How long to wait for the simulator to answer.  Without it, fifteen\nseconds.
sit:section:When the grid says no	sit	section	When the grid says no	A refusal arrives as an alert, and the grid's own words are quoted\nwhole:\n\n    sit "the long bench"\n    slsh: sit: sl: the simulator refused the sit: "No room to sit\n    here, try another spot."\n\nThose words are printed as they arrived.  They are not always\naccurate -- a key that names no object at all is refused by the grid\nwith a sentence about the region -- and they are still the only thing\nsaid.  A key this region has never described is refused here first, in\nthe same words `touch` and `take` use for it, before anything is sent.\n\nAn alert is a general channel, so something else alerting while a sit\nis in flight can be read as the answer to it.  That is arranged to fail\nthe safe way round: whether the avatar is seated is looked at first, so\nthe mistake this can make is a refusal that was somebody else's news,\nand never a sit reported as having worked when it did not.\n\nSilence is a third thing, neither a yes nor a no: the request may have\ntaken effect without this hearing about it.  The wait before it gives\nup is fifteen seconds.
sit:example:5	sit	example	When the grid says no	sit "the long bench"
sit:section:One object, and only one	sit	section	One object, and only one	An object is named by the word the region calls it or by its key, the\nway `touch` and `take` name one.
