A key is taken as itself.  A name is\nlooked up among the objects the region has described, and a name that\ntwo of them answer to is refused, with both keys, rather than guessed\nat.  Guessing costs more here than it does for `touch`: the wrong guess\nmoves the avatar.\n\nA name with a space in it is one argument, so quote it.  That is\nstricter than `tp` and `landmark`, which join the rest of the line.
sit:section:Examples	sit	section	Examples	    sit\n    sit greeter\n    sit "the long bench"\n    sit --wait 30 greeter\n\nSee also: `stand` for getting up again, `where` for the position this\nprints, `objects` for finding the thing to sit on, `touch` for clicking\nit instead, and `tp` for moving the avatar somewhere a sit cannot reach.
sit:example:6	sit	example	Examples	sit
sit:example:7	sit	example	Examples	sit --wait 30 greeter
source	source	line		source FILE\nrun the commands in a file\nalso called: .\nhelp groups: shell\nkeywords: run execute batch file of commands commands from file
source:intro	source	intro		`.` runs the commands in a file, one to a line, as though each had\nbeen typed at this prompt.  `source` is the same command under a\nsecond name.\n\n    . probes\n\nIt is the other half of redirection, and the two together are the\npoint of this shell being a shell: a listing written out with `>`,\nedited in whatever editor is to hand into a list of commands, and\nrun.  Blank lines are skipped and so is anything beginning with a\nhash, so an edited listing keeps its headings and its notes.\n\nEach line is a command line and nothing more.  There are no\nvariables, no loops and no conditionals, and a line may redirect its\nown output exactly as it would at the prompt.
source:example:1	source	example		. probes
source:section:It stops at the first failure	source	section	It stops at the first failure	The rest of the file is not run, and the report says which line\nfailed, what it said, and how many lines were left.\n\nA file of this kind usually begins by changing folder, and carrying\non after that failed would run every remaining line somewhere else\nentirely -- which, when the remaining lines are removals, is not a\nthing to discover afterwards.\n\nA file may run another, and one that is more than a few deep is\nrefused on the grounds that a file sourcing itself looks exactly\nlike this.
source:section:Running one without a prompt at all	source	section	Running one without a prompt at all	The shell will take a file on its own command line and run it\ninstead of reading keys -- `slsh -f FILE`, or `slsh --file FILE` --\nwhich is how this becomes something another program can drive.  A\nfailed line ends that run too, and ends it with a status that says\nso, rather than leaving whatever called it to read the output and\nguess.
source:section:Examples	source	section	Examples	Write a listing out, edit it into commands, and run it:\n\n    find probe > probes\n    . probes\n\nSee also: `echo` for putting a line into such a file, `quit` for\nending one that is meant to be the whole session, and `help all`,\nwhich is where the redirection is written down.
source:example:2	source	example	Examples	find probe > probes
stand	stand	line		stand [-w SECONDS]\nget up, from either kind of sit\nalso called: unsit\nhelp groups: region\nkeywords: stand up get up unsit leave seat
stand:intro	stand	intro		Gets the avatar up, from either kind of sit, and waits for the\nsimulator to show that it happened before saying so.  An avatar sits on\none thing at a time and the simulator knows which, so there is nothing\nhere for anybody to name.\n\n`unsit` is the same command under a second name.  `stand` is what the\nworld calls the button; `unsit` is what a script calls the function,\nand both spellings work so that neither has to be remembered.
stand:option:-w, --wait SECONDS	stand	option	-w, --wait SECONDS	How long to keep asking until the avatar is up.  Without it, fifteen\nseconds.
stand:section:It says where that left the avatar	stand	section	It says where that left the avatar	The second line is the position, in the words `where` and `tp` use.\nStanding up does not undo the journey the sit made.  An avatar seated\non a box seven metres away is left about six metres from where it had\nbeen standing.  This line is where the avatar is now, and not where it\nwas before any of it began.
stand:section:What counts as having stood up	stand	section	What counts as having stood up	From an object sit it is the un-parenting: the avatar's own update\ncomes back with no seat in it.  From a ground sit there is nothing to\nunsit from, so what says the avatar is up is the ground animation\nstopping -- and this shell hears animations only while one of these\ncommands is running, having asked for them and given them back.\n\nThere is a gap in that, and it is worth knowing rather than being\nsurprised by.  A ground sit this shell's session performed is known\nabout.  One performed by a viewer driving the same avatar, or by\nanother client while this session was not listening, is not, and reads\nhere as standing.  A `stand` then still sends the request -- the avatar\ndoes get up -- and returns at once, having had nothing to wait for.
stand:section:It may take a moment	stand	section	It may take a moment	A stand asked for while the avatar is still settling into the last\nthing it was told to do can be swallowed.  The command keeps asking\nuntil the avatar is up, or until fifteen seconds have passed.
stand:section:Examples	stand	section	Examples	    stand\n    unsit\n    stand --wait 30\n\nSee also: `sit` for going the other way, `where` for the position this\nprints, and `tp` for moving the avatar somewhere sitting cannot reach.
stand:example:1	stand	example	Examples	stand
stand:example:2	stand	example	Examples	unsit
stand:example:3	stand	example	Examples	stand --wait 30
start	start	line		start [-w SECONDS] OBJECT [SCRIPT]\nstart a script inside a rezzed object, or every script in it\nhelp groups: region\nkeywords: run running enable resume script turn on object
start:intro	start	intro		`start` sets a script inside a rezzed object running.  `stop` is the\nother direction, and the pair is the tick box a viewer's script editor\ncalls Running.  Neither has anything to say about inventory: a script\nin a folder does not run and cannot be made to, because an object is\nthe only place a script runs at all.\n\n    start lantern greeter\n\nThe object comes first and the script takes the rest of the line, as in\n`drop`, so an object whose name has a space in it has to be quoted and\na script whose name has one need not be.  The object is named by the\nword the region calls it or by its uuid.  With no script named, every\nscript in the object is started -- scripts only, since starting a\nnotecard is not a thing, and an object holding none says so.
start:example:1	start	example		start lantern greeter
start:option:-w, --wait SECONDS	start	option	-w, --wait SECONDS	How long to give the object to agree.  Without it, fifteen.
start:section:One line for every script, and no stopping early	start	section	One line for every script, and no stopping early	    started    greeter\n    already    listener\n    no answer  watcher\n\nAn object of six scripts is six separate requests and they need not\nagree with each other, so nothing stops at the first one that will not\nchange: every script is asked and every script gets its line, in the\ncolumns a listing uses with the name last.\n\n`already` is a line and not an error.  A script that is running when\nsomebody asks for it to run is in the state they wanted; saying\n`started` would be a claim about something that did not happen, and\nfailing would be a complaint about getting what was asked for.
start:example:2	start	example	One line for every script, and no stopping early	no answer  watcher
start:section:What `no answer` means	start	section	What `no answer` means	Nothing replies to the request that flips the switch, so what is waited\nfor is the object agreeing the script is running.  A script that never\nagreed is not a script that failed.  The request went out and may yet\nhave taken effect; running the same command again says what the object\nthinks now.  The count of those that did not agree is what the command\nfinally fails with, once the listing that explains it is already on the\nscreen -- or, where the object held one script, that script's name.\n\nOne kind of `no answer` never turns into anything else.  A script put\ninto an object with `drop` has been copied in and not compiled, so\nthere is no bytecode for the switch to turn on, and the object will not\nagree that it is running however long it is asked.  Nothing here can\nmend that, because the mending is to have put the script in with\n`new --in`, which compiles it.
start:section:Examples	start	section	Examples	    start lantern\n    start lantern greeter\n\nSee also: `stop`, `new` for putting a script into an object compiled\nand already running, `drop`, and `ls` for what an object holds.
start:example:3	start	example	Examples	start lantern
status	status	line		status\nhow this session and its circuit are doing\nhelp groups: avatars\nkeywords: connection circuit health network packets session statistics
status:intro	status	intro		`status` is about the plumbing rather than about the world: how the\ncircuit under this session is doing, and what has been crossing it.\n\n    status\n\nIt answers for the session this shell is attached to, and it is the\ndaemon's answer -- a shell that logged in for itself has nothing to\nask and says so.  The heading names the avatar, the profile it is\nhosted under in brackets after it, and the region -- in that order,\nwhich is the other way round from the `agents` listing, where the\nprofile is the first column.  Under it the simulator's own channel\nversion, the packet counters, how many clients the daemon has\nattached to this session, and how many capabilities the simulator\ngranted.
status:example:1	status	example		status
status:section:What the counters are for	status	section	What the counters are for	They say whether a session that looks idle is idle or broken.  A\nquiet region and a dead circuit look identical from the prompt:\nnothing is being printed either way.  Packets still arriving is a\nquiet region; packets going out and being resent and then abandoned\nis a circuit that has stopped being listened to at the far end.\nDuplicates are the region saying something twice, which is ordinary\nin small numbers.\n\nThe counters belong to the connection the session is on now.  A\nsession that dropped and was brought back is counting from the new\ncircuit, so small numbers under a session that has been up for hours\nare a reconnection rather than a quiet afternoon.
status:section:The messages with no handler	status	section	The messages with no handler	Anything listed under that heading arrived and this build had\nnothing to do with it, commonest first.  The list is long and that\nis ordinary: a region telling a client about sound and weather and\ntime produces a lot of messages nothing here has ever wanted.  A big\nnumber is not by itself a hole worth filling; what is worth reading\nis a name appearing that was not there yesterday.  An empty list\nprints nothing at all rather than an empty heading.
status:section:Examples	status	section	Examples	    status\n    status > status-before-the-run\n\nSee also: `watch` for the messages themselves as they arrive,\n`agents` for the other sessions, and `caps` for the capabilities\ncounted here.
status:example:2	status	example	Examples	status > status-before-the-run
stop	stop	line		stop [-w SECONDS] OBJECT [SCRIPT]\nstop a script inside a rezzed object, or every script in it\nhelp groups: region\nkeywords: halt pause disable turn off script not running object
stop:intro	stop	intro		`stop` takes a script inside a rezzed object out of the running\nstate.  `start` is the other direction, and the two of them are one\ntick box in a viewer's script editor rather than two operations.\n\n    stop lantern greeter\n\nThe object comes first and the script takes the rest of the line, as in\n`drop`, so an object whose name has a space in it has to be quoted and\na script whose name has one need not be.  With no script named, every\nscript in the object is stopped; an object holding none says so, and\n`ls --in` lists what it does hold.
stop:example:1	stop	example		stop lantern greeter
stop:option:-w, --wait SECONDS	stop	option	-w, --wait SECONDS	How long to give the object to agree.  Without it, fifteen.
stop:section:Stopping is not removing	stop	section	Stopping is not removing	A stopped script stays where it is, in the object's contents, and\nappears in the listing exactly as before.  Getting it out is\n`rm --in OBJECT`, and that copy is then gone: anything wanted back has\nto come from the original in inventory.
stop:section:The listing, and the answers in it	stop	section	The listing, and the answers in it	One line per script, in the columns a listing uses:\n\n    stopped    greeter\n    already    listener\n    no answer  watcher\n\nEvery script is asked, and nothing stops at the first one that will not\nchange, since the requests are separate and need not agree.  `already`\nis not an error -- a script that is not running when somebody asks for\nit to be stopped is in the state they wanted.\n\n`no answer` is not a failure either.  Nothing replies to the request\nitself, so the object is asked again until the wait runs out.  The\nrequest went out and may yet have taken effect, and running the command\nagain says what the object thinks now.  The count of those that did not\nagree is what the command fails with at the end, under the listing that\nexplains it -- or the one script's name, where the object held only one\nof them.
stop:example:2	stop	example	The listing, and the answers in it	no answer  watcher
stop:section:Examples	stop	section	Examples	    stop lantern\n    stop lantern greeter\n\nSee also: `start`, `ls` for what an object holds, `rm` for taking a\nscript out of it, and `new` for putting one in and starting it.
stop:example:3	stop	example	Examples	stop lantern
take	take	line		take [--copy] [--into FOLDER] [-w SECONDS] NAME|UUID\ntake a rezzed object into inventory; --copy tries to leave the original\nhelp groups: region\nkeywords: pick up take into inventory object ground world copy back up
take:intro	take	intro		`take` picks a rezzed object up off the ground and puts it into\ninventory.  It is the other direction of `place`, and the two of them\nare how an object is moved from one region to another, backed up, or\ncopied out of the world into a folder.  `drop` is a different\ndirection again: it puts an inventory item inside a rezzed object\nthat is still standing.\n\n    take probe\n\nThe object is named by the word the region calls it, or by its uuid,\nand a name with a space in it has to be quoted.  Names are not unique:\nwhere several objects answer to one word, `take` refuses and prints\ntheir uuids rather than picking, since taking the wrong one is not\nsomething that can be put back.  `objects TEXT` is the listing those\nuuids come from.\n\nAn avatar can only take what it may take, and a take it may not make is\nsilence rather than a refusal.  Nothing answers a take at all: the item\nis waited for in the folder it was to land in, so somebody else's\nobject produces no message from the region, and after forty seconds the\ncommand gives up saying only that the item never appeared.\n\nAn object with several prims comes in as one item -- a linkset is one\nobject, which is why `unlink` exists.
take:example:1	take	example		take probe
take:option:--copy	take	option	--copy	Take a copy and try to leave the original standing.  It works where\nthe object's permissions allow copying, and it is the one that can\nturn the region's silence into a sentence, because it has something\nto look at afterwards -- the object still standing where it was.  A\nbare take says the object `is in inventory`; this says it is there\n`and still where it was`.
take:option:--into FOLDER	take	option	--into FOLDER	The folder it lands in, as a path or as the folder's uuid.  Without\nit, a take lands in Objects, which is where the viewer puts one and\ntherefore where a person will look for it.
take:option:-w, --wait SECONDS	take	option	-w, --wait SECONDS	How long to let the region describe itself before giving up on finding\nthe object.  Without it, thirty.
take:section:The flags come before the name	take	section	The flags come before the name	Option parsing stops at the first argument that is not a flag, and on\nthis command that argument is the object, so anything after it is not\nread as an option at all.  `take probe --into experiments` takes probe\nand puts it in Objects: the two words after the name are dropped, the\nfolder is never looked at, and the report says the object is in\ninventory without saying where.  `take --into experiments probe` is the\nsame line with the flag in front of the name, and does what it says.\n\n`--copy` goes the same way and costs more.  After the name it is\nignored, so the object is taken rather than copied and the original is\ngone from the region -- and the line printed is the bare take's, which\nis the only sign that what happened was not what was asked for.
take:section:Taking loses where it stood	take	section	Taking loses where it stood	Second Life does not remember where an object was before it was taken,\nso nothing can put it back.  A round trip that has to end where it\nstarted has to write the position down first: `objects NAME` prints it,\nand `move` puts the object back afterwards.
take:section:Examples	take	section	Examples	Take one thing, by name:\n\n    take probe\n\nTake a copy and leave the original where it is, into a folder of its\nown:\n\n    take --copy --into Objects/experiments probe\n\nThe whole round trip, position and all:\n\n    objects probe\n    take probe\n    place Objects/probe\n    move probe 33 73 1000\n\nSee also: `place`, `move`, `objects`, `perms`, and `unlink` for an\nobject that should not have come in as one item.
take:example:2	take	example	Examples	take --copy --into Objects/experiments probe
take:example:3	take	example	Examples	objects probe
take:example:4	take	example	Examples	place Objects/probe
take:example:5	take	example	Examples	move probe 33 73 1000
talk	talk	line		talk\nthe conversations chat mode cycles between\nhelp groups: people\nkeywords: conversations list chats open sessions switch
talk:intro	talk	intro		talk lists the conversations, and marks the one a line typed in chat\nmode would go to.  Chat mode shows only the current one, in its prompt;\nthis is the whole set tab moves between.\n\n    talk\n\nIt takes nothing.  Open chat is always the first entry, called `Local`,\nand each person spoken to or heard from is another, in the order they\nappeared.  A star marks where typing would land.
talk:example:1	talk	example		talk
talk:section:The numbers are positions and nothing takes them	talk	section	The numbers are positions and nothing takes them	The number in each row is where that conversation sits in the cycle, so\nthat a listing of six can be counted rather than squinted at.  Nothing\naccepts it as an argument: the way to another conversation is tab in\nchat mode, or naming the person to `im` or `chat`.  A conversation that\nopened while this listing was on the screen has moved the numbers under\nit, which is the other reason not to type one.\n\nThe danger is that typing one is not refused.  A number given to `im`,\n`offer`, `give` or `profile` means the last listing of people -- what\n`who`, `friends` or `lookup` printed, or the several people a `profile`\nlisted rather than guess between -- and this is not one of those: it\nleaves that listing exactly as it was.  So `talk` and then `im 2`\nwrites to the second person of whatever was listed before, who has\nevery chance of being somebody else entirely, and the shell reports it\nas a success because nothing about it went wrong.  With no such listing\nat all it says there is no 2 in the last listing, which is the harmless\nhalf of the same thing.
talk:section:A conversation opens by itself and does not close	talk	section	A conversation opens by itself and does not close	The list grows when somebody sends an instant message, and a notice\nsays so at the moment it happens -- that notice is worth reading,\nbecause it means tab now has one more stop on it.  It grows quietly\nwhen this side starts one: `im WHO TEXT` that opens a conversation says\nso, but `chat WHO` and `im WHO` with nothing to say simply switch to a\nrow that was not there before.  Nothing removes an entry; the list is\nthe session's memory of who has been talked to, and it ends when the\nshell does.  `Local` cannot be closed either, which is what makes tab a\ncycle: pressing it enough times always comes back to speaking out loud.\n\nThe names can improve, but not by themselves.  An instant message\ncarries a key and sometimes no name at all, so a conversation can\nappear as the first eight characters of a key in brackets --\n`(d8467e57)`.  Nothing here then asks the grid who that is.  What\nrelabels it is a later message from that person that does carry a name,\nor a command naming them by key -- `chat d8467e57-...` asks the grid\nand puts the answer in the row.
talk:section:Examples	talk	section	Examples	    talk\n\nSee also: `chat` for entering the mode this describes, `im` for opening\na conversation with somebody, and `who` for everybody in the region,\nwhether or not there is a conversation with them.
texture	texture	line		texture [--alpha N] [--color R,G,B] [-f N] [--fullbright] [--glow N] [--id UUID] [--no-fullbright] [--offset S,T] [--repeats S,T] [--rot DEGREES] [--shiny N] NAME|UUID\nset what a face looks like, or with no flags say what it looks like\nhelp groups: region\nkeywords: face appearance colour color texture image repeats alpha glow shiny fullbright look change
texture:intro	texture	intro		`texture` is two commands wearing one name, and which one it is depends\non whether it was asked to change anything.  Given a flag that changes\nsomething it changes it; given none, it says what the faces look like\nnow.\n\n    texture lantern\n\nThe object is named by the word the region calls it or by its key.
texture:example:1	texture	example		texture lantern
texture:option:-f, --face N	texture	option	-f, --face N	Which face, counting as LSL does.  Without it, every face, which is\nwhat "make this thing red" usually means.  On its own it reports that\none face rather than changing anything.
texture:option:--id UUID	texture	option	--id UUID	The texture to put on a face.
texture:option:--repeats S,T	texture	option	--repeats S,T	How many times it tiles.
texture:option:--offset S,T	texture	option	--offset S,T	How far it slides, each from -1 to 1.
texture:option:--rot DEGREES	texture	option	--rot DEGREES	How far it turns.
texture:option:--color R,G,B	texture	option	--color R,G,B	The tint, each of R, G, B from 0 to 255.
texture:option:--alpha N	texture	option	--alpha N	Opacity, 0 clear to 255 solid.  Without it, the face is left as it is.
texture:option:--fullbright	texture	option	--fullbright	Ignore lighting.
texture:option:--no-fullbright	texture	option	--no-fullbright	Stop ignoring it.  The two together are refused.
texture:option:--shiny N	texture	option	--shiny N	Shininess, 0 none to 3 high.  Without it, the face is left as it is.
texture:option:--glow N	texture	option	--glow N	Glow, 0 to 255.  Without it, the face is left as it is.
texture:section:A change reads the object first	texture	section	A change reads the object first	The message that changes an appearance carries the whole of it -- every\nface, together -- so a change has to read what the object looks like\nnow and send it all back with one face altered.\n\nThe region never describes the result of a change on its own.  After a\nchange this session forgets the appearance it holds for that object, so\nthe next look asks the region again.  Two runs in a row then compose --\nevery face red and then face 2 green leaves five red faces and a green\none.\n\nSeveral changes to one object still belong in one command line, where\nthey are applied to one reading and sent once.  Everything is parsed\nbefore anything is sent, so a typo in the last flag refuses the lot\nrather than leaving the object half changed.
texture:section:What the report says	texture	section	What the report says	Faces that look alike are printed once, under all of their numbers,\nbecause that is how a prim usually is -- five sides of a box alike and\none different -- and six identical lines say less than one.\n\nHow many faces there are comes from the shape the region described: six\nfor a box, three for a cylinder, one for a sphere, and more where a cut\nor a hollow has opened new surfaces.  Where the shape is not known the\nanswer is the eight a prim can have at most.\n\nA prim nothing has described the appearance of is asked about first,\nand reads as plain white if the region does not answer within three\nseconds.  White is the truth about such a prim rather than a guess: an\nobject can be known to be there before anything has said what it looks\nlike.\n\nThe report reads what a change reads, so an object whose appearance was\ndropped by a change is described again before it is printed.  What it\nprints is what the region says now.
texture:section:Examples	texture	section	Examples	Ask what a thing looks like, then one face of it:\n\n    texture lantern\n    texture -f 2 lantern\n\nEverything meant for one object in one line:\n\n    texture --id d8467e57-... --glow 40 sign\n\nSee also: `dump`, which describes an object as JSON and leaves textures\nout of it deliberately, and `reform` for the rest of what a prim is.
texture:example:2	texture	example	Examples	texture -f 2 lantern
texture:example:3	texture	example	Examples	texture --id d8467e57-... --glow 40 sign
touch	touch	line		touch [--at X,Y,Z] [-f N] [-H SECONDS] [--move SECONDS] [--normal X,Y,Z] [--press SECONDS] [--rate N] [--st S,T] [--uv U,V] NAME|UUID [POINT ...]\nclick an object, on a named face at a named point\nhelp groups: region\nkeywords: click press use activate object drag button prim
touch:intro	touch	intro		`touch` clicks an object, the way a viewer's mouse does: a script sees\n`touch_start` and `touch_end` from the same messages a viewer would\nhave sent.  Given points and durations it becomes a drag instead, which\nis the general case a plain click is the smallest of.\n\n    touch greeter\n\nThe object is named by the word the region calls it, or by its key,\nand a name several objects answer to is refused rather than guessed\nat.  A bare click lands in the middle of face 0, pointing up, at the\nobject's own position.
touch:example:1	touch	example		touch greeter
touch:option:-f, --face N	touch	option	-f, --face N	Which face, counting as LSL does.  Without it, face 0.  `-1` is what a\nviewer sends when it hit the object but not a face of it.
touch:option:--uv U,V	touch	option	--uv U,V	Where on that face, each number from 0 to 1.  Without it, `0.5,0.5`.
touch:option:--st S,T	touch	option	--st S,T	The same point in the texture's own coordinates.  Without it, the\nmiddle of the texture.
touch:option:--at X,Y,Z	touch	option	--at X,Y,Z	The point in region coordinates, which is what `llDetectedTouchPos`\nreports.  Without it, the object's own position.
touch:option:--normal X,Y,Z	touch	option	--normal X,Y,Z	The direction the surface faces there.  Without it, `0,0,1`.
touch:option:--press SECONDS	touch	option	--press SECONDS	Hold still at the first point before moving.
touch:option:--move SECONDS	touch	option	--move SECONDS	How long the path takes.  Two points or more are refused without it.
touch:option:-H, --dwell SECONDS	touch	option	-H, --dwell SECONDS	Rest at the last point before letting go.
touch:option:--rate N	touch	option	--rate N	How finely a moving touch is sampled, not how many events a script\nsees: while a touch is held, the `touch` event fires 22.5 times a\nsecond however many updates go out.  Without it, 45.
touch:section:Where on the object a touch lands	touch	section	Where on the object a touch lands	A viewer decides what was clicked by casting a ray from the camera\nthrough the mouse, and then sends the answer: the object, the face,\nthe point of intersection, the surface normal, the texture\ncoordinates.  The simulator does no geometry of its own -- it hands\nthose numbers to the script as `llDetectedTouchFace`,\n`llDetectedTouchPos` and the rest.\n\nThat makes this more precise than a viewer rather than less.  A test\nthat has to touch the third face of a prim nine tenths of the way along\none edge does not have to place a camera and aim -- it says face 3 and\na point on it.  Nothing checks that these numbers describe a point\nreally on the object; the simulator does not check either.
touch:section:Points after the first are a drag	touch	section	Points after the first are a drag	Each further argument is another point, read on top of the one before\nit, so a drag across a single face names the face once.  Two numbers\nare a place on a face and three are a place in the region, which is\nwhat tells them apart without another flag; a leading number and a\ncolon names the face, so a drag can cross from one to another.  Two\npoints or more are refused without `--move`, since a path with no\nduration is a jump.\n\nNothing acknowledges a touch either: what to wait for is the script's\nown output rather than the line printed here.
touch:section:Examples	touch	section	Examples	    touch greeter\n    touch --face 2 --uv 0.9,0.5 sign\n    touch --press 2 --move 1.5 --dwell 0.5 sign 2:0.9,0.1\n\nSee also: `objects` for finding the thing to click, `texture` for its\nfaces, and `dump` for the scripts inside it.
touch:example:2	touch	example	Examples	touch --face 2 --uv 0.9,0.5 sign
touch:example:3	touch	example	Examples	touch --press 2 --move 1.5 --dwell 0.5 sign 2:0.9,0.1
tp	tp	line		tp [-w SECONDS] REGION [X Y Z] | X Y Z | home\nmove to another region by name, to a position: X Y Z here, outside the region for the next one along, ~N to move by, or home\nhelp groups: region\nkeywords: teleport travel go visit region position coordinates home jump
tp:intro	tp	intro		Moves the avatar: to a point in this region, to another region by\nname, or home.  It waits for the arrival rather than reporting the\nrequest.  What is printed afterwards is where the avatar actually\nended up, read back, and not the point that was asked for.\n\nThree numbers and nothing else -- X Y Z, in this region's metres -- is\na move to that position.  The word `home` on its own is this account's\nhome position.  Anything else is a region's name, joined with spaces so\nthat a name with one in it needs no quoting, and the last three words\nare the position when all three are numbers:\n\n    tp 128 128 25\n    tp home\n    tp Example Landing\n    tp Example Landing 33 73 1000.5\n\nA trailing comma on a number is ignored, so a position read off `where`\nor off the line above a map pastes back in as it stands: `tp 128, 128, 25`\nis the first of those three again.
tp:example:1	tp	example		tp 128 128 25
tp:example:2	tp	example		tp home
tp:example:3	tp	example		tp Example Landing
tp:example:4	tp	example		tp Example Landing 33 73 1000.5
tp:option:-w, --wait SECONDS	tp	option	-w, --wait SECONDS	How long to wait for the avatar to arrive.  Without it, thirty\nseconds.
tp:section:Home	tp	section	Home	`tp home` goes to wherever this account's home is set.  It is the one\ndestination that is not typed out: the map is asked nothing, no name is\nlooked up and no position is computed, because the grid keeps home\nitself and the whole of what goes out is "home".  It is\n`landmark --home` under a shorter name and behaves identically,\nincluding the wait.\n\nThe word is matched without regard to case, as every other name this\nshell matches is, so `tp HOME` is the same trip.\n\nGoing home while standing at home is refused: the grid will not shorten\na teleport that arrives where it started, and says `CouldntTPCloser`.\nThe line says what it usually means.\n\nThe bare word is home, and only the bare word.  A region really called\n"home" is still reached in the forms that carry more than one word --\n`tp home 128 128 25` is that region, as it always was.  On 2026-09-01\nthe map had nine regions whose names begin with "home" and none called\nexactly that, so nothing is out of reach today; a region named tomorrow\nwould be reached with a position after it.\n\nWhere home is cannot be read from here, and going there is the only way\nto find out.  `landmark --set-home` is what puts it somewhere else: it\nsets home to where the avatar is standing, so the way to move home is\nto go there and then set it.
tp:section:A position outside this region is the region it really is in	tp	section	A position outside this region is the region it really is in	A region is 256 metres square and the grid is regions laid edge to\nedge, so x of 300 is not an error and not a point in this region: it is\n44 metres into the region to the east.  x of -10 is 246 metres into the\none to the west.  The shell works out which region owns the point and\nteleports there, the way the 25th hour of Monday is one in the morning\non Tuesday.\n\n    tp 300 128 25          44 metres into the region east of here\n    tp -10 128 25          246 metres into the region west of here\n    tp 128 -1 25           255 metres into the region to the south\n\nIt generalises as far as the arithmetic does.  x of 1000 is three\nregions east and 232 metres in.  The line printed before the waiting\nnames the region by its square on the grid rather than by name; what is\nprinted after the arrival is the name, read back with the position.\n\nIf nothing is standing on that square the grid answers `no_host`.  A\nposition that would be off the edge of the grid altogether is refused\nhere instead.
tp:example:5	tp	example	A position outside this region is the region it really is in	tp 300 128 25          44 metres into the region east of here
tp:example:6	tp	example	A position outside this region is the region it really is in	tp -10 128 25          246 metres into the region west of here
tp:example:7	tp	example	A position outside this region is the region it really is in	tp 128 -1 25           255 metres into the region to the south
tp:section:~ moves by, rather than to	tp	section	~ moves by, rather than to	A coordinate written with a leading `~` is measured from where the\navatar is standing rather than from the region's corner.  `~` on its\nown is that axis left alone, `~10` is ten metres further along it, and\n`~-10` is ten metres back:\n\n    tp ~10 ~ ~             ten metres east, same y, same height\n    tp ~ ~ ~50             fifty metres up, without moving\n    tp 128 128 ~           the middle of the region, same height\n    tp ~-5 ~-5 ~           five metres back on both axes\n\nIt is per axis, which is why it is a mark on the number and not an\noption: an option would make all three relative or none.  A bare `-10`\nalready means ten metres west of this region's corner -- a real place\nin the region next door -- so a leading minus cannot also mean "ten\nmetres back".\n\nRelative and outside-the-region compose: from x of 100, `tp ~300 ~ ~`\nis x of 400, which is 144 metres into the region to the east.\n\nTwo of the same relative teleport go twice as far, where two of the\nsame absolute one go nowhere the second time.  That is the point of it.\n\nIt is measured from where the avatar is at the moment the command runs.\nAn avatar that has just walked off a platform, or fallen, is moved from\nthere.  An absolute height is the way back.\n\n`~` cannot be given with the name of another region: the avatar is not\nstanding in that region, so there is nothing for it to be measured\nfrom.  A position given with a region name is that region's own, and is\nnot read as the next region along.\n\nA name with no position arrives in the middle of the region, at 128,\n128.  The height asked for is nothing, which the simulator reads as\nground level -- and ground level in the middle of a region is sometimes\nunder water.  Name a height to land somewhere else.
tp:example:8	tp	example	~ moves by, rather than to	tp ~10 ~ ~             ten metres east, same y, same height
tp:example:9	tp	example	~ moves by, rather than to	tp ~ ~ ~50             fifty metres up, without moving
tp:example:10	tp	example	~ moves by, rather than to	tp 128 128 ~           the middle of the region, same height
tp:example:11	tp	example	~ moves by, rather than to	tp ~-5 ~-5 ~           five metres back on both axes
tp:section:Several regions can answer to one name	tp	section	Several regions can answer to one name	The map's search matches from the start of a name and ignores case, so\n`Example Landing` comes back beside Example Landing North and everything\nelse beginning with those words.  A name that matches one of them\nexactly is that one, even when longer names shared its prefix.  Anything\nelse that matched more than once is printed, in the listing `regions`\nprints, and refused.\n\nRefused rather than guessed at, because the guess is not a listing to\nread again: it is an avatar somewhere it was not sent.  Type the name\nin full, or run `regions` first to see what the name catches.\n\nTwo regions may share a name outright.  There is no more of the name to\ntype then, and the refusal says so.
tp:section:How long it waits	tp	section	How long it waits	A teleport to another region prints a line before the waiting starts,\nbecause a shell that has silently stopped answering is\nindistinguishable from one that has hung.  A move inside this region\nprints no such line: the first thing it prints is the position read\nback after the arrival.\n\nThe wait is thirty seconds and covers the arrival, in this region or\nanother one, rather than the map lookup in front of it.\n\nEverything the shell knew about the old region is dropped on arrival:\nthe objects, who owns them, what they are called, what is inside them.\nWhat survives is what was never a region's to say -- who people are,\ninventory, friends, the offers and dialogs still waiting.\n\nA line of notice says the avatar is now somewhere else.  It appears\nwhoever asked for the move, alongside this command's own answer.  The\ntwo say different things: one is the session's news, the other is the\nanswer to what was typed.
tp:section:When the grid says no	tp	section	When the grid says no	A refusal is the grid's, and it arrives in two voices: a key a program\ncould act on and a sentence meant for a person.  Both are printed where\nthey differ.  Where the grid puts the same word in both places the line\ncarries it once, which is what a handle that is no region does: the\nrefusal ends in `no_host` and says it once, not twice.  An estate can\nrefuse an arrival for reasons the map has no field for, so a region the\nmap listed is not a region that will have this avatar.\n\nThe session is left standing where it was.\n\nSilence is the third answer.  Once this avatar has been handed to\nanother simulator, the one it left answers no further teleport request\nat all, so a teleport asked for while one is under way runs out its\nwait and says so.
tp:section:Landing near, rather than exactly	tp	section	Landing near, rather than exactly	The height is the simulator's to settle.  The avatar arrives at\nwhichever is higher of the height asked for and the ground under the\npoint, plus about a metre.  Asking for nothing is asking for ground\nlevel rather than for a fall.  The line printed afterwards is the\nposition to believe -- with the one exception `where` explains: above\n1020 metres a read-back is the height the arrival stated, not the\nheight the avatar may since have fallen or settled to.\n\nA teleport within a region that is refused -- a parcel that will not\nhave the avatar -- produces no error and no reply of any kind.  That is\nwhy this waits and reads back: without it a refusal would be\nindistinguishable from success.
tp:section:A name that ends in three numbers cannot be typed here	tp	section	A name that ends in three numbers cannot be typed here	The last three words are the position when all three of them are\nnumbers, so a region actually called something ending in three numbers\nis out of reach, and so is one whose whole name is numbers.\n\nA line that is nothing but numbers and is not three of them is a\nposition typed short, and is refused as one rather than sent to the map\nas a name.
tp:section:A negative coordinate	tp	section	A negative coordinate	`tp -10 128 25` begins with something that looks like an option, so the\nshell puts an end-of-options mark in front of the position.  It goes\nthere only where the options have not ended already, which is the\nposition typed on its own and the position after a flag:\n`tp --wait 60 -10 128 25` has its flag read as one and its position\nread as a position.  A region's name ends the options where it stands,\nso `tp Example Landing -10 128 25` needs no mark and gets none; one put\nthere would be joined onto the name.
tp:section:Examples	tp	section	Examples	    tp 128 128 25\n    tp ~10 ~ ~\n    tp 300 128 25\n    tp Example Landing\n    tp Example Landing 128 128 2001\n    tp --wait 60 Example Shallows\n    tp home\n\n`tp` moves this avatar and nobody else.  Offering a teleport to\nsomebody else is `lure`.\n\nSee also: `regions` for finding out what a name matches and where it is,\n`where` for the position this is given in, `look` for what the region\nunderfoot says about itself, `neighbours` for walking over a border\ninto the next region rather than teleporting across the grid, `move`\nfor shifting an object rather than the avatar, `landmark` for a place\nsaid by name in inventory and for setting where home is, and `answer`\nfor accepting a teleport somebody else offered.
tp:example:12	tp	example	Examples	tp ~10 ~ ~
tp:example:13	tp	example	Examples	tp 300 128 25
tp:example:14	tp	example	Examples	tp Example Landing 128 128 2001
tp:example:15	tp	example	Examples	tp --wait 60 Example Shallows
unlink	unlink	line		unlink [-w SECONDS] NAME|UUID\ntake a linked object apart; naming one prim of it frees only that prim\nhelp groups: region\nkeywords: separate split break apart linkset unjoin prim
unlink:intro	unlink	intro		`unlink` takes a linked object apart.  It is the way back from `link`,\nand the reason `take` is not the whole answer to an object that should\nnever have been one thing: a linkset comes into inventory as a single\nitem, so the taking apart happens in the world.\n\n    unlink garden chair\n\nIt names one thing, and its arguments are joined back into one name, so\n`unlink a lamp` means what it looks like.  A key may be given instead,\nwhich is the way past a name several objects answer to.
unlink:example:1	unlink	example		unlink garden chair
unlink:option:-w, --wait SECONDS	unlink	option	-w, --wait SECONDS	How long to let the region describe itself.  Without it, thirty.
unlink:section:A root means all of it; a prim means that prim	unlink	section	A root means all of it; a prim means that prim	Naming the root takes the whole object apart.  Naming one prim of it\nfrees that prim and leaves the rest linked.\n\nThat is the viewer's pair of behaviours -- clicking an object selects\nthe linkset and Unlink frees all of it, while editing linked parts\nselects one prim and Unlink frees only that.  The root is never among\nthe prims being freed: it has no parent to lose, so naming it frees\nnothing.\n\nNaming a child is a real thing to want rather than a mistake to guard\nagainst.  A prim of a linkset has its own name, `objects -c` prints\nthem, and a linkset is otherwise all or nothing.
unlink:section:What the pieces are called afterwards	unlink	section	What the pieces are called afterwards	A linkset answers to its root's name, so a name means a four-prim thing\nwhile the four prims are linked and one prim afterwards.  The pieces\nkeep the names they had inside it, which for anything built by hand is\noften `Object` for every one of them -- so an unlink can leave several\nthings in the region answering to a single word, and every command that\ntakes a name refuses an ambiguous one rather than picking.\n\nThat is why this lists the pieces with their keys instead of printing\na line saying it worked.  The root is in that listing with the rest,\nalthough it was never freed: what is wanted afterwards is a handle on\neach of the things now standing there, and the root is one of\nthem.  The keys are the only handle certain to work, and the moment\nthey are wanted is the moment the object comes apart.  Where two\npieces share a name it says so as well.\n\nFreeing one prim out of a set that stays a set is the exception, and\nis one line rather than a listing: only one piece came out, and what\nis left is still one object under its root, so the line names the prim\nthat left, the object it left, and how many prims that object has\nnow.  The keys are in it all the same.
unlink:section:Examples	unlink	section	Examples	    unlink garden chair\n    unlink d8467e57-...\n\nSee also: `link`, `objects` and its `-c` for the prims inside an\nobject, `take`, and `dump` for writing a linkset down before disturbing\nit.
unlink:example:2	unlink	example	Examples	unlink d8467e57-...
viewer	viewer	line		viewer [-l]\nwhere a real viewer can take this session over, and start one\nhelp groups: avatars\nkeywords: viewer client graphics eyes see avatar login password take over
viewer:intro	viewer	intro		`viewer` hands a running session to a real viewer: it says where the\ndaemon serves viewer logins and whether one has taken this session.\nIt is how to get eyes on an avatar the shell drives without logging\nthat avatar out and in again elsewhere.\n\n    viewer\n\nA shell that logged in for itself has no daemon serving viewer\nlogins, so there is nothing for it to hand over and it says so.\n\nServing viewer logins is not the daemon's default.  One that serves\nnone says so in words, and names the flag it would have to be\nrestarted with, rather than printing an empty address nobody could\ntell from a working one.\n\nWhether a viewer is there is only half known: one that takes the\nsession announces itself, one that quits says nothing, so that line\nis the last thing that happened rather than what is happening now.
viewer:example:1	viewer	example		viewer
viewer:option:-l, --launch	viewer	option	-l, --launch	Start a viewer and log it in as this avatar.  Without it, the address\nis printed and nothing is started.
viewer:section:The password is minted, used once, and expires	viewer	section	The password is minted, used once, and expires	A profile keeps only a digest of a password, so nothing on this side\ncan know one a viewer could be told to type.  The daemon mints a\nfresh one, good for a single login and for a few minutes, and it\ngoes straight to the viewer being started.  Nothing here prints,\nlogs or keeps it; the command line is echoed with it struck out,\nsince a launch that appears to do nothing is unreadable.\n\nA profile with no `viewer_password` line in it is refused rather\nthan minted for.  That line is not one a profile has unless somebody\nput it there: it is what marks a profile as one that may be handed\nto a viewer at all.  The refusal names the line to add and says\nslgod has to be restarted after it.\n\nIt does reach that viewer's arguments, where any process this user\nowns can read them.  Single use is the answer to it: an onlooker who\ncopies the password out is racing a viewer already logging in with\nit, and loses the moment it does.  The expiry is the second line of\ndefence, and generous, because a viewer takes the better part of a\nminute to reach a login screen.
viewer:section:The viewer has to be one that can be told about another grid	viewer	section	The viewer has to be one that can be told about another grid	A viewer built for the main grid may have the part that reads its\nown list of grids compiled out, and then cannot be pointed at a\nprivate grid at all: it falls back to a main grid login screen, with\nnothing on it to say why.  The build to have is the one made for\nOpenSim grids, which does read that list.\n\nThe grid has to be added to the viewer once, by hand, before any of\nthis works: its preferences, the login URI this command prints, and\na nickname.  What a viewer takes on its command line is that\nnickname and not a URI; handed the URI it answers `unknown grid` and\ngoes to the main grid again.\n\nFour settings carry the local half: `viewer_app` names the\napplication, `viewer_grid` that nickname, `viewer_launch` the\ncommand that starts it, and `viewer_running` the command that says\nwhether one is up already.  `set` is where they are changed.\n\nA viewer already running is refused rather than launched: raising an\napplication that is up does not pass it the arguments again, so a\nsecond launch would log nobody in and report success all the same.
viewer:section:Examples	viewer	section	Examples	    viewer\n    viewer --launch\n\nSee also: `agents`, `status`, `set` for the viewer settings, and\n`logout` for the other way to free an avatar up.
viewer:example:2	viewer	example	Examples	viewer --launch
waiting	waiting	line		waiting [-a]\nwhat is waiting for an answer: teleports, dialogs, offers, permissions, invitations; -a includes ignored\nhelp groups: waiting\nkeywords: pending requests notifications dialogs teleport offers permissions invitations queue
waiting:intro	waiting	intro		`waiting` lists everything that wants an answer, numbered.\n\n    waiting\n\nSeven kinds arrive that way and they share one list: a teleport, a\nscript asking permission, a blue menu, a text box, an item being\nhanded over, an offer of friendship, an invitation into a group.  A\nviewer draws these on the screen until somebody deals with them.  A\nshell has no screen to leave them on, so they are gathered here, the\nprompt says how many there are, and nothing is answered by being\nlooked at.\n\nEach line is the number, the kind -- dialog, text box, teleport,\ninventory, friendship, permission, group -- then who is asking and\nwhat for.  The line under it says what may be typed: the buttons a\ndialog offers, the two shapes a text box takes, the fee an invitation\nwants, the note that taking a teleport waits for the arrival, or the\nplain `answer`, `no`, `ignore`.\n\nA group invitation may cost money, so its fee has to be typed back.  A\nteleport offer is followed rather than answered and forgotten:\naccepting one moves the avatar and waits for it to arrive, and\neverything this shell knew about the region left behind is dropped on\nthe way.
waiting:example:1	waiting	example		waiting
waiting:option:-a, --all	waiting	option	-a, --all	Include the things `ignore` has set aside.  Without it they are not\nlisted and not counted at the prompt, which is what `ignore` is for.\nIf everything waiting has been ignored, the listing says so and points\nat `-a`.
waiting:section:The number belongs to the thing	waiting	section	The number belongs to the thing	A number is handed out when a thing is first seen and kept until it\ngoes, so a number on the screen still means the same thing after\nsomething else has been answered.  Numbering by position looks\nidentical until the moment it matters -- answer the first of two and\nthe second becomes 1, and the `answer 2` already decided on is now\neither an error or, worse, something else.  The list can therefore\nlook sparse: the thing that was 2 is still 2.  When nothing is left\nwaiting the numbers start again at one.\n\nEvery kind leaves the listing as it is answered, whichever command\nanswered it, so the count at the prompt falls by one.
waiting:section:What the listing is a record of	waiting	section	What the listing is a record of	Everything this shell has seen arrive, and -- through slgod -- the\noffers that arrived before it attached.\n\nslgod keeps five of the seven kinds whether or not any client is\nattached: teleports, requests for a teleport, items, friendship and\ngroup invitations.  A shell is handed whatever of those is still\nwaiting when it attaches, and they are listed with the rest, marked\n`(from before this shell)` because nobody saw them arrive.  Starting a\nshell says how many there are.\n\nDialogs and permission requests are not kept.  One raised before this\nshell attached is not known here; it is waiting in the world, and a\nviewer would show it.\n\nSo an empty listing says what it is an account of, rather than\n`nothing waiting` alone, which reads as an answer:\n\n    nothing waiting -- slgod has kept every offer, teleport and invitation\n    made to this avatar since 09:14 and holds none unanswered; a script's\n    dialog or permission request from before this shell attached is not kept\n\nslgod's record starts when it logged the avatar in, and keeps the\nhundred most recent; when it has had to drop older ones, the line says\nhow many.  Against a slgod too old to keep a record it says anything\noffered before the shell attached is not known here, and a shell that\nlogged in with `--direct` has seen everything since its own login.
waiting:section:Another shell, or slbotd, on the same avatar	waiting	section	Another shell, or slbotd, on the same avatar	Every client attached to the avatar is handed the same offers, and the\nfirst to answer one is the only one that does.  When another client\nanswers something this shell is listing, it leaves the listing at once\nand a line says so:\n\n    12:03:04 * the teleport Example Resident offered was accepted by slbotd -- it is no longer waiting\n\nAnswering one that another client answered a moment before is refused\nrather than sent twice, and says who answered it and how.
waiting:section:Examples	waiting	section	Examples	    waiting\n    waiting -a\n\nSee also: `answer`, `no`, `ignore`, `offers` for the older way to the\nfriendship and inventory offers, which appear here too, and `tp` for\ngoing somewhere without waiting to be invited.
waiting:example:2	waiting	example	Examples	waiting -a
watch	watch	line		watch [-t D] [NAME...]\nprint grid messages as they arrive; no NAME means everything\nhelp groups: avatars\nkeywords: messages packets protocol debug monitor traffic log
watch:intro	watch	intro		`watch` prints grid messages as they arrive, decoded as far as this\nbuild can decode them.  Where `status` counts what has crossed the\ncircuit, this shows it.\n\n    watch ChatFromSimulator\n\nThe arguments are message names as the protocol spells them --\n`ChatFromSimulator`, `ObjectUpdate` -- and naming none means\neverything.  A name this build has never heard of is subscribed to\nrather than refused, since the daemon may know a message the shell\ndoes not, and nothing arriving under it is then the answer.\n\nIt runs for half a minute, and prints how many messages it saw.\n\nA shell that logged in for itself has no daemon to relay from, so\nthis is one of the few things such a shell cannot do at all and it\nsays so rather than showing an empty watch.
watch:example:1	watch	example		watch ChatFromSimulator
watch:option:-t, --for D	watch	option	-t, --for D	How long to watch.  Without it, half a minute.  *D* is a length of\ntime and not a number of seconds, so it has to carry a unit:\n`-t 30s`, `-t 5m`.  A bare `-t 30` is refused with\n`time: missing unit in duration`.
watch:section:What will not appear	watch	section	What will not appear	Only what comes over the circuit.  A good deal now arrives on the\ngrid's event queue instead -- LLSD bodies rather than the binary\nmessage encoding -- and `watch` reads only the message half of it.\nA name that has moved to the queue subscribes cleanly and still\nshows nothing.\n\nA message the template does not describe is still reported, as its\nnumber and a byte count, and one that will not decode is reported as\nundecodable.  Both are worth seeing: an unknown number is what a\nprotocol change looks like from here, and silence would hide it.
watch:section:Examples	watch	section	Examples	    watch ChatFromSimulator\n    watch -t 5m ObjectUpdate ImprovedTerseObjectUpdate > updates\n\nSee also: `status`, and `agents` for which session is being watched.
watch:example:2	watch	example	Examples	watch -t 5m ObjectUpdate ImprovedTerseObjectUpdate > updates
wear	wear	line		wear [--at POINT] [--replace] PATH|UUID\nput an inventory object on, alongside whatever is already on that point\nhelp groups: region\nkeywords: wear put on attach clothing outfit accessory hud dress item
wear:intro	wear	intro		`wear` puts an inventory object on the avatar.  The argument is an\ninventory path, relative to the folder the shell is in, or the uuid of\nan item -- the same sort of argument `place` takes.\n\n    wear Objects/badge\n\nThe report names the point the region gave back rather than one guessed\nhere, so nobody has to know where a HUD asked to land.
wear:example:1	wear	example		wear Objects/badge
wear:option:--at POINT	wear	option	--at POINT	Where to wear it.  Without it, wherever the object itself says, which\nis the point its maker set.\n\nThe point may be the way `worn` prints it (`left hand`, `HUD top\nright`), the way the viewer names it on its own menu (`Skull`, `R Upper\nArm`, `Top Right`), or its number.  Case and surrounding space do not\nmatter, and the American `center` is taken for `centre`.  The numbers\nrun from 1 to 127; `0` asks for wherever the object itself says, the\nsame as leaving `--at` off.  A word that is neither a printed name nor\na viewer name -- `elbow` -- is refused rather than guessed at.\n\nOnly the printing differs from the viewer.  `R Upper Arm` is written\nout as `right upper arm`, HUD points are said to be HUD ones rather\nthan left as the viewer's bare `Top` and `Center`, and three are\ndifferent words: the point the viewer calls `skull` is `head` here,\n`spine` is `back` and `stomach` is `belly`.  `--at` understands both\nspellings.
wear:option:--replace	wear	option	--replace	Take off whatever is on that point, instead of wearing alongside it.\nA bare `wear` adds.  A wrong add is visible and costs a `detach`.  A\nwrong replace is invisible and costs whatever was there.\n\nThe report names what it displaced -- `X is worn on chest; Y came\noff` -- by looking afterwards rather than by predicting, because a\nreplace displaces one attachment and not the whole point's worth of\nthem.  Where the clause is missing, either nothing came off or the\nregion has not caught up; the line is never a guess.
wear:section:The same item twice is refused	wear	section	The same item twice is refused	Adding makes it possible to wear one inventory item twice, and `wear`\nwill not.  An attachment is known here by the item it came from, so two\nattachments from one item agree in every field a person could name one\nby: `detach` would find both and refuse as ambiguous.  The refusal\nnames the point the item is already on, and `--replace` and `detach`\nare the two ways on from there.\n\nA folder is refused: `wear` takes one object.
wear:section:Clothing and body parts	wear	section	Clothing and body parts	A shirt, a skin, a shape, a pair of eyes or a hair base is a system\nwearable rather than an object.  `wear` takes one all the same and\nworks out which it is from the item.\n\nIt does not attach.  A wearable is worn by putting a link to it in the\nCurrent Outfit folder -- which is the grid's own record of what an\navatar has on, and what the baking service reads -- and then asking the\nregion to rebuild the appearance.  The report says the slot rather than\na point, because there is no point:\n\n    wear Clothing/a blue shirt\n    a blue shirt is worn as shirt\n\nThe slot is what a person is choosing between when they have four\nshirts and can wear one of each layer.  It is `shape`, `skin`, `hair`,\n`eyes`, `shirt`, `pants`, `shoes`, `socks`, `jacket`, `gloves`,\n`undershirt`, `underpants`, `skirt`, `alpha`, `tattoo`, `physics` or\n`universal`.\n\n**A body part always replaces.**  There is no such thing as an avatar\nwearing two skins, so a bare `wear` of one is a replace however it is\nworded, `--replace` or not, and the line says what came off.  Clothing\nlayers, so it adds unless `--replace` is given -- the same default, and\nthe same reason, as wearing an object.\n\n`detach` takes clothing off again.  It will not take off a body part:\nan avatar is never without a shape, a skin, hair or eyes, so the way\nout of one is to wear another.  The viewer draws the same line without\nsaying so, by offering Take Off for clothing and not for a body part.
wear:example:2	wear	example	Clothing and body parts	wear Clothing/a blue shirt
wear:section:The Current Outfit folder	wear	section	The Current Outfit folder	Everything this command does to a wearable happens in that folder, and\n`ls -L "/Current Outfit"` is how to read it: one line per worn thing,\nclothing and attachments alike, with the links followed.\n\nAttachments go in it too.  An object put on here is linked into the\nfolder, and taken out of it by `detach`, so that what is worn now is\nwhat `dress` and `slbotd` put back after the next login.  A viewer\nwrites the folder for both kinds in the same way.\n\nEvery change to the folder is followed by a request to rebake the\navatar, as a viewer makes one.  For clothing that is what makes the\nchange visible.  For an attachment it is what brings the simulator's\nown list of what is attached up to date -- the list `worn` and `dress`\ncheck against.  A rebake that fails does not undo the wearing, and is\nreported on the same line.
wear:section:Examples	wear	section	Examples	Put a HUD on wherever it asks to go:\n\n    wear Objects/badge\n\nPut one on a named point, over whatever is there:\n\n    wear --replace --at "HUD bottom right" Objects/meter\n\nSee also: `detach`, `worn` for what is on and where, and `place` for\nputting an inventory object into the world instead of onto the avatar.
wear:example:3	wear	example	Examples	wear --replace --at "HUD bottom right" Objects/meter
where	where	line		where\nthe region and position this avatar is at\nhelp groups: region\nkeywords: location position coordinates current region am sitting
where:intro	where	intro		The region, the position in it, what the avatar is sitting on if it is\nsitting on anything, and which group it is acting as.\n\n    where\n    Testville at 33, 73, 1000\n    sitting on "a bench" 45557e57-... (local 8360)\n    acting as group Example Builders (d8467e57-...)\n\nThe position is in the region's own metres, to the metre -- the same\nnumbers `tp` and `move` take, and the same numbers `place --at` takes.
where:example:1	where	example		where
where:section:How exact it is	where	section	How exact it is	Exact on arriving in a region and after a teleport.  While the avatar\nwalks, x and y stay good to the metre and the height is in steps of\nabout four metres.\n\nAbove 1020 metres the height stops updating.  The printed Z is then\nwhere the avatar was put -- the arrival or the last teleport -- and not\nwhere it has settled or fallen since.  `landmark --make` is the way to\nread a height up there; x and y stay good the whole time.
where:section:The sitting line	where	section	The sitting line	It is there when the avatar is sitting on something and missing when it\nis not, and it is in the same shape `sit` prints, since it is the same\nfact.\n\nIt belongs on this listing because a seated avatar's position is not\nsomewhere it walked to.  A sit picks the avatar up and carries it to\nthe seat from as much as ten metres off, so the coordinates on the line\nabove are the seat's doing, and this is what explains them.\n\nThe answer is the region's rather than this session's memory: an avatar\nsitting on something is PARENTED to it, and the parent is part of how\nthe region describes the avatar.  So a shell that attached a minute ago\ngets the same answer as one that was connected when the sit happened.\nIt used to get "not sitting", because an avatar sitting still since\nbefore we attached had never been described to us.\n\nA sit on the GROUND is different and is reported only when this session\nperformed it.  Nothing is parented in a ground sit -- there is nothing\nto be parented to -- and what says it is happening is an animation,\nwhich this session hears only while it is waiting on one.
where:section:The group line	where	section	The group line	A second line names the active group and its key.  It is missing when\nthe avatar is acting as none, which is how a headless login starts.\n\nThat line is the usual answer when building is refused.  A parcel often\ngrants "create objects" to a group rather than to a person, and the\nrefusal talks about the land rather than the group.  The name says\nwhether this is the group the land wants; the key is what `group` takes.
where:section:Examples	where	section	Examples	    where\n\nSee also: `parcel` for which piece of the region this position is on,\n`look`, `who`, `map` for this position and everybody else's drawn as a\npicture, `objects`, `tp` for moving the avatar, `regions` for a region\nthe avatar is not in, and `group` for setting the one this reports.
who	who	line		who\nwho else is in the region, nearest first\nhelp groups: people region\nkeywords: nearby people avatars around near me close region list others distance
who:intro	who	intro		who lists the other avatars in the region, nearest first.  It is the\npeople half of what `objects` does for the furniture: `where` says\nwhere this avatar is standing, `look` describes the region, and this\nnames who else is in it.\n\n    who\n\nIt takes no argument.  Each line is a number, the name, the distance in\nmetres and the key.  The distance is a straight line through all three\naxes rather than a distance across the ground, so somebody a hundred\nmetres overhead is a hundred metres away and not the nearest person in\nthe room.  This avatar is not in its own listing.  When nobody else is\nin range, it says so.
who:example:1	who	example		who
who:section:The number down the left is meant to be typed	who	section	The number down the left is meant to be typed	It is not decoration.  Every command that names a person -- `im`,\n`chat`, `give`, `offer` -- takes a number in place of a name, and it\nmeans whoever was on that line of the last listing this shell printed.\n\nThat makes it the shell's count rather than the region's.  A second run\nrenumbers everybody, since somebody who has walked closer changes\nplaces; and a listing from some other command -- `friends`, `lookup` --\nreplaces it, so the number to use is always the one from the listing\nstill on the screen.
who:section:A name that has not arrived yet	who	section	A name that has not arrived yet	An avatar's name is only had by asking, and the answer is given a few\nseconds to come back before the listing is printed.  Whoever is still\nunanswered appears as the first eight characters of their key, in\nbrackets.  That is a question in flight rather than a person with no\nname, and the next run usually has it.
who:section:Examples	who	section	Examples	Who is here, and then a word to the second of them:\n\n    who\n    im 2 is the lantern by the gate yours?\n\nSee also: `map` for the same people drawn as a picture, `where`,\n`look`, `objects`, `lookup` and `profile` for somebody who is not\nstanding here, and `im` for talking to one who is.
who:example:2	who	example	Examples	im 2 is the lantern by the gate yours?
worn	worn	line		worn [-l] [TEXT]\nthe objects being worn, and where; -l for the ids\nhelp groups: region\nkeywords: wearing attachments attached clothing outfit what have on body parts hud
worn:intro	worn	intro		`worn` lists everything the avatar has on: clothing, body parts and\nattachments together, with where each one is worn.\n\n    worn\n\nThe first column is the slot for a system wearable -- `shirt`, `skin`,\n`shape`, `eyes` -- and the attachment point for an object.  A wearable\nhas no attachment point, because it is not attached to anything.\n\nWith no argument it is everything.  A word matches the name, without\nregard to case, and the name only -- `worn hud` does not list the HUD\nattachments.  It lists whatever is called something with `hud` in it.\nWearables come first, then attachments sorted by point, so the HUD\nones fall together without being asked for.\n\nThe word comes last.  Flags are read only until the first ordinary\nargument, so `worn -l auto` is the long listing of the auto objects and\n`worn auto -l` searches for the name `auto -l` and matches nothing.
worn:example:1	worn	example		worn
worn:section:Two records, and where they disagree	worn	section	Two records, and where they disagree	This reads two things that are not the same thing.\n\nThe **Current Outfit folder** is what should be on: a link per worn\nthing, written by whatever put it on.  It is the grid's own record and\nthe one the baking service reads.\n\nThe **region's description of the objects around us** is what is on,\nand covers attachments only, since a shirt is not an object.\n\nBoth are listed, and a line that came from only one of them says so.\n\n    (in the outfit, not described)\n\nThe folder names it and the region has not mentioned it.  It may have\nfailed to rez at login, which happens; or this session may never have\nbeen told, since an attachment is announced when it goes on and at\nlogin and at no other time.  It is still worn, and `detach` still\ntakes it off -- that needs the inventory item and nothing about the\nobject.\n\n    (not in the outfit, so it will not come back)\n\nThe region describes it and the folder does not hold it.  It is on the\navatar now and will be gone at the next login, because nothing wrote\nthe record.  Wearing it again from here puts that right.\n\n    (in the outfit, and the item is gone)\n\nThe link outlived what it pointed at.
worn:section:A third record, which settles the first line	worn	section	A third record, which settles the first line	"In the outfit, not described" can mean off, or on and not yet\ndescribed, and the two records above cannot tell those apart.  The\nsimulator's own list of what is attached can.  It arrives with each\nbake of the avatar's appearance, and names every attachment on the body\nby its object -- the object, not the inventory item, so it cannot say\nwhich line is which, only how many are on.\n\nIt is shown only when it settles something:\n\n    (the simulator lists 1 attachment that nothing here has described:)\n    head               ?\n\nSomething is on that nothing has described; with `-l` its object is\nprinted too.\n\n    (the simulator lists nothing that is not described here, so what is\n    marked not described is off -- unless it is a HUD, which it never lists)\n\nHUDs are never in the list, not even in the copy an avatar is sent\nabout itself, so it can say nothing about one.\n\n    (the simulator's list of what is worn is from before the last change\n    to the outfit, so it cannot settle what is not described)\n\nThe list is sent only when the avatar is baked.  `wear` and `detach`\nask for a bake afterwards, as a viewer does, so this should be seen\nonly after something else changed the outfit, or after a bake that\nfailed.
worn:option:-l	worn	option	-l	Print two keys: the inventory item first, then the object.  The item is\nthe one that does not change.  The object is rezzed afresh, with a new\nkey, every time it goes on and every time the avatar logs in, so a key\nwritten down from a previous session names nothing.\n\nA wearable has no object at all and prints `-` in that column, and so\ndoes an attachment nothing has described.\n\nThe item id is also what tells two attachments apart when their names\nagree, which is the refusal `detach` gives for a word that is worn\ntwice.
worn:section:The names come from inventory	worn	section	The names come from inventory	A worn object will not answer a request for its properties, so the name\nprinted is the inventory item's.  That is the name `detach` matches\nagainst, and unlike the object's name it does not change.\n\nInventory is walked a few levels down rather than in full.  Anything\nnot found in that much of it is printed as its item key -- the item may\nsit deeper than the listing went, or have been deleted while still\nworn, which Second Life allows.  A key here means the name was not to\nhand, not that the thing is nameless.
worn:section:What this can and cannot see	worn	section	What this can and cannot see	The region describes an attachment when it goes on, and again at every\nlogin, and never otherwise.  Whatever held the session at login heard\nall of that, and is what answers the second half of this listing, so a\nshell that attached later is not at a disadvantage.\n\nWhat no one heard, no one can place.  That is what the folder is for:\na thing nobody described is still listed, still named, and still comes\noff, with the first column left as `-` rather than a point invented for\nit.\n\nMeasured on Agni, an avatar sitting on a chair had one attachment of\nten described and the other nine only in the folder.  That was not the\nregion being quiet.  It had described all ten; the daemon had then\nthrown them away, because the chair itself had never been described\nand so nothing hanging off the avatar on it could be placed.  That is\nmended, and an avatar that has been logged in for a while should show\nnothing here.  A line that does is worth a look rather than a shrug.\n\nClothing and body parts are not described by the region at all, in any\ncircumstances.  They come from the folder alone.
worn:section:Examples	worn	section	Examples	    worn\n    worn -l auto\n    detach lantern\n\nSee also: `wear`, `detach`, `objects` for the same things as the region\nsees them, and `auto` for the worn objects the benchmarks use.
worn:example:2	worn	example	Examples	worn -l auto
worn:example:3	worn	example	Examples	detach lantern
